      309 +> - **Application service** — *coordinates*: opens the transac
          +tion / acquires the guard around the whole check→mutate→save c
          +ritical section, and *reacts* to conflicts (catch `Concurrency
          +Error`, retry or fail the use case).                          
      310 +> - **Infrastructure, behind a port** — supplies the *atomic m
          +echanism* the shared resource offers (DB version check in the 
          +repository; a `LockManager` adapter for Redis/threading). The 
          +domain never knows whether the shared thing is a row, a file, 
          +or a Redis key.                                               
      311 +                                                              
      312 +### Concretely, for *this* system (multiple workers, one share
          +d DB)                                                         
      313 +                                                              
      314 +`threading.Lock` is out — separate processes don't share it. T
          +he clean default is **DB-level optimistic locking**: a `versio
          +n` column the repository checks on save (`UPDATE … WHERE id=? 
          +AND version=?`); the loser gets 0 rows → `ConcurrencyError` → 
          +the application service retries or fails. The entity may *hold
          +* a `version` field but does nothing with it; the repository d
          +oes the compare-and-set; the service reacts. Reach for a distr
          +ibuted lock (Redis behind a `LockManager` port) only to guard 
          +something the DB can't see. Same layering as always — **domain
          + pure, mechanism in infrastructure, coordination in the servic
          +e.**                                                          
      315 +                                                              
      316 +```python                                                     
      317 +# application service — coordinates; the DB enforces          
      318 +def borrow_book(self, member_id, copy_id):                    
      319 +    copy = self._copies.get(CopyId(copy_id))      # load (carr
          +ies version)                                                  
      320 +    self._borrowing.check_can_borrow(...)          # business 
          +check (NOT race protection)                                   
      321 +    copy.issue()                                   # entity in
          +variant                                                       
      322 +    loan = Loan.create(member_id, copy.id, self._clock.today()
          +)                                                             
      323 +    try:                                                      
      324 +        self._copies.save(copy)                    # repo: UPD
          +ATE ... WHERE version=? (the real guard)                      
      325 +        self._loans.save(loan)                                
      326 +    except ConcurrencyError:                                  
      327 +        ... # retry, or surface "please try again"            
      328 +```                                                           
      329 +                                                              
      330 +> **The one-liner:** the invariant check tells you *whether* a
          +n operation is allowed; the shared-resource mechanism (DB vers
          +ion/lock, file lock, atomic cache op) makes that check *hold u
          +nder concurrency*. The service decides and coordinates; the sh
          +ared layer enforces.                                          
      331 +                                                              
      332 +---                                                           
      333 +                                                              
      334  ## 9.6 The layered picture so far
      335  
      336  ```

⏺ Added §9.5.1 "Concurrency: who actually prevents a race?" to step 9, slotted
  right after the transaction-boundary section (§9.5) and before the layered
  picture (§9.6).

  It captures the whole arc we worked through, in order:

  1. Entity declares the invariant, doesn't enforce concurrency — pure, no
     locks.
  2. The race + the 0% point — worked example showing both service-side checks
     pass; the check is business logic, not concurrency control. Includes the
     "kill this misconception" callout on the "double guarantee" framing.
  3. Where the race lives — separate processes share no memory but share the DB 
     row; that's the contended resource; why threading.Lock is useless across
     workers.
  4. The general principle — enforce at the shared resource, with the
     native-mechanism table (DB / file / Redis / memory / dir-mutex / socket).
  5. Atomicity over locks — don't-share > atomic-op > lock (last resort, keyed
     per-aggregate).
  6. Division of labor — entity / aggregate boundary / application service /
     infrastructure-behind-a-port.
  7. Concretely for this system — multiple workers + one DB → optimistic version
     locking, with code.
  8. The closing one-liner.

  I kept the cross-references live (§9.2.1 for domain purity, §5.2 for
  small-aggregate/contention), so it stitches into the existing docs.

❯ ok, till now invariants resides in entity and make it impossible to break.    
  And if any invariants reuires more entities then those entitoes and VO        
  becomes aggregate...right ?                                                   

⏺ Direction is right, and you've got the spine of it. Let me confirm the first
  part and then add the one refinement that step 5 forced on us — because your
  second sentence is the first cut, not the final rule.

  Part 1 — invariants reside in the owning object ✅ (one tweak)

  Correct, with a small widening: invariants live in whatever object owns the 
  data — which is entities and value objects:
  - VO-level: Money ≥ 0, ISBN well-formed → enforced in the VO constructor.
  - Entity-level: "issue only if available" → enforced in BookCopy.issue().

  And "make it impossible to break" = enforce in constructor / mutating method /
  or remove the illegal operation. ✅

  Part 2 — "spans more entities → they become an aggregate" ⚠️ first cut, needs 
  refining

  This is the step-4 instinct, and it's a good signal — but step 5 taught us
  it's not automatic. A spanning invariant tells you "there's a consistency need
  here," but it does not automatically mean those objects fuse into one
  aggregate. Two outcomes are possible:

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │       When the spanning objects...        │           Result            │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ have no independent life (child           │ → one aggregate, parent is  │
  │ meaningless without parent —              │ root, child lives inside    │
  │ Order+OrderLine)                          │                             │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ each have a strong independent lifecycle  │ → separate aggregates + the │
  │ (queried, referenced, changed on their    │  rule moves to a domain     │
  │ own)                                      │ service                     │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  Our own Member + Loan is the second case, and it's the whole lesson:
  - Step 4 said "≤2 loans" spans Member+Loan → looked like one aggregate (Member
    contains Loans).
  - Step 5 refined it: a Loan has its own lifecycle and is queried on its own
    (overdue list), and we prefer small aggregates → so Loan became its own
    aggregate, referencing MemberId by ID.
  - The invariant didn't disappear — it relocated to a borrowing domain service
    that counts active loans during the borrow operation.

  So the corrected rule:

  ▎ A spanning invariant signals a consistency requirement. Whether that becomes
  ▎ one aggregate (objects fused, lacking independent identity) or separate 
  ▎ aggregates + a domain service (objects with independent lifecycles) is 
  ▎ decided by lifecycle independence and the keep-aggregates-small preference —
  ▎ not automatically "they merge."

  Your summary, tightened

  ▎ Invariants live in the object that owns the data (entity or VO), enforced so
  ▎ they can't break. A single invariant spanning multiple objects signals a 
  ▎ consistency boundary — if those objects have no life of their own, they form
  ▎ one aggregate; if they each have independent lifecycles, they stay separate
  ▎ aggregates and the cross-object rule moves to a domain service.

  You've basically reconstructed steps 3→4→5 from memory — that's exactly the
  arc. The only thing to carry forward is: spanning invariant ≠ guaranteed 
  single aggregate. It might be a domain service instead.

  Ready for next (step 6: relationships among aggregates)?

✻ Cogitated for 25s

❯ what is aggregate vs aggregate roots ?                                        

⏺ Clean distinction — easy to blur, so here it is precisely:

  The two terms

  ▎ - Aggregate = the whole cluster — the root entity + any child entities + 
  ▎ their value objects — treated as one consistency unit. It's the boundary 
  ▎ (the box).
  ▎ - Aggregate Root = the one entity inside that cluster that is the sole legal
  ▎ entry point. It's the gatekeeper (the door into the box).

  So they're not two different things competing — the root is part of the
  aggregate. The aggregate is the team; the root is the team captain you're 
  allowed to talk to.

  The restaurant analogy 🍽️

  - Aggregate = the whole Order (the order itself + its line items + quantities
    + total).
  - Aggregate Root = the Order object you actually call. You say "add a pizza"
    to the Order; you never phone the kitchen to mutate "line item #3" directly.
    The root keeps the line items and total consistent on your behalf.

  The line items are inside the aggregate but are not the root — you can't reach
  them from outside.

  Why the root exists (its job)

  The root is the guardian of the aggregate's invariants. Because it's the only
  way in:
  1. Every change goes through it → it can enforce the rules on every operation.
     Nothing sneaks past.
  2. Outsiders may hold a reference to the root only — never to a child inside.
     (You hold Order, never a loose OrderLine.)
  3. The whole aggregate loads/saves as one unit, through the root, via one 
     repository (one repo per aggregate root — your CLAUDE.md rule).

  In our LMS

  Here it's especially crisp because every one of our aggregates is a single 
  entity — so the aggregate and its root are almost the same object, just viewed
  two ways:

  ┌─────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬────────────────────────┐
  │     Aggregate (the      │ Aggregate Root (the  │    Children inside     │
  │    cluster/boundary)    │    entry entity)     │                        │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Loan aggregate          │ Loan                 │ — (just VOs:           │
  │                         │                      │ DateRange, LoanStatus) │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ BookCopy aggregate      │ BookCopy             │ — (VO: CopyStatus)     │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Member aggregate        │ Member               │ —                      │
  └─────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴────────────────────────┘

  Because none of ours have child entities, "the aggregate" and "the root" point
  at the same object. The distinction only becomes visually obvious in a
  multi-entity aggregate like Order (root) + OrderLines (children inside) —
  there the aggregate is the whole thing, the root is just the Order.

  The one-liner

  ▎ The aggregate is what must stay consistent (the boundary); the aggregate 
  ▎ root is who guards it (the single entry point). Every aggregate has exactly 
  ▎ one root; you always talk to the root, never reach inside.

  Want this added to step 5 as a note (it would sit naturally next to §5.1), or
  move on to next?

✻ Cooked for 17s

❯ now summarize everything in points to remember while designing aggregates.    

⏺ Aggregate Design — Points to Remember

  Deriving the boundary

  1. Don't pick boundaries by taste — derive them. Start from invariants +
     concurrency needs, not from "what feels related."
  2. A single invariant that spans multiple objects is the signal that a
     consistency boundary lives there.
  3. Spanning invariant ≠ automatic single aggregate. Decide by lifecycle:
     - Child has no life of its own (meaningless apart from parent) → one 
       aggregate, child inside (Order + OrderLine).
     - Each has an independent lifecycle (queried/referenced/changed alone) →
       separate aggregates, and the rule moves to a domain service (our
       Member/Loan).
  4. The inside-or-outside test: "Does this object have a life of its own that 
     others reference independently?" Yes → its own root. No → child inside the
     parent.

  Structure

  5. Aggregate = the cluster (root + child entities + their VOs) = the boundary.
     Aggregate root = the one entity that's the sole entry point = the
     gatekeeper.
  6. Exactly one root per aggregate. Outsiders reference/call the root only,
     never reach inside to a child.
  7. An aggregate can hold multiple entities + VOs — but only entities bound by
     a shared invariant and lacking independent identity. Otherwise they're
     separate aggregates.

  The four classic rules

  8. Protect true invariants inside one consistency boundary — data that must
     always agree lives together, changes together.
  9. Design small aggregates. The default mistake is too big. Small = fast to
     load, less lock contention, clear transactions. Default to one entity; add
     more only when an invariant forces it.
  10. Reference other aggregates by ID, never by object. Loan holds member_id,
      not a Member. Keeps aggregates small, boundaries clear, layering honest
      (forces you through repositories).
  11. One transaction = one aggregate. Cross-aggregate rules → domain service (+
      eventual consistency at large scale), not multi-aggregate transactions.
      (Bending this is OK with one local DB — know the rule and its reason.)

  Invariants & purity

  12. Invariants live in the object that owns the data — entity or VO. Enforce
      so they're impossible to break: constructor / guarded method / remove the
      illegal operation entirely.
  13. The root guards the aggregate's invariants on every operation — that's why
      it's the only entry point.
  14. One repository per aggregate root. Interface in domain/, implementation in
      infrastructure/. The whole aggregate loads/saves as one unit through the
      root.
  15. Keep the domain pure — no repository/DB/cache/framework access inside
      entities. Data is handed in by the application service.

  Concurrency (around the aggregate, not inside it)

  16. The aggregate is the unit of concurrency control — small boundaries let
      unrelated items lock independently (low contention).
  17. The entity declares the invariant; it does NOT enforce concurrency. A
      service-side check does 0% of race prevention — both racers pass it.
  18. The race lives at the shared resource (the DB row, file, cache key — not
      process memory). Enforce there, using that resource's native atomic
      mechanism (DB version/FOR UPDATE, file lock, Redis SETNX…).
  19. Prefer: don't-share > atomic operation > explicit lock. Lock only for
      multi-step critical sections, keyed per-aggregate (never one global lock).
  20. Division of labor: entity = what's consistent · aggregate boundary = what 
      unit to guard · application service = coordinate (transaction,
      retry-on-conflict) · infrastructure-behind-a-port = how to enforce.

  The mental tests

  - "If I'm holding another aggregate's object and calling its methods, I've 
    probably merged two aggregates by accident." → hold the ID, look it up.
  - "What's the smallest box such that everything an invariant touches lives 
    inside it?" → that box is the aggregate.
  - Design is iterative — boundaries shift as you learn (our "Member owns ≤2
    loans" → "Loan is its own root, rule in a service"). That's maturing, not
    backtracking.

  ---

  Want me to drop this as a quick-reference checklist at the end of the step-5
  doc? Otherwise, say next for step 6 (relationships among aggregates).

✻ Baked for 26s

❯ what is meant by this Inside an aggregate, objects relate by direct reference 
  (composition). Across aggregates, they relate by ID (association). The        
  aggregate boundary is the line between those two worlds. ?                    

⏺ This is the central idea of step 6, and it's really just the by-ID rule (§5.4)
  restated as a principle about relationships. Let me unpack it.

  Two ways one object can "relate to" another

  When object A needs to be connected to object B, there are exactly two ways to
  wire it:

  1. Direct reference (composition) — A holds the actual B object in a field:
  class Order:
      self.lines: list[OrderLine]   # the real OrderLine objects live here
  # I can do: order.lines[0].quantity — reach straight in and touch B

  2. ID reference (association) — A holds only B's identifier, not B itself:
  class Loan:
      self.member_id: MemberId      # just an ID string, NOT a Member object
  # To touch the Member I must go fetch it: members.get(loan.member_id)

  The difference: with #1 you can navigate directly (order.lines[0]). With #2
  you only have a pointer — to act on the real thing, you must look it up
  through its repository.

  The rule: which style depends on whether you're crossing the boundary

  ▎ - Inside the same aggregate → use direct reference (composition). The 
  ▎ objects live and die together as one unit, so holding the real object is 
  ▎ fine — even required.
  ▎ - Across aggregate boundaries → use ID reference (association). The other 
  ▎ aggregate is a separate unit with its own lifecycle, so you hold only its 
  ▎ ID.

  Why this exact split — and why it's not arbitrary

  It falls straight out of everything from step 5:

  - Inside the boundary, objects must stay transactionally consistent and
    load/save as one unit. Direct references let the root reach its children to
    enforce invariants — and since they're saved together anyway, there's no
    harm. (Order contains its OrderLines.)
  - Across the boundary, holding the real object would drag a whole separate 
    aggregate inside yours — defeating "keep aggregates small," blurring "one
    transaction = one aggregate," and creating lock contention. So you hold only
    the ID; to act on it you fetch it through its repository. (Loan holds
    member_id, never a Member.)

  "The boundary IS the line between those two worlds"

  This is the elegant part. You don't need a separate rule for "when do I use
  composition vs. association." The aggregate boundary you already drew answers 
  it automatically:

          ┌─────────── Loan aggregate ───────────┐
          │  Loan ──direct──> DateRange (VO)      │   inside  → object reference
          │       ──direct──> LoanStatus (VO)     │            (composition)
          │  member_id ─┐                         │
          └─────────────┼─────────────────────────┘
                        │ across → ID reference (association)
                        ▼
                ┌─── Member aggregate ───┐
                │  Member (its own root) │
                └────────────────────────┘

  - Same side of the line → direct object reference.
  - Crossing the line → ID reference.

  So the boundary isn't just about consistency and transactions (step 5) — it
  also dictates how objects are allowed to point at each other. Drawing the
  boundary already decided your relationship style; you read it off the box.

  In our LMS

  ┌──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬────────────────────────┐
  │       Relationship       │   Same aggregate?   │         Style          │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Loan → its DateRange /   │ Yes                 │ direct reference       │
  │ LoanStatus               │                     │ (composition)          │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Loan → Member            │ No (different       │ member_id              │
  │                          │ aggregates)         │ (association)          │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Loan → BookCopy          │ No                  │ copy_id (association)  │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ BookCopy → its           │ Yes                 │ direct reference       │
  │ CopyStatus               │                     │                        │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ BookCopy → Book          │ No                  │ book_id (association)  │
  └──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴────────────────────────┘

  ▎ The one-liner: inside an aggregate, hold the real object; across aggregates,
  ▎ hold only the ID and look it up. The boundary you drew in step 5 is exactly
  ▎ the line that tells you which.

  Want this added to the step-6 doc as a note, or move on with next?

✻ Baked for 26s

❯ but tell me why two aggregates need to connect via ID ? what issue will it    
  cause if i pass object to th eaggregate ?                                     

⏺ Great question — "reference by ID" gets repeated like a mantra, but you should
  see the concrete damage object references cause. Let me give you the real
  failure modes, not just "it's the rule."

  Setup — the tempting version:
  class Loan:
      def __init__(self, member: Member, copy: BookCopy):
          self.member = member   # the whole Member object
          self.copy = copy       # the whole BookCopy object

  Here's what breaks.

  1. You can't load a Loan without loading half the database

  To create/fetch a Loan, you now need a fully-built Member and BookCopy. But
  Member might hold its loans, fines, profile… and BookCopy holds its Book,
  which holds… To load one loan, the object graph drags in a chain:

  Loan → Member → [its fines, its loans → each Loan → its Member → …] 💥

  This is the "load one thing, accidentally load everything" problem. With IDs,
  a Loan is tiny — two ID strings and a date range — and loads instantly. The
  boundary stays small (the step-5 rule), in practice.

  2. Two copies of the same Member that disagree

  If Loan holds a Member object, and the Member aggregate is also loaded
  elsewhere, you now have two in-memory Member objects for the same person — and
  they drift:

  loan.member.suspend()        # this copy: suspended
  member = members.get(id)     # a DIFFERENT copy: still active
  # Which one is true? Which one gets saved? 🤷

  Whose state wins on save? You get lost updates and stale reads. With an ID,
  there's exactly one way to get the Member — members.get(id) — so there's one
  authoritative copy. (This is the "identity map" problem; IDs sidestep it.)

  3. The transaction boundary dissolves — "one transaction = one aggregate" 
  breaks

  Holding the object invites mutating it:
  loan.member.deactivate()   # I'm "in" a Loan operation but mutating a Member!
  loans.save(loan)           # does this save the Member too? cascade? when?
  Now a single save touches two aggregates with unclear commit semantics —
  exactly the multi-aggregate transaction step 5 warned against. Cascading saves
  silently write objects you didn't mean to, and rollback gets murky. With IDs
  you can't casually mutate the Member — you'd have to deliberately fetch and
  save it, making the boundary explicit.

  4. Lock contention explodes

  To modify the Loan you load (and often lock) its Member and BookCopy too. So
  returning a book locks the member — blocking every other operation on that
  member. Unrelated operations queue up on locks they never needed. With IDs,
  the Loan locks only itself. (This is the §5.2 contention point, caused
  directly by object references.)

  5. You can't split storage or scale later

  Object references hard-wire the assumption that Member and Loan live in the
  same store, loadable together. The day you want loans in a different
  table/service/cache, the object graph fights you. IDs are storage-agnostic —
  an ID is just a value; what's behind it can move anywhere.

  6. Circular references & serialization hell

  Loan → Member → Loan → Member… — serializing (to JSON, to a cache) either
  infinite-loops or needs hacks to break the cycle. IDs are flat values; no
  cycles, trivial to serialize/cache.

  7. The boundary becomes a lie

  The whole point of an aggregate is a clear "what's inside / what's outside"
  line. The moment Loan holds a Member object, Member is effectively inside Loan
  — the boundary you carefully drew in step 5 has silently dissolved. IDs keep
  the line honest: a member_id visibly says "this lives outside me; go through 
  its repository."

  The summary

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Object reference across aggregates   │          ID reference           │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Loads a huge object graph             │ Loads a tiny aggregate          │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Multiple drifting copies → lost       │ One authoritative copy via repo │
  │ updates                               │                                 │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Transaction spans many aggregates     │ One transaction = one aggregate │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Locks unrelated aggregates →          │ Locks only itself               │
  │ contention                            │                                 │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Storage hard-wired together           │ Storage-agnostic, scalable      │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Circular refs, serialization pain     │ Flat values, trivial to         │
  │                                       │ serialize                       │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Boundary silently dissolves           │ Boundary stays explicit         │
  └───────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  ▎ The root cause behind all seven: holding the object means containing it. The
  ▎ instant Loan contains Member, they're no longer two aggregates — they're 
  ▎ one big tangled one, and every benefit of small aggregates (fast loads, 
  ▎ clean transactions, low contention, independent scaling) evaporates. The ID 
  ▎ is what keeps them genuinely separate.

  Note the one legitimate exception: passing an object transiently into a method
  — member.borrow(copy) — is fine. That's not storing a reference; the Member
  uses the copy for one operation and never holds onto it. The rule is about
  persistent fields (self.copy = copy), not method arguments.

  Want me to add these failure modes to the step-6 doc?

✻ Cooked for 29s

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Step 6 — Relationships Among Aggregates

  Step 5 gave us five aggregates that point at each other by ID. This step turns
  those loose pointers into a precise relationship map — and produces the
  skeleton of our class diagram and ER diagram. Three questions to answer for
  each connection: how many? (cardinality), which way? (direction), what kind?
  (the OOP relationship spectrum).

  The unifying idea — which you already grasped in the last few questions:

  ▎ Inside an aggregate, objects relate by direct reference (composition). 
  ▎ Across aggregates, they relate by ID (association). The aggregate boundary 
  ▎ is the line between those two worlds.

  6.1 The OOP relationship spectrum → DDD

  Six object relationships, weakest coupling to strongest, each mapped to where
  it shows up in our model:

  Relationship: Dependency
  Meaning: "uses temporarily" (a parameter)
  LMS example: BorrowingService uses a repo passed in
  DDD home: how services get collaborators (step 9)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Relationship: Association
  Meaning: "connected to," longer-term, independent lifecycles
  LMS example: Loan → Member via member_id
  DDD home: across aggregates (by ID)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Relationship: Aggregation
  Meaning: weak "has-a," part can outlive whole
  LMS example: Member has loans
  DDD home: across aggregates
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Relationship: Composition
  Meaning: exclusive ownership, part dies with whole
  LMS example: Fine ◆── Money, Loan ◆── DateRange
  DDD home: inside an aggregate
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Relationship: Inheritance
  Meaning: "is a"
  LMS example: BorrowingLimitExceededError is a DomainError
  DDD home: used sparingly — exception hierarchy
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Relationship: Realization
  Meaning: "implements interface"
  LMS example: InMemoryLoanRepository realizes LoanRepository
  DDD home: repository pattern (step 10)

  The key collapse: Composition = inside an aggregate (VOs owned by the root,
  die with it). Association/Aggregation = across boundaries (by ID, per step 5's
  rule). That's exactly the two-worlds idea, now with names.

  ▎ Rule of thumb: if you ever want composition across an aggregate boundary (a 
  ▎ direct object reference with shared lifecycle), that's a signal the two 
  ▎ things might belong in the same aggregate — go re-check step 5. (This is the
  ▎ inverse of the "don't hold an object across boundaries" failure modes you 
  ▎ just worked through.)

  A pleasing fact about our model: every aggregate (Book, BookCopy, Member,
  Loan, Fine) is a single entity composed only of value objects — none contains
  a child entity. So our only inside-aggregate relationship is entity → VO
  (composition). We dissolved the child-entity problem entirely by keeping
  aggregates small. Feature, not gap.

  6.2 Cardinality — invariants seen as numbers

  ┌────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Relationship  │                      Cardinality                       │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Book →         │ 1 ──< * (one title, many physical copies)              │
  │ BookCopy       │                                                        │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Member → Loan  │ 1 ──< * over time, but 0..2 active ← the borrowing     │
  │                │ limit                                                  │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ BookCopy →     │ 1 ──< * over time, 0..1 active ←                       │
  │ Loan           │ issue-only-if-available                                │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Loan → Fine    │ 1 ── 0..1 (at most one fine, only if late)             │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Member → Fine  │ 1 ──< *                                                │
  └────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The insight: invariants from step 4 reappear as cardinality constraints. "≤ 2
  active loans" is the 0..2 edge. Cardinality and invariants are two views of
  the same rules.

  6.3 Direction — the "many" side holds the reference

  We strongly prefer unidirectional references across aggregates, pointing from
  the dependent (many) side to the stable (one) side:

  Book     ◄──── BookCopy   (BookCopy holds book_id; Book doesn't list its
  copies)
  Member   ◄──── Loan       (Loan holds member_id; Member holds no loan list)
  BookCopy ◄──── Loan       (Loan holds copy_id)
  Member   ◄──── Fine       (Fine holds member_id)
  Loan     ◄──── Fine       (Fine holds loan_id)

  Three reasons — all things you essentially derived already:
  - If Member held a List[Loan], loading a member drags in all their loans → the
    big-aggregate problem again.
  - Bidirectional refs are a trap → two places that can disagree, must be kept
    in sync. Unidirectional = one source of truth. (Your failure-mode #2.)
  - "So how does a member find their loans?" → a repository query:
    loan_repository.find_active_by_member(member_id). You navigate the
    relationship via the repository, not an in-memory object graph.

  6.4 / 6.5 The skeletons → schema for free

  When you draw it out, ◆ (composition) always sits inside a boundary and ──▶
  (association by ID) always crosses one — that visual rule is the whole chapter
  in one picture. And the payoff: every by-ID reference becomes a foreign key,
  every aggregate root becomes a table. Clean step-5 boundaries translate
  directly into a clean schema — no accident.

  One discipline note from the doc: the ERD here is a sanity check ("do my
  boundaries map to a sane schema?"), not the source of truth. Real persistence
  design waits for step 10 (repository interfaces) and step 11 (concrete DB) —
  because DDD designs the domain as if the database doesn't exist, and storage
  plugs in last.

  ---

  This step is structural — no code yet (that's step 8). Quick check before step
  7: why does the reference live on Loan (holding member_id) rather than on 
  Member (holding a list of loans)? Answer in your words and I'll confirm — or
  say next for step 7 (aggregate responsibilities).

✻ Baked for 30s

❯ example to explain diff between aggregation and association                   

⏺ Good one to pin down, because aggregation and association are genuinely fuzzy
  — even the UML community argues about it. Let me give you the clean version
  with examples.

  The honest starting point

  ▎ Association is the general "A is connected to B" relationship. Aggregation 
  ▎ is just a special kind of association — a "whole-part" association where A 
  ▎ is a container/owner of B, but B can still live independently.

  So aggregation is association + a "has-a / is-part-of" flavor. That's why they
  feel blurry: aggregation is a subset of association.

  Plain association — "knows about / uses"

  A peer-to-peer link. Neither owns the other; there's no whole-part feeling.
  They're just connected.

  class Student:
      def __init__(self, advisor: Professor):
          self.advisor = advisor      # a Student is associated with a Professor

  A Student knows a Professor. But a professor isn't "part of" a student, and a
  student isn't "made of" professors. They're independent peers who happen to be
  linked. Both exist on their own; the link is just a reference.

  LMS example: Loan → Member. A loan is connected to the member who took it, but
  the member isn't a "part" of the loan. Peer link → plain association.

  Aggregation — "has-a / whole-part, but parts survive"

  A whole-part relationship: A is a container of Bs. But the parts have their
  own independent lifecycle — destroy the whole, and the parts live on.

  class Team:
      def __init__(self):
          self.players: list[Player] = []   # a Team HAS players

      def add(self, player: Player):
          self.players.append(player)        # players exist BEFORE and AFTER 
  the team

  The classic example: a Team and Players. The team is made of players
  (whole-part — that's the "aggregation" feel). But if the team disbands, the
  players don't cease to exist — they go join other teams. The parts outlive the
  whole.

  Another: a Playlist and Songs. A playlist has songs (whole-part), but deleting
  the playlist doesn't delete the songs — they exist independently in your
  library.

  The contrast — and where composition sits

  The cleanest way to feel all three is the lifecycle test:

  ┌─────────────┬──────────────┬────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │             │ Whole-part   │ Do parts die with  │        Example        │
  │             │    feel?     │     the whole?     │                       │
  ├─────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Association │ No — just    │ Independent        │ Student ↔ Professor;  │
  │             │ peers        │                    │ Loan ↔ Member         │
  ├─────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Aggregation │ Yes (has-a)  │ No — parts survive │ Team ◇── Players;     │
  │             │              │                    │ Playlist ◇── Songs    │
  ├─────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Composition │ Yes (owns)   │ Yes — parts die    │ House ◆── Rooms; Fine │
  │             │              │ with it            │  ◆── Money            │
  └─────────────┴──────────────┴────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  The deciding question between the two "has-a" cases:

  ▎ "If I destroy the whole, do the parts still make sense / still exist?"
  ▎ - Yes, they live on independently → Aggregation (Team disbands, players 
  ▎ remain).
  ▎ - No, they're meaningless without the whole → Composition (House demolished,
  ▎ its rooms are gone).

  And association vs aggregation:

  ▎ "Is there a whole-part / owner-container feel, or are they just peers?"
  ▎ - Owner/container of → Aggregation (Team has Players).
  ▎ - Just connected peers → Association (Student advised by Professor).

  Why DDD mostly collapses this distinction

  Here's the practical punchline for our project. In DDD, both association and 
  aggregation cross aggregate boundaries (both sides have independent
  lifecycles), so both are implemented the same way — by ID reference. Only
  composition (parts die with the whole) stays inside an aggregate as a direct
  object reference.

  So for modeling purposes:
  - Composition → inside the aggregate, direct object reference (Fine ◆── 
    Money).
  - Association or Aggregation → across aggregates, by ID. (The fine academic
    distinction between them rarely changes your code.)

  That's why our step-6 doc lumps them: Loan → Member could be argued as either
  association or aggregation, but it doesn't matter — it's by-member_id either
  way. The distinction that earns its keep is composition vs. not (does it die
  with the whole?), because that's the one that decides inside-vs-across the
  boundary.

  ▎ One-liner: association = "knows about" (peers); aggregation = "has, but 
  ▎ parts outlive it" (Team/Players); composition = "owns, parts die with it" 
  ▎ (House/Rooms). In DDD only the last is inside an aggregate — the first two 
  ▎ cross boundaries by ID.

  Want this added to step 6 as a note next to the relationship table?

✻ Cooked for 24s

❯ If my req is to get all the Loans from a member, what should be best way      
  considering unidirectinal from Mamber <- Loan.                                

⏺ Exactly the right question — because with the unidirectional Member ◄── Loan
  design, Member has no loans list to read. So the answer is: you query the 
  LoanRepository, not the Member.

  The right way: a repository query

  The LoanRepository (which lives in the Loan aggregate's world) gets a finder
  method keyed by member_id:

  # domain/repositories.py  — interface (port), lives in domain
  class LoanRepository(Protocol):
      def get(self, loan_id: LoanId) -> Loan: ...
      def save(self, loan: Loan) -> None: ...
      def find_by_member(self, member_id: MemberId) -> list[Loan]: ...        # 
  all loans, history
      def find_active_by_member(self, member_id: MemberId) -> list[Loan]: ... # 
  just active ones
      def count_active_for_member(self, member_id: MemberId) -> int: ...      # 
  for the ≤2 rule

  The application service uses it:

  class MemberLoansQuery:
      def __init__(self, loans: LoanRepository):
          self._loans = loans

      def all_loans_for(self, member_id: str) -> list[Loan]:
          return self._loans.find_by_member(MemberId(member_id))

  In the DB implementation that's just SELECT * FROM loan WHERE member_id = ? —
  the member_id foreign key (the by-ID reference from step 6) is exactly what
  makes this query trivial and indexable.

  Why this is the right answer (not a workaround)

  This is the practical consequence of "navigate via repositories, not object
  graphs" (step 6, §6.3). The relationship still exists — you just resolve it
  through the repo instead of an in-memory pointer.

  ┌───────────────┬────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │               │ member.loans (object   │   loans.find_by_member(id)    │
  │               │    list on Member)     │         (repo query)          │
  ├───────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Loading a     │ drags in all their     │ stays small — load loans only │
  │ member        │ loans every time       │  when asked                   │
  ├───────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Source of     │ two places can         │                               │
  │ truth         │ disagree               │ one — the Loan table          │
  │               │ (bidirectional trap)   │                               │
  ├───────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Filtering     │ load all, filter in    │ WHERE status='ACTIVE' — DB    │
  │ ("active      │ memory                 │ does it, indexed              │
  │ only")        │                        │                               │
  ├───────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Aggregate     │ Member bloats with     │ Member stays lean             │
  │ size          │ history                │                               │
  └───────────────┴────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  Pick the specific finder you need — don't fetch-all-then-filter

  The important nuance: add a purpose-built query method for each real need,
  rather than always pulling every loan and filtering in Python:

  - Need the borrowing-limit check (≤2)? → count_active_for_member(id) — the DB
    returns a number, you never load loan objects at all.
  - Need the member's current loans for a screen? → find_active_by_member(id) —
    WHERE member_id=? AND status='ACTIVE'.
  - Need full history for an audit page? → find_by_member(id).

  Each pushes the filtering into the database (fast, indexed) instead of loading
  everything and filtering in memory (slow, wasteful). This is the step-6
  lesson — "repositories answer the give-me-the-other-direction questions" —
  applied concretely.

  The one rule to remember

  ▎ The reference points Member ◄── Loan, so Member doesn't know its loans. To 
  ▎ go "the other way," ask the LoanRepository with a finder keyed by member_id.
  ▎ Keep the finders specific (active / count / history) so the DB does the 
  ▎ filtering — never put a loans collection back on Member, or you re-create 
  ▎ the big-aggregate problem you deliberately avoided.

  Want me to add this find_by_member / count_active_for_member finder pattern to
  the step-6 (or step-10 repositories) doc as a worked note?

✻ Sautéed for 21s

❯ so, you are saying we should not have a method inside Member Entity to        
  list_all_loans which uses repository ?                                        

⏺ Correct — Member should NOT have list_all_loans() that reaches into a 
  repository. And this isn't a new rule — it's the exact "entities never touch
  repositories" principle (§9.2.1) you already learned, now showing up in a
  tempting disguise. Let me make the connection explicit.

  Why it's forbidden

  For Member.list_all_loans() to work, the Member would need a LoanRepository
  inside it:

  # ❌ This is the violation, just wearing a friendly name
  class Member:
      def __init__(self, member_id, loan_repo: LoanRepository):  # 🚨 repo 
  inside the entity
          self._loans = loan_repo

      def list_all_loans(self):
          return self._loans.find_by_member(self.id)             # 🚨 domain 
  doing I/O

  This breaks everything we established:
  1. Domain purity gone — the Member now depends on infrastructure; the
     pure-Python, zero-framework promise is broken.
  2. Dependency arrow reversed — the innermost layer (entity) now points outward
     at a repository.
  3. Testability gone — you can't construct a Member without a repo (real or
     mocked); Member(id, name) no longer works.
  4. Hidden I/O — member.list_all_loans() looks like reading a field but
     secretly hits the database. Surprise latency, surprise failures inside what
     reads like plain domain code.

  It's the same sin as loan.return_copy() calling a repo — the friendly method
  name (list_all_loans) just hides it better.

  Where the method does belong

  The method is fine — it just lives one layer out, in the application service,
  which is allowed to hold repositories:

  # ✅ application/  — application service owns the repo and the query
  class MemberLoansQuery:
      def __init__(self, loans: LoanRepository):
          self._loans = loans

      def list_all_loans(self, member_id: str) -> list[Loan]:
          return self._loans.find_by_member(MemberId(member_id))   # repo lives 
  HERE, legally

  Same method, same name even — just on a service, not on the entity. The entity
  stays pure; the service does the fetching.

  The principle restated

  ▎ An entity may only act on data it already holds or is handed. The instant a 
  ▎ "method on the entity" needs to go fetch something, it stops being domain 
  ▎ logic and becomes orchestration — and orchestration belongs in the 
  ▎ application service, the only place repositories are allowed.

  The litmus test

  Ask: "Can I call this method on a Member I just built in memory, with no 
  database anywhere?"
  - member.change_email(new_email) → ✅ yes, pure → belongs on the entity.
  - member.borrow(copy, active_count) → ✅ yes, data handed in → belongs on the 
    entity.
  - member.list_all_loans() → ❌ no, it must hit a repo → belongs on the 
    application service, not the entity.

  So: keep Member free of repository-backed methods. "Get all loans for a
  member" is a service-level query against LoanRepository, exactly as in the
  last answer — never a method on Member.

  Want me to capture this "tempting disguise" example in the docs (it's a sharp
  clarification of §9.2.1)?

⏺ Step 7 — Aggregate Responsibilities

  We have the structure (steps 5–6). Now each aggregate gets a job description —
  its precise behavior — before any code in step 8. This is the step that
  decides whether your model is rich (objects with behavior that protect
  themselves) or anemic (dumb data bags whose logic leaks into services). One
  OOP idea drives it:

  ▎ Tell, Don't Ask. Don't pull an object's data out to decide about it 
  ▎ elsewhere — tell the object what to do and let it decide, because it owns 
  ▎ the data and the rules.

  For each aggregate root we write four things — and the fourth is the one 
  people skip:
  1. Commands it accepts (state changes)
  2. Invariants it guards (from step 4)
  3. Queries it answers about itself
  4. What it must NOT do (anti-responsibilities)

  Rich vs. anemic — and how to choose the home

  The anemic trap: BookCopy is a dumb bag with a public status, and the rule
  ("only available copies issue") floats in a service that every caller must
  remember. Miss the check once → corrupt state.

  The rich fix puts the rule inside the data it protects — copy.issue() guards
  its own status, and nowhere else can change it. That single fact enforces
  invariants I-2/I-7 for the whole system. (This is exactly the "tempting 
  disguise" and Tell-Don't-Ask threads you already worked through.)

  Which object gets a responsibility? GRASP Information Expert — assign it to 
  the class that holds the data needed for it. BookCopy owns status → owns
  issue(). Loan owns due_date → answers is_overdue().

  The contracts — and the two that surprise people

  Most are straightforward (Book.update_metadata, Fine.pay/waive). Two are
  deliberately counter-intuitive, and they're the payoff of everything we
  discussed:

  Member is narrower than intuition says. It must NOT count its own active loans
  or hold a Loan list. Intuition wants Member.can_borrow() checking "< 2 loans"
  — but loans live in their own aggregate, so Member can't answer that (it
  doesn't hold them). This is literally the question you just asked — "should
  Member have list_all_loans()?" — now written into the contract as a hard
  limit. Member owns only member-level facts (is_active); the loan count comes
  from the LoanRepository, combined by a domain service.

  Loan reports a fact, never a price. Loan.days_overdue() says "12 days late."
  It must NOT turn that into ₹60 — that's a policy, owned by the fine Strategy
  (step 8). This is the invariant-vs-policy split from step 4, now a
  responsibility boundary.

  The "Must NOT" column discovers your domain services

  The valuable insight: every behavior you push out of an aggregate has to land
  somewhere — and that somewhere is a domain service. The "must not" rows
  literally enumerate them:

  ┌───────────────────────┬───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │  Pushed-out behavior  │    Why no single      │        Lands in         │
  │                       │   aggregate owns it   │                         │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ "Can this member      │ needs Member +        │ BorrowingService        │
  │ borrow this copy?"    │ BookCopy + loan count │                         │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ "Enforce ≤ 2 active   │ spans Member + Loan   │ BorrowingService        │
  │ loans"                │                       │                         │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ "Days overdue → ₹     │ a swappable policy,   │ FineCalculationStrategy │
  │ amount"               │ not a fact            │                         │
  └───────────────────────┴───────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  ▎ Skipping the "must not" column is how aggregates quietly bloat into 
  ▎ god-objects.

  CQS — a free discipline

  Each contract splits Commands (change state, return nothing — copy.issue())
  from Queries (return data, no side effects — copy.is_available). A method does
  or answers, never both. Queries become safe to call freely; commands become
  the only places state changes. Cheap, and makes the model far easier to reason
  about.

  ---

  This step turns the structural map into a behavioral contract per aggregate —
  so step 8 ("write the Python") becomes mechanical, the thinking already done.

  Notice how cleanly this absorbed your last few questions: the
  Member-can't-list-its-loans rule, the fact-vs-policy split, Tell-Don't-Ask.

  Step 8 is where code finally begins (VOs → entities → Strategy/State
  patterns). Per your teaching pace, I'll stop here and wait — say next when you
  want to start step 8, and I'll walk it as a lesson before generating
  anything.

⏺ Step 8a — Writing the Domain Classes: Value Objects, IDs & Exceptions

  This is where code finally begins. The key principle: build bottom-up, because
  that's the dependency order.

  ▎ Value objects depend on nothing → entities depend on value objects → 
  ▎ services depend on entities. So we build the foundation first.

  Why foundation-first pays off: if Money guarantees it can never be negative,
  then every entity holding a Money inherits that guarantee for free. Get the
  small immutable pieces airtight, and the big mutable pieces have far less to
  guard.

  What 8a builds (in order)

  Five files, all in domain/ — pure Python, zero framework imports:

  1. exceptions.py — the DomainError hierarchy. Built first because everything
     imports it. Named errors (not generic ValueError) so the name documents the
     rule (BorrowingLimitExceededError), and step 11's API can catch the base
     DomainError once → map to 400 (Open/Closed).
  2. ids.py — typed IDs via a frozen EntityId base. The payoff is BookId("abc") 
     != CopyId("abc") — different types never equal, so the "swapped arguments"
     bug becomes impossible. frozen=True also gives free hashing (IDs as dict
     keys for repositories).
  3. enums.py — CopyStatus, LoanStatus, FineStatus. The legal alphabet of states
     (enums, not typo-able strings); 8b adds the grammar (which transitions are
     legal — the State pattern).
  4. value_objects.py — the real building blocks: Money, ISBN, DateRange. Each
     immutable, self-validating, equal-by-value — the step-3 definition made
     concrete.

  The three design decisions worth internalizing

  These connect directly to everything we discussed:

  - Money as int paise, never float — 0.1 + 0.2 != 0.3 in floating point;
    integer paise makes money arithmetic exact. And add/multiply return a new
    Money (the immutability-replace rule from §3.6, in code).
  - Invariants in __post_init__ — a negative Money, malformed ISBN, or inverted
    DateRange simply cannot be constructed → never propagates → entities in 8b
    can blindly trust their VOs. (This is "make illegal states unrepresentable"
    from step 4.)
  - DateRange.days_overdue() returns a fact, not a price — "12 days late," never
    "₹60." The fact lives here; the ₹ policy waits for the fine Strategy in 8b.
    The fact-vs-policy boundary from steps 4 & 7, starting right at this
    method.

  The result: ~80 lines that make whole bug classes impossible (negative money,
  ID mix-ups, bad ISBNs, float rounding, typo'd statuses) — all caught at the
  boundary of creation.

  ---

  ⚠️ One thing I need to flag before we write code. The repo already has
  src/domain/value_objects/money.py (a 30-line Money, written ahead of the
  lesson), but this doc lays out a flat domain/value_objects.py module. So
  there's a structure decision to make when we start: keep the doc's flat
  layout, or use a value_objects/ package (money.py, isbn.py, date_range.py).
  I'll also want to read the existing money.py to reconcile it rather than
  blindly overwrite.

  Per your teaching pace, I've taught the lesson and I'm holding off on writing 
  any code. When you're ready, tell me to start — and I'll begin with
  exceptions.py (the dependency root). Two quick decisions I'll need from you
  then:

  1. Flat value_objects.py (as the doc shows) or a value_objects/ package
     (matches the existing money.py)?
  2. Start by reconciling the existing money.py, or write the foundation fresh
     in dependency order (exceptions → ids → enums → values)?

  Say the word when you want code.

❯  loan can have how many bookcopy ?                                            

⏺ A Loan references exactly one BookCopy — one loan, one copy.

  From the ubiquitous language (CLAUDE.md): a Loan = "a specific copy out to a 
  specific member, with a due date." Singular copy, singular member. The Loan
  holds one copy_id and one member_id.

  The cardinalities around it (from step 6):

  ┌───────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Relationship  │                       Cardinality                       │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Loan →        │ exactly 1 — a loan covers one physical copy             │
  │ BookCopy      │                                                         │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ BookCopy →    │ 1 ──< * over time, but 0..1 active — a copy has many    │
  │ Loan          │ loans across its life, at most one active at any moment │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Member → Loan │ 0..2 active — a member borrows up to 2 copies, which    │
  │               │ means up to 2 active loans                              │
  └───────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  So the "borrow at most 2 copies" rule (your CLAUDE.md invariant) doesn't mean
  one loan holding 2 copies — it means 2 separate Loan records, each for one
  copy. One loan is always one copy.

  This is the keystone rule showing up in cardinality: you issue a BookCopy, 
  never a Book — and each issue creates one Loan for that one copy. If a member
  borrows 2 books, that's 2 copies → 2 loans.

  Want to keep going, or back to step 8?

✻ Brewed for 11s

❯ in microservices, aggregate is one service ?                                  

⏺ Good question, and the answer is a firm no — an aggregate is not a 
  microservice. This is one of the most common and damaging DDD misconceptions,
  so let me lay out the right mapping.

  The correct unit: Bounded Context, not Aggregate

  ▎ A microservice boundary should align (at most) with a Bounded Context, not 
  ▎ an aggregate. An aggregate is far too small to be a service.

  The granularity, from largest to smallest:

  Bounded Context   ← a microservice maps here (at most)
     └── contains several Aggregates
            └── each contains entities + value objects

  An aggregate is a consistency boundary (a few objects that change together in
  one transaction). A bounded context is a meaning boundary (a whole subdomain
  with its own ubiquitous language). A service built per-aggregate would be
  absurdly fine-grained.

  Why "one aggregate = one service" is a trap

  If you made each aggregate its own service, in our LMS you'd get five services
  — Book, BookCopy, Member, Loan, Fine — and immediately suffer:

  1. Chatty network calls for one use case. "Borrow a book" touches Member +
     BookCopy + Loan. Three services = three network round-trips, with
     failure/retry/latency on each, for one button click.
  2. Distributed transactions everywhere. The borrow flow now spans service
     boundaries → you're forced into sagas / eventual consistency for what was a
     single local DB transaction. Enormous complexity for ~100 members.
  3. Aggregates that change together are torn apart. Things designed to be
     transactionally consistent now live in separate databases. You've maximized
     coupling across the network — the opposite of the goal.

  The right mapping for our system

  Our CLAUDE.md already made this call:

  ▎ "One bounded context (the Library context) — deliberately not microservices 
  ▎ for this scale."

  So:

  ┌────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Concept     │                        Our LMS                         │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Bounded        │ the Library context — one deployable (could be one     │
  │ Context        │ microservice, but we chose a modular monolith)         │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Aggregates     │ Book, BookCopy, Member, Loan, Fine — all in that one   │
  │ inside it      │ service                                                │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                │ Lending (core), Catalog, Membership, Fines,            │
  │ Subdomains     │ Notifications — internal modules, not separate         │
  │                │ services                                               │
  └────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  If this ever did go microservices, you'd split along subdomain / 
  bounded-context lines — e.g. a Lending service and a Catalog service — each
  owning several aggregates, never one service per aggregate.

  The nuance: aggregates guide where you could split

  Here's what's true and useful: aggregates are still relevant to microservices
  — they tell you where a split is safe. Because aggregates already reference
  each other by ID, not by object (step 5), the seams between them are clean. So
  a service boundary should fall between aggregates, never through one — you
  never split an aggregate across two services (that would put a single
  transaction across the network). Aggregates are the grain; the cut follows the
  grain, but each plank holds many grains.

  ▎ One-liner: a microservice ≈ a bounded context (a subdomain), which contains 
  ▎ many aggregates. An aggregate is a transactional consistency boundary — keep
  ▎ it whole inside one service; let service boundaries fall between 
  ▎ aggregates, never split them.

  So for us: one bounded context → one service (we chose a monolith), holding 
  all five aggregates. Splitting per-aggregate would be textbook
  over-engineering — exactly the KISS/YAGNI default the project holds to.

  Want me to capture this aggregate-vs-bounded-context-vs-microservice mapping
  in the docs?

❯ so, in step1 the subdomains categorized are eligible for microservices in     
  case our system grows beyond ?                                                

⏺ Yes — exactly right. Those subdomains are the natural fault lines along which
  you'd split if the system outgrew one deployable. But let me add the precision
  that turns "roughly right" into "correctly right."

  Why subdomains are the candidate seams

  The subdomains from step 1 are:

  ┌───────────────────┬────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Subdomain     │    Type    │         Microservice candidate?         │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Lending /         │ core       │ ✅ strong — the busiest, most likely to │
  │ Circulation       │            │  need independent scaling               │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Catalog           │ supporting │ ✅ — different load profile (read-heavy │
  │                   │            │  search)                                │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Membership        │ supporting │ ✅                                      │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Fines / Billing   │ supporting │ ✅ — may even integrate with real       │
  │                   │            │ payment systems later                   │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Notifications     │ generic    │ ✅ — classic extract-first candidate    │
  │                   │            │ (often off-the-shelf)                   │
  └───────────────────┴────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┘

  They're good candidates because of why they were grouped in the first place: a
  subdomain is a cluster of high cohesion inside, low coupling outside — which
  is exactly the property you want at a service boundary. Splitting along seams
  that already have low coupling means few cross-service calls. Splitting across
  a cohesive subdomain would create chatty, fragile services. So the work you
  did in step 1 (finding subdomains) is also the work of finding where future
  services could live.

  The one nuance: subdomain → bounded context → service

  The chain has a middle step people skip:

  ▎ Subdomain (problem space — a slice of the business) → Bounded Context 
  ▎ (solution space — a model with its own language + boundary) → Microservice 
  ▎ (a deployable).

  A subdomain is a candidate; it becomes a service only once you give it its own
  bounded context — its own model, its own data, talking to others only through
  explicit contracts (APIs/events), not shared objects or a shared database.
  Right now all five subdomains live inside one bounded context (the Library
  context) as internal modules. To extract one, you'd "promote" it to its own
  context first.

  What makes the promotion cheap (and what doesn't)

  Here's the payoff of the DDD work we've done — it determines how expensive the
  future split is:

  - ✅ Aggregates reference each other by ID, not by object (step 5). So Loan
    already holds member_id, not a Member — pulling Membership into its own
    service means that ID becomes a remote lookup instead of a local one. The
    seam is pre-cut.
  - ✅ Cross-subdomain rules already live in domain services / events — not
    tangled inside entities. So "≤ 2 active loans" is already a clean
    coordination point.
  - ⚠️ The transaction that spans aggregates (the borrow flow touches BookCopy +
    Loan) is the thing to watch. As long as those stay in the same subdomain
    (Lending), you keep a local transaction. If a split would put them in
    different services, you'd convert to a saga / eventual consistency — and
    that cost is what should drive the decision, not fashion.

  The honest framing

  ▎ The subdomains are your pre-identified service seams — keep them as clean 
  ▎ internal module boundaries now, and the day scale genuinely demands it, you 
  ▎ extract a subdomain into its own bounded context + service along a seam 
  ▎ that's already low-coupling. Notifications and Fines are the usual first to 
  ▎ leave; Lending (core) is the last thing you'd fragment.

  But — and this is the project's KISS/YAGNI spirit — don't pre-split. Keep the
  seams visible and clean (separate modules, by-ID references, no
  cross-subdomain object reach-ins) so the option stays cheap, but stay a
  modular monolith until a real scaling or team-autonomy pressure forces a
  split. The modular monolith is the design that keeps microservices a future
  option without paying their cost today.

  So your instinct is correct: step 1's subdomains = the microservice candidates
  — with the refinement that each must first become its own bounded context,
  and you extract them lazily (along the seams already cut by by-ID references),
  driven by real pressure, not anticipation.

  Want this captured in the docs (it'd fit step 1, or step 13/15 where
  deployment models are discussed)?

⏺ Step 8b — Aggregate Roots & Patterns

  8a gave us airtight value objects. Now we compose them into behavior-rich 
  aggregate roots that enforce every step-7 contract — and introduce two
  patterns exactly where the design demanded them (never preemptively):

  - Strategy for fine calculation (the "₹5/day might change" policy seam)
  - State for the loan lifecycle (the active → returned divergent behavior)

  The discipline to watch: build the simplest thing that works first, reach for 
  a pattern only when the simple thing starts to hurt. Watching when that
  happens is the real lesson.

  The shared entity shape

  Every entity carries (from step 3) a typed identity and equality by identity, 
  not attributes:

  def __eq__(self, other): return isinstance(other, BookCopy) and self.id ==
  other.id
  def __hash__(self):      return hash(self.id)

  No shared Entity base class — explicit is fine; a base earns its place only
  when shared behavior grows (KISS).

  The two design decisions worth pausing on

  1. BookCopy uses a transition table, NOT the State pattern. It has a rich
  status lifecycle but no divergent behavior per state — issue/return/damage are
  just "is this move legal?" So the simplest correct tool is a _LEGAL_MOVES
  dict guarding transitions. _status is private → the only path to a new status
  is a guarded command. No pattern needed.

  2. Loan is the State-pattern candidate — and here's the honest part.
  Loan.return_copy() behaves differently by state (ACTIVE → compute lateness;
  RETURNED → raise I-8). That divergence is the State-pattern signal. The doc
  builds it properly (a LoanState ABC with ActiveState/ReturnedState) — then 
  argues you probably shouldn't use it for two states. The enum-guard version
  (if status == RETURNED: raise) is shorter and clearer. State earns its keep
  only when states multiply (5+) and behavior genuinely diverges across many
  operations and you keep adding states.

  ▎ Knowing a pattern includes knowing when not to use it. This is the most 
  ▎ important meta-lesson in the chapter — judgment over dogma.

  Where the patterns genuinely pay off

  Strategy for fines is the clean win. "₹5/day" is a policy that will change →
  hard-coding it in Loan/Fine violates OCP. Instead, a FineCalculationStrategy
  interface with swappable implementations (StandardFineStrategy,
  GracePeriodFineStrategy, CappedFineStrategy). Adding a new policy touches zero
  existing code — textbook OCP + LSP + DIP. And it's the fact-vs-policy split
  from steps 4 & 7 finally wired: Loan.days_overdue() produces the fact, the
  Strategy turns it into Money (the policy).

  Loan.create() is a Factory Method — the only way to mint a loan, guaranteeing
  the due-date invariant (I-3) at birth. You can't construct an invalid loan.

  Member has a deliberate absence — no can_borrow(), no loan count (the step-7
  surprise, now in code with a comment explaining why). The aggregate boundary
  shows up as a missing method.

  ---

  This completes the domain layer in prose. Everything from steps 4–7 pays off
  here as readable, self-protecting code.

  ⚠️ Same flag as 8a, now bigger: this doc uses a domain/entities/ +

❯ I am planning to follow below structure,

  │   │       └── money.py                                                      
  │   └── infrastructure                                                        
  │       └── __init__.py

      └── infrastructure                                                        

⏺ Good — this is a clean, conventional layout and it matches the four-layer
  architecture exactly. The tests/ tree mirroring src/ is the right instinct.
  Let me give you my honest read, because there's one trade-off worth deciding
  consciously before we write anything.

  What's clearly right ✅

  - value_objects/ as a package with one VO per file (money.py, isbn.py,
    date_range.py) — this is the better call than the doc's flat
    value_objects.py. VOs each have validation + behavior, so one-file-each
    stays readable and reconciles with the existing money.py.
  - tests/ mirroring src/ — standard, and pythonpath=["src"] already supports
    it.
  - Layer dirs (api/application/domain/infrastructure) — spot on.

  The one thing to decide: packages vs. modules for the small stuff

  You've made enums/, exceptions/, ids/ into directories (packages). Ask
  yourself what goes inside each:

  ┌────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │  Concern   │        Realistic content        │   A package buys you…    │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ exceptions │ one DomainError tree, ~10       │ nothing — they're tiny   │
  │            │ classes                         │ and cohesive             │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ ids        │ one EntityId base + 5 one-line  │ nothing — they're        │
  │            │ subclasses                      │ trivial                  │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ enums      │ 3 enums (CopyStatus,            │ nothing — all small      │
  │            │ LoanStatus, FineStatus)         │                          │
  └────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  For these three, a single module (exceptions.py, ids.py, enums.py) is the
  KISS-correct choice — splitting ~10 trivial lines across a package directory +
  __init__.py is structure without payoff, and it fights the project's YAGNI
  default. A package earns its place when content grows enough that one file
  gets unwieldy — which is true for value_objects/ (real logic per file) but not
  for the others.

  My recommendation:

  domain/
  ├── __init__.py
  ├── exceptions.py          # module — the DomainError hierarchy
  ├── ids.py                 # module — EntityId + typed IDs
  ├── enums.py               # module — the three status enums
  └── value_objects/         # package — one VO per file (real logic)
      ├── __init__.py
      ├── money.py
      ├── isbn.py
      └── date_range.py

  This is a judgment call, not a rule — if you prefer the uniform package look
  (everything a directory), that's perfectly defensible too; it just costs a
  little ceremony now. Your project, your call.

  Which do you want?

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · How should the small domain concerns (exceptions, ids, enums) be 
     structured? → Modules for small, package for VOs (Recommended)

⏺ Settled — here's the layout we'll build:

  src/domain/

  ├── exceptions.py          # module — DomainError hierarchy
  ├── ids.py                 # module — EntityId + typed IDs
  ├── enums.py               # module — CopyStatus, LoanStatus, FineStatus
  └── value_objects/         # package — one VO per file
      ├── __init__.py
      ├── money.py           # (already exists — we'll reconcile it)
      ├── isbn.py

  alongside.

✻ Churned for 36s

❯ what is the role of api, application, domain, infrastructure, tests ?         

⏺ Here's each layer's job, in the order dependencies flow (outermost → innermost
  → outermost again). The golden rule first:

  ▎ Dependencies point inward. domain is the centre and depends on nothing. 
  ▎ Everything depends on the domain; the domain depends on no one.

     api ──► application ──► domain ◄── infrastructure
    (HTTP)   (use cases)    (rules)    (DB/adapters)
                              ▲
                         depends on NOTHING

  domain/ — the business rules (the heart)

  Role: the model — entities, value objects, aggregates, domain services, and
  the repository interfaces. All the what's true and what's allowed in the
  business.
  Knows about: nothing but itself. Pure Python, zero framework imports (no
  FastAPI, no ORM, no DB driver).
  LMS examples: Money, ISBN, BookCopy.issue(), Loan, the ≤2 loans rule,
  FineCalculationStrategy.
  Why isolated: so the rules are trivially unit-testable and never break when
  you swap a database or web framework.

  application/ — the use-case orchestrator

  Role: runs each use case as a recipe — load → decide → mutate → save. It's the
  transaction boundary. Contains orchestration, not business rules.
  Knows about: the domain, and repository interfaces (injected). Never imports
  infrastructure directly.
  LMS examples: borrow_book(member_id, copy_id), return_book(loan_id) — fetch
  the aggregates, call the domain service / entity methods, save. (This is the
  layer that's allowed to hold repositories — the answer to your earlier "should
  Member have list_all_loans?" → no, it lives here.)
  Rule of thumb: if you see an if enforcing a business rule here, it's misplaced
  — push it into the domain.

  infrastructure/ — the adapters (the "how")

  Role: concrete implementations of the domain's interfaces — real persistence,
  external systems, the mechanical details.
  Knows about: the domain (it implements its interfaces) + actual tech (DB
  driver, Redis, SMTP).
  LMS examples: SqlLoanRepository implementing the LoanRepository port, a
  RedisLockManager, a SystemClock, an email Notifier. This is where "behind the
  port" lives — the version-check optimistic locking from our concurrency
  discussion sits here.
  Why outermost: it's the most likely to change (swap Postgres for SQLite, email
  for SMS) — so it depends inward on the stable domain, never the reverse.

  api/ — the HTTP edge (thin translator)

  Role: translate HTTP ↔ application services. Parse the request, validate input
  (not invariants — that's the domain), call an application service, map the
  result/exception to an HTTP response.
  Knows about: the application layer + FastAPI/Pydantic.
  LMS examples: POST /loans → parse body → call borrow_book(...) → catch
  DomainError → return 400. Keep it thin — no business logic, no orchestration.
  (Recall step 8a: catch the base DomainError once → map to 400.)

  tests/ — the safety net (mirrors src/)

  Role: verify each layer, with the easiest tests concentrated where the value
  is — the domain.
  Structure: mirrors src/ (tests/domain/, tests/application/, …).
  The payoff of the layering: because domain/ is pure,
  tests/domain/test_money.py runs with plain objects — no DB, no mocks, instant.
  Application tests use fake/in-memory repositories. Infrastructure tests are
  the few that touch real I/O. The purity you bought in domain/ is what makes
  the bulk of your tests fast and trivial.

  The whole thing in one table

  ┌────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │     Layer      │       Job        │    Imports     │    LMS example     │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │                │ HTTP ↔ app       │ application,   │ POST /loans        │
  │ api            │ services; input  │ FastAPI        │ controller         │
  │                │ validation       │                │                    │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │                │ orchestrate use  │ domain, repo   │                    │
  │ application    │ cases;           │ interfaces     │ borrow_book()      │
  │                │ transactions     │                │                    │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ domain         │ business rules;  │ nothing        │ BookCopy.issue(),  │
  │                │ pure             │                │ Money              │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │                │ implement the    │ domain + real  │                    │
  │ infrastructure │ interfaces (DB,  │ tech           │ SqlLoanRepository  │
  │                │ etc.)            │                │                    │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ tests          │ verify each      │ the layer      │ test_money.py      │
  │                │ layer            │ under test     │                    │
  └────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────────────┴────────────────────┘

  ▎ The mental model: domain = what's true · application = what to do, in what 
  ▎ order · infrastructure = how it physically happens · api = how the outside 
  ▎ world asks · tests = proof it all holds. The arrows always point at the 
  ▎ domain — that's what keeps the rules pure and everything else swappable.

  Want this added to the docs, or shall we keep going / start coding?
