✻ Baked for 36s

❯ Add library_management_system to python path so that vscode does not show     
  error.                                                                        

  Listed 1 directory (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The issue is exactly this: pythonpath = ["src"] in pyproject.toml only
  configures pytest — VSCode's Pylance doesn't read pytest config for import
  resolution. So from domain.value_objects.money import Money-style imports show
  as unresolved in the editor even though tests run fine.

  The fix is to tell Pylance where the source root is via .vscode/settings.json:

⏺ Write(.vscode/settings.json)
  ⎿  Wrote 8 lines to .vscode/settings.json
      1 {
      2   // Make Pylance resolve imports the same way pytest does (pytho
        npath = ["src"]).
      3   // Domain code imports as `from domain...`, `from application..
        .`, etc.
      4   "python.analysis.extraPaths": ["src"],
      5   "python.testing.pytestEnabled": true,
      6   "python.testing.unittestEnabled": false,
      7   "python.testing.pytestArgs": ["tests"]
      8 }

⏺ Created .vscode/settings.json with:

  - python.analysis.extraPaths: ["src"] — the actual fix. Pylance now resolves
    from domain..., from application... etc. as a root inside src/, matching
    pytest's pythonpath = ["src"]. No more red squiggles on intra-project
    imports.
  - pytest wiring (pytestEnabled, args ["tests"]) — bonus, so the VSCode Test
    Explorer discovers and runs your tests using the same config.

  Two things to know

  1. This assumes VSCode is opened at the library_management_system_v2/ folder.
  extraPaths is relative to the workspace root. If you instead open the parent

❯ what is difference between src/doamin/services vs application/ ?              

⏺ This is the single most important distinction in step 9 — and the most
  commonly conflated. Both are "services," but they answer completely different
  questions.

  The one-line split

  ▎ A domain service answers a business question ("is this allowed?"). An 
  ▎ application service runs a use case ("carry out this request: fetch, decide,
  ▎ save, commit").
  ▎ The domain service decides; the application service orchestrates.

  Side by side

  ┌─────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │             │           Domain Service            │ Application Service │
  │             │       (src/domain/services/)        │  (src/application/) │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Layer       │ domain/ — pure                      │ application/        │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │             │ business rules that span multiple   │ orchestration — the │
  │ Contains    │ aggregates                          │  recipe for a use   │
  │             │                                     │ case                │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Business    │ Yes — it is domain logic            │ No — it only        │
  │ logic?      │                                     │ coordinates         │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Talks to a  │                                     │ Yes — loads/saves   │
  │ DB /        │ Never — it's given its data         │ via repository      │
  │ repository? │                                     │ interfaces          │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Transaction │                                     │ Yes — it's the      │
  │ s?          │ No                                  │ transaction         │
  │             │                                     │ boundary            │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │             │                                     │ repositories, the   │
  │ Knows about │ domain objects only                 │ domain service,     │
  │             │                                     │ ports (Clock,       │
  │             │                                     │ Notifier)           │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ LMS example │ BorrowingService.check_can_borrow(m │ borrow_book(member_ │
  │             │ ember, copy, count)                 │ id, copy_id)        │
  └─────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  In our LMS, concretely

  Domain service — BorrowingService (pure rule, spans aggregates):
  # domain/services/borrowing.py
  class BorrowingService:
      def check_can_borrow(self, member, copy, active_loan_count) -> None:
          if not member.is_active:               raise MemberBlockedError(...)
          if not copy.is_available:              raise
  CopyNotAvailableError(...)
          if active_loan_count >= 2:             raise
  BorrowingLimitExceededError(...)
          # decides "is this allowed?" — touches NO database

  Application service — borrow_book (orchestration, transactions):
  # application/services.py
  def borrow_book(self, member_id, copy_id):
      member = self._members.get(member_id)          # LOAD (repo)
      copy   = self._copies.get(copy_id)
      active = self._loans.count_active(member.id)

      self._borrowing.check_can_borrow(member, copy, active)  # DECIDE (domain 
  service)

      copy.issue()                                    # MUTATE
      loan = Loan.create(member.id, copy.id, today)
      self._copies.save(copy); self._loans.save(loan) # SAVE (repo)
      return str(loan.id)

  Notice: the application service has zero business rules — "can they borrow?"
  is delegated to the domain service; it only loads, calls, saves. The domain
  service has zero I/O — it's handed member, copy, count and just decides.

  Why split them (not bureaucracy)

  They change for different reasons → SRP says split:
  - Business knowledge ("is this allowed?") vs plumbing ("fetch/save/commit").
  - The domain service is testable with three plain objects — no DB. If you
    crammed the rules into borrow_book alongside repo.get/save, you couldn't
    test the rule without persistence.
  - The rule becomes reusable — a "what can I borrow?" preview screen calls the
    same check_can_borrow without dragging in the save-everything machinery.

  ▎ 🍳 Analogy: the domain service is the chef's judgment ("needs more salt") — 
  ▎ pure expertise, portable to any kitchen. The application service is the 
  ▎ recipe steps ("fetch from fridge, preheat, plate, serve") — logistics. Keep 
  ▎ judgment separate and every recipe can call on the chef.

  The honest nuance

  Not every use case needs a domain service. A use case with no cross-aggregate
  rule — "update a book's title" — just calls book.update_metadata() and saves;
  there's no domain service, only an application service. Create a domain
  service only when genuine business logic spans aggregates. Forcing an empty
  domain service onto every use case is the "service layer per aggregate"
  over-engineering the project explicitly avoids.

  ▎ The deciding test: does the logic need data from one aggregate? → method on 
  ▎ that aggregate. Several aggregates? → domain service. No business logic, 
  ▎ just fetch/call/save? → application service.

  This is all in step 9 (§9.1–9.3) if you want the longer treatment. Want to
  move on with next, or start coding?

✻ Brewed for 22s

❯ sp, does that mean application layer first talks to domain service layer and  
  then domain layer ?                                                           
⏺ Careful — there's a subtle but important correction here. The domain service 
  is not a separate layer "above" the domain — it lives inside the domain layer.
  So it's not three layers in a waterfall; it's two layers, and the domain
  layer happens to contain both domain services and entities.

  The structure (only two layers involved)

  application/                          ← orchestration
     │ calls into ↓
  domain/                               ← ONE layer, contains BOTH:
     ├── services/  BorrowingService    (cross-aggregate rules)
     └── entities/  Member, BookCopy…   (single-aggregate rules)

  domain/services/ and domain/entities/ are siblings in the same layer, not
  stacked. So "application → domain service → domain" isn't quite right — the
  domain service is domain.

  The actual flow (not a strict order)

  The application service orchestrates, and within one use case it calls
  whatever it needs in the domain — sometimes a domain service, sometimes entity
  methods directly, usually both:

  borrow_book():                                    [application layer]
    member = repo.get(...)        # LOAD   ── via repository
    copy   = repo.get(...)
    active = repo.count(...)

    borrowing.check_can_borrow(member, copy, active) # DECIDE ── domain SERVICE
    copy.issue()                                     # MUTATE ── domain ENTITY
  (direct!)
    loan = Loan.create(...)                          #        ── domain ENTITY
  (direct!)

    repo.save(copy); repo.save(loan)                 # SAVE   ── via repository

  Notice: the application service calls the domain service (check_can_borrow)
  and calls entity methods (copy.issue(), Loan.create()) directly — it doesn't
  have to route everything through the domain service. There's no rule "service
  first, then entities."

  When does it call which?

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │        What the application service needs         │    What it calls    │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ A rule spanning multiple aggregates ("can this    │ the domain service  │
  │ member borrow this copy?")                        │                     │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ A rule/command on one aggregate ("issue this      │ the entity method   │
  │ copy", "create this loan")                        │ directly            │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Load/save                                         │ a repository        │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  And a domain service, when it runs, itself calls entity methods (e.g.
  member.is_active, copy.is_available) — it's handed the loaded objects and
  reads/uses them. So entities get touched both directly by the application
  service and indirectly through the domain service.

  The corrected mental model

  ▎ The application layer orchestrates a use case by calling into the domain 
  ▎ layer — which contains both domain services and entities as siblings. It 
  ▎ calls a domain service for cross-aggregate decisions and entity methods for 
  ▎ single-aggregate commands, in whatever order the use case needs (typically: 
  ▎ load → decide → mutate → save). It's not a fixed "service-then-entity" 
  ▎ waterfall, and the domain service is not a layer above the domain — it's 
  ▎ part of it.

  So: not "application → domain service → domain" (three tiers). It's
  application → domain, where inside the domain the application freely calls
  both services and entities. The "many use cases need no domain service at all"
  point from before is the proof — for "update a book's title," the application
  service skips any domain service and calls book.update_metadata() directly.

  Does that reframe it? The key fix: domain service ⊂ domain layer, sibling to
  entities — not a separate tier in between.

✻ Sautéed for 22s

❯ how many entity class ?                                                       

⏺ Five entity classes — and in our model each is also its own aggregate root
  (step 5):

  ┌─────┬──────────┬─────────────────┬────────────────┐
  │  #  │  Entity  │    Aggregate    │   Subdomain    │
  ├─────┼──────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ 1   │ Book     │ Book (root)     │ Catalog        │
  ├─────┼──────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ 2   │ BookCopy │ BookCopy (root) │ Catalog        │
  ├─────┼──────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ 3   │ Member   │ Member (root)   │ Membership     │
  ├─────┼──────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ 4   │ Loan     │ Loan (root)     │ Lending (core) │
  ├─────┼──────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ 5   │ Fine     │ Fine (root)     │ Fines          │
  └─────┴──────────┴─────────────────┴────────────────┘

  So 5 entities = 5 aggregates = 5 repositories (one repo per aggregate root).
  They map 1:1 here precisely because we kept aggregates small — none contains a
  child entity, so there are no "inner" entities beyond these five.

  Don't confuse these with entities

  A few classes look like they belong on the list but aren't entities:

  - Value objects (no identity, immutable): Money, ISBN, DateRange — not
    entities.
  - Typed IDs: BookId, CopyId, MemberId, LoanId, FineId — these are value
    objects too, not entities.
  - Enums: CopyStatus, LoanStatus, FineStatus — value objects (a fixed set of
    values).
  - Services: BorrowingService (domain), FineCalculationStrategy — behavior, not
    entities.
  - Librarian / Admin — these are actors/roles, not domain entities (until we
    add auth in the production steps). From step 3's classification.

  So the clean count: 5 entities (Book, BookCopy, Member, Loan, Fine), built
  from the value objects, guarded by the services.

✻ Brewed for 10s

❯ how many domain service classes ?                                             

⏺ One true domain service: BorrowingService. There's a second resident of
  domain/services/ — the FineCalculationStrategy family — but whether you count
  it as a "domain service" depends on how strictly you define the term. Let me
  give the honest breakdown.

  The one classic domain service

  BorrowingService — the textbook case: a stateless object holding a business
  rule that spans multiple aggregates:
  - check_can_borrow(member, copy, active_count) — needs Member (active?) +
    BookCopy (available?) + Loan count (< 2?).
  - Enforces "≤ 2 active loans" (Member + Loan).

  No single aggregate owns this, so it can't be an entity method → it's a domain
  service. ✅

  The borderline one

  FineCalculationStrategy (with implementations StandardFineStrategy,
  GracePeriodFineStrategy, …) also lives in domain/services/, but it's a
  Strategy, not a classic spanning-rule domain service:
  - It's pure domain logic (days_overdue → Money), stateless, framework-free —
    so it's domain-layer code.
  - But it doesn't "span aggregates"; it encapsulates a swappable policy. It's a
    domain service in the loose sense ("a stateless domain operation that isn't
    a natural method on any entity"), but a Strategy in the precise sense.

  So the count, two ways

  ┌─────────────────────────────┬────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │         Definition          │   Count    │            Which            │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Strictly "cross-aggregate   │ 1          │ BorrowingService            │
  │ business rule"              │            │                             │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Loosely "stateless domain   │ 2          │ BorrowingService +          │
  │ operations in               │ (families) │ FineCalculationStrategy     │
  │ domain/services/"           │            │                             │
  └─────────────────────────────┴────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  Both interpretations are fine; just know why they differ.

  Why so few — and that's correct

  Domain services are deliberately rare. Most behavior lives inside the 
  aggregates (Information Expert, step 7) — copy.issue(), loan.return_copy(),
  fine.pay(), book.update_metadata() are all entity methods, not services. A
  service appears only when logic genuinely fits no single aggregate. Two such
  pieces in the whole system is healthy — a long list of domain services usually
  signals an anemic model (logic that should be on the entities leaking out
  into services).

  ▎ Bottom line: 1 strict domain service (BorrowingService), plus the 
  ▎ FineCalculationStrategy policy family sharing domain/services/. Everything 
  ▎ else is entity methods — exactly the rich-domain shape we want.

❯ how to write class of just Book Entity ? Can you guide me ?                   

⏺ Absolutely. Let me guide you through it the way the design intends — building
  it up piece by piece so each line has a reason, not just pasting a finished
  class. Book is the gentlest entity (Catalog is a supporting subdomain —
  "boringly simple"), so it's the perfect first one.

  We'll answer four questions from the step-7 contract, in order.

  Step 1 — What is Book's contract? (recall step 7)

  Book : update_metadata · matches(filter)   | guards: valid ISBN, non-empty
  title/author
         Must NOT: manage its copies · know about loans/members

  So: identity + a few fields, one command, one query, a couple of invariants.
  That's the whole job.

  Step 2 — Identity & fields

  A Book is an entity → it has a typed identity (BookId) and is equal by id, not
  attributes. Its fields are catalog metadata. Note the types:

  - id: BookId — typed id (not a raw str) — kills primitive obsession.
  - isbn: ISBN — a value object (already validated in 8a) — so Book can trust
    it's well-formed.
  - title, author, genre — plain str here. Per our step-3 rule ("don't wrap dumb
    scalars"), these have no rules beyond "non-empty," so primitives are fine.

  from domain.ids import BookId
  from domain.value_objects import ISBN

  class Book:
      def __init__(self, book_id: BookId, isbn: ISBN, title: str,
                   author: str, genre: str | None = None):
          self.id = book_id
          self.isbn = isbn
          self.title = title
          self.author = author
          self.genre = genre

  (Imports use domain.ids / domain.value_objects — matching your src-on-path
  layout and the value_objects/__init__.py that re-exports ISBN.)

  Step 3 — Guard the invariants at construction

  Step 4's principle: make illegal states unconstructable. ISBN validity is
  already guaranteed (it's a VO). What's left for Book to guard: non-empty 
  title/author. Validate in __init__ and store the cleaned value:

      def __init__(self, book_id, isbn, title, author, genre=None):
          if not title.strip():
              raise ValueError("Book title cannot be empty")
          if not author.strip():
              raise ValueError("Book author cannot be empty")
          self.id, self.isbn = book_id, isbn
          self.title = title.strip()
          self.author = author.strip()
          self.genre = genre.strip() if genre else None

  ▎ Why a plain ValueError and not a DomainError? This is input-shape validation
  ▎ (step 4's "validation vs invariant" split), not a business-rule violation —
  ▎ ValueError is honest here. A business invariant like the borrowing limit 
  ▎ gets a named DomainError.

  Step 4 — Equality by identity

  Two Book objects are "the same book" iff same id — even if a title was
  corrected. This is the entity trait from step 3:

      def __eq__(self, other) -> bool:
          return isinstance(other, Book) and self.id == other.id
      def __hash__(self) -> int:
          return hash(self.id)

  Step 5 — The command and the query (CQS)

  One command (changes state, returns nothing) and one query (returns data, no
  side effects) — keeping them separate is CQS from step 7:

      def update_metadata(self, *, title=None, genre=None) -> None:   # COMMAND
          if title is not None:
              if not title.strip():
                  raise ValueError("title cannot be empty")
              self.title = title.strip()
          if genre is not None:
              self.genre = genre.strip()

      def matches(self, keyword: str) -> bool:                        # QUERY 
  (UC-7 search)
          k = keyword.lower()
          return k in self.title.lower() or k in self.author.lower()

  Note update_metadata lets you fix title/genre but not the ISBN or author —
  those identify the work; you don't "edit" them. The method shape encodes that
  rule.

  The whole class

  # src/domain/entities/book.py
  from domain.ids import BookId
  from domain.value_objects import ISBN


  class Book:
      """Catalog metadata for an abstract work. You never issue a Book — you 
  issue a BookCopy."""

      def __init__(self, book_id: BookId, isbn: ISBN, title: str,
                   author: str, genre: str | None = None):
          if not title.strip():
              raise ValueError("Book title cannot be empty")
          if not author.strip():
              raise ValueError("Book author cannot be empty")
          self.id = book_id
          self.isbn = isbn
          self.title = title.strip()
          self.author = author.strip()
          self.genre = genre.strip() if genre else None

      def __eq__(self, other) -> bool:
          return isinstance(other, Book) and self.id == other.id

      def __hash__(self) -> int:
          return hash(self.id)

      def update_metadata(self, *, title: str | None = None, genre: str | None =
  None) -> None:
          if title is not None:
              if not title.strip():
                  raise ValueError("title cannot be empty")
              self.title = title.strip()
          if genre is not None:
              self.genre = genre.strip()

      def matches(self, keyword: str) -> bool:
          k = keyword.lower()
          return k in self.title.lower() or k in self.author.lower()

  What Book deliberately does NOT have (the step-7 "must not")

  - ❌ No copies list / add_copy() — BookCopy is a separate aggregate; Book
    references nothing about copies. (Cross-aggregate = by ID, the copy holds
    book_id, not the reverse.)
  - ❌ No knowledge of loans or members.
  - ❌ No repository access — it acts only on data it holds.

  That absence is the design working: Book stays a small, focused catalog
  entity.

  ---

  ⚠️ One prerequisite: this imports BookId (from domain/ids.py) and ISBN (from
  domain/value_objects/), neither of which exists yet — we've only got money.py.
  Per dependency order, those should come first.

  Want me to create this book.py file now (and the ids.py /
  value_objects/__init__.py it needs to import cleanly), or are you writing it
  yourself and just wanted the walkthrough? If you want me to write files, I'd
  suggest doing the foundation in order — exceptions.py → ids.py → enums.py → 
  value_objects/ — then entities like Book.

❯ but why this is required ?                                                    
⏺ Good — let me separate what's strictly required from what's a convenience 
  choice, because they're different.

  Required vs. optional

  Required fixes (the code is genuinely broken without these):
  - entity_exceptions.py had from lld.library_management_system_v2.src.domain...
    — that path doesn't exist on sys.path, so it would ModuleNotFoundError on
    import. Must fix.
  - value_objects.py (exceptions) referenced NegativeMoneyError /
    InvalidDateRangeError that didn't exist anywhere — anything importing them
    would crash. Must add.

  Those aren't style — without them, nothing imports.

  Optional (what you're asking about): the __init__.py re-export is not strictly
  required. You could skip it and import straight from the submodule:

  # works WITHOUT any __init__.py re-export:
  from domain.exceptions.value_objects import InvalidISBNError
  from domain.exceptions.entity_exceptions import CopyNotAvailableError

  So why did I add the re-export? Three reasons — but it's a judgment call, not
  a must:

  Why re-export in __init__.py

  1. A clean public API for the package. Callers say from domain.exceptions 
  import InvalidISBNError — they shouldn't need to know which file inside the
  package it lives in. The package presents itself as one unit; the internal
  split (domain_exceptions.py / value_objects.py / entity_exceptions.py) becomes
  a private organizational detail.

  2. Freedom to reorganize. If you later move InvalidISBNError to a different
  file, every caller using from domain.exceptions import InvalidISBNError keeps
  working — you only update the one re-export line. Without it, every call site
  hard-codes the submodule path and breaks when you move things.

  3. It matches what the docs/entities already assume. The doc code (and the
  Book we're about to write) imports from domain.exceptions import 
  CopyNotAvailableError, etc. — the short form. The re-export makes those lines
  work as written.

  The honest trade-off

  ┌───────────────┬─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │               │    Re-export in     │  Import from submodule directly   │
  │               │     __init__.py     │                                   │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ from                │ from                              │
  │ Call site     │ domain.exceptions   │ domain.exceptions.value_objects   │
  │               │ import              │ import InvalidISBNError           │
  │               │ InvalidISBNError    │                                   │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Hides         │                     │                                   │
  │ internal file │ ✅                  │ ❌ caller knows the file          │
  │  structure    │                     │                                   │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Survives      │                     │                                   │
  │ reorganizing  │ ✅                  │ ❌ breaks                         │
  │ files         │                     │                                   │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Extra code    │ one __init__.py     │ none                              │
  └───────────────┴─────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘

  For a package whose whole point is "here's the exception vocabulary," the
  re-export is the conventional, tidy choice — it's why you made exceptions/ a
  package rather than scattering files. But if you'd rather keep __init__.py
  empty and import from submodules explicitly, that's completely valid too —
  just more verbose at every call site.

  Want me to keep the re-export approach, or leave __init__.py empty and import 
  from submodules directly? Your call — I'll wire the rest (ids, value_objects,
  Book) to match whichever you pick.

❯ move to another entity and you tell me i will do it for this entity.          

⏺ Next entity: BookCopy — the keystone of the whole domain ("you issue a
  BookCopy, never a Book"). It's the most interesting of the simple entities
  because it has a real status lifecycle, so it teaches the lightweight
  state-machine technique. I'll give you the spec; you write it.

  The contract (from step 7)

  BookCopy : issue · return_copy · mark_damaged · mark_lost   (commands)
             is_available · is_visible_to_member             (queries)
             guards: I-2/I-7 — only an AVAILABLE copy can be issued; only legal
  transitions
             Must NOT: create Loans · calculate fines · know about Member

  What to build, piece by piece

  1. Fields
  - id: CopyId — its own identity.
  - book_id: BookId — cross-aggregate reference by ID (step 6). A copy points at
    its Book, never holds a Book object.
  - _status: CopyStatus — private (underscore), defaulting to AVAILABLE. Private
    is the whole point: the only way to change status is a guarded command, so
    no one can poke it into an illegal state.

  2. Equality by id — same __eq__/__hash__ shape as Book.

  3. The transition table — the key idea. BookCopy has a lifecycle but no 
  divergent behavior per state (issue/return/damage are just "is this move
  legal?"). So the simplest correct tool is a module-level dict of legal moves —
  not the State pattern. Something like:

  AVAILABLE → {LOANED, DAMAGED, LOST}
  LOANED    → {AVAILABLE, DAMAGED, LOST}
  DAMAGED   → {AVAILABLE}        # repaired back into circulation
  LOST      → {}                 # terminal — no way out

  4. A private _move_to(target) helper — checks target is in the legal set for
  the current status; raises IllegalStatusTransitionError if not; otherwise sets
  _status. Every command routes through this.

  5. Commands (CQS — change state, return nothing):
  - issue() — guard I-2 first: if not available, raise CopyNotAvailableError;
    else _move_to(LOANED).
  - return_copy() → _move_to(AVAILABLE)
  - mark_damaged() → _move_to(DAMAGED)
  - mark_lost() → _move_to(LOST)

  6. Queries (CQS — return facts, no side effects):
  - status (property) → the CopyStatus
  - is_available → status is AVAILABLE
  - is_visible_to_member → status in (AVAILABLE, LOANED) — this implements rule 
    #6 (lost/damaged hidden from members).

  Imports you'll need

  from domain.ids import CopyId, BookId
  from domain.enums import CopyStatus
  from domain.exceptions import CopyNotAvailableError,
  IllegalStatusTransitionError

  ⚠️ Prerequisite — do this first

  enums/enums.py is still empty, and enums/__init__.py too. BookCopy needs
  CopyStatus, so before the entity you'll need to:
  1. Define CopyStatus (an Enum with AVAILABLE / LOANED / LOST / DAMAGED) in
     enums/enums.py.
  2. Re-export it from enums/__init__.py (from domain.enums.enums import 
     CopyStatus) — same pattern as ids/value_objects, so from domain.enums 
     import CopyStatus works.

  Self-check when you're done

  - issue() on an already-loaned copy → CopyNotAvailableError
  - mark_lost() then return_copy() → IllegalStatusTransitionError (LOST is
    terminal)
  - is_visible_to_member is False for LOST/DAMAGED, True for AVAILABLE/LOANED
  - two copies with the same id are equal

  Go ahead and write enums/enums.py + enums/__init__.py + entities/bookcopy.py.
  When you're done, paste it or say the word and I'll review it against the
  contract.

✻ Cogitated for 27s

❯ DDD principle can be applied on new feature aswell ?                          

⏺ Yes — and honestly, incrementally on new features is where DDD shows its value
  most, more than big-bang greenfield design. The whole tactical toolkit is
  meant to be applied one feature at a time. Let me show how.

  DDD is iterative by nature

  Nothing about DDD requires modeling the whole system up front. You've actually
  seen this already in our build: step 5 revised step 4's decision ("Member
  owns ≤2 loans" → "Loan is its own aggregate"). That refinement-as-you-learn is
  the DDD method. A new feature is just the same loop, scoped smaller.

  The mini-loop for a new feature

  When a feature lands ("members can reserve a book that's currently all loaned
  out"), you run the same steps 1→8, but only over what the feature touches:

  1. Language — is there a new term? Reservation, "hold", "queue position". Add
     it to the ubiquitous language. Same word in code, conversation, tickets.
  2. Entity or VO? — Reservation has a lifecycle (waiting → fulfilled → expired)
     and is referred to individually → Entity. QueuePosition might be a VO.
  3. Invariants — "a member can't reserve a copy that's available to them," "one
     active reservation per member per title." Pin each to its owner.
  4. Aggregate — does it fit an existing aggregate, or is it new? Reservation
     has an independent lifecycle → its own aggregate, referencing
     MemberId/BookId by ID. (Exactly the reasoning from step 5.)
  5. Responsibilities — commands (fulfill(), expire()), queries, and the "must
     not."
  6. Service? — does a rule span aggregates ("notify next in queue when a copy
     is returned")? → a domain service / domain event.
  7. Code it — VO → entity → repository interface → application service → API.
     Same layering.

  The feature slots into the existing layers without disturbing them — that's 
  the payoff of the architecture.

  Why the existing design makes new features cheap

  This is the real answer to your question. Because we did DDD properly, a new
  feature:

  - Drops into clean seams. A new Reservation aggregate + its repository + an
    application service — the layering tells you exactly where each piece goes.
    No hunting.
  - Doesn't break what exists. Reservation references Book/Member by ID, so it
    couples loosely — adding it doesn't force changes to Loan or Member.
  - Reuses the vocabulary and VOs. MemberId, Money, the exception hierarchy —
    already there.
  - Extends via patterns, not edits. New fine rule? A new
    FineCalculationStrategy subclass — zero edits to existing entities (that's
    the Open/Closed payoff we built the Strategy for). New notification channel?
    A new adapter behind the port.

  ▎ A well-modeled domain is open for extension, closed for modification — so 
  ▎ new features are mostly additions, not surgery on existing code.

  The discipline that keeps it healthy

  Two cautions, both DDD principles:

  - Apply patterns only when the feature earns them (KISS/YAGNI). A trivial new
    field on Book doesn't need a new aggregate or strategy — just add the field.
    Don't ceremony-up a simple feature.
  - Watch for new bounded-context pressure. If a feature speaks a genuinely
    different language (e.g. a full "Acquisitions/purchasing" workflow with
    vendors, budgets), that's a signal it might be a new subdomain/context, not
    just another aggregate in the Library context. Recognizing that is the
    strategic side of DDD applied to growth — and ties straight back to the
    "subdomains are future microservice seams" note we added to step 1.

  The one-liner

  ▎ DDD isn't a one-time upfront ceremony — it's a repeatable per-feature loop 
  ▎ (language → classify → invariants → aggregate → responsibilities → code). 
  ▎ Doing it well the first time is precisely what makes each new feature land 
  ▎ as a clean addition in an obvious place, rather than a risky edit to a 
  ▎ tangle.

  So: absolutely apply it to new features — and you'll feel the dividend of the
  upfront work every time you do.

  Want this captured as a doc note (it'd fit step 28, "life of a change," or as
  a general principle), or carry on with BookCopy?

✻ Cogitated for 30s

❯ where should i create state pattern for book copy, how in industry people     
  does this ?                                                                   

⏺ Important re-anchor first: BookCopy should not use the State pattern — and
  that's the industry-correct call, not a shortcut. Let me explain why, then
  answer where State does go (for Loan) and how industry actually handles this.

  BookCopy → transition table, not State pattern

  The State pattern earns its place only when behavior diverges per state across
  operations. BookCopy's operations (issue/return/damage/lost) are all just "is
  this transition legal?" — there's no per-state behavior difference. So the
  right tool is the transition table (the _LEGAL_MOVES dict). Reaching for the
  full State pattern here would be over-engineering — exactly what KISS/YAGNI
  warns against.

  Loan is the one that might warrant State, because return_copy() genuinely
  behaves differently by state (ACTIVE → compute lateness; RETURNED → raise).
  Even there, step 8b argues two states isn't enough to justify it.

  Where State-pattern code lives (when you do use it)

  In our package structure, the state classes are pure domain, so they sit in
  the domain layer next to the entity they belong to:

  src/domain/entities/
  ├── loan.py          # the entity — delegates to its current state
  └── loan_state.py    # LoanState (ABC) + ActiveState, ReturnedState,
  OverdueState

  If states multiply across several entities, people promote it to a subpackage:

  src/domain/entities/loan/
  ├── __init__.py
  ├── loan.py
  └── states.py        # or states/active.py, states/returned.py …

  The key architectural rule: state objects belong in domain/, never 
  infrastructure. They encode business behavior. Persisting "which state a loan
  is in" is separate — that's just storing the status enum in a column, and
  infrastructure maps LoanStatus.ACTIVE ↔ "active". So: behavior in the domain, 
  the stored state-name in the DB.

  How industry actually handles entity state machines

  There's a spectrum — teams pick by complexity, exactly the judgment step 8b
  teaches:

  ┌────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │       Complexity       │         Approach         │        When         │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Few states, no         │ enum + transition table  │ the common case —   │
  │ divergent behavior     │ (our BookCopy)           │ most entities       │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Few states, divergent  │ GoF State pattern (one   │ behavior differs    │
  │ behavior               │ class per state)         │ per state across    │
  │                        │                          │ multiple methods    │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Many                   │                          │ the table/pattern   │
  │ states/transitions,    │ a state-machine library  │ gets unwieldy       │
  │ guards, hooks          │                          │                     │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Long-running,          │                          │ sagas, human        │
  │ cross-service          │ a workflow/orchestration │ approvals, retries, │
  │ workflows              │  engine                  │  days-long          │
  │                        │                          │ processes           │
  └────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  The libraries people reach for when hand-rolling stops scaling:
  - Python: transitions, python-statemachine — declarative states + transitions,
    guard callbacks, on-enter/on-exit hooks, even diagram export.
  - Java: Spring StateMachine.
  - JS/TS: XState (statecharts).
  - Distributed/long-running: Temporal, Camunda, AWS Step Functions.

  What experienced teams actually do most of the time: start with the enum + 
  transition-table guard (our BookCopy approach) because it's ~10 lines, fully
  testable, and zero dependencies. They escalate to the State pattern only when
  if/elif status == chains start repeating across methods, and to a library only
  when there are many states with guards/side-effects worth declaring. Reaching
  for a state-machine library on a 4-state entity is a classic over-engineering
  smell — the same lesson the docs hammer.

  ▎ The honest industry summary: most entity "state machines" in real codebases 
  ▎ are just a status enum + a guarded transition table. The GoF State pattern 
  ▎ is the next step up (divergent per-state behavior), and dedicated 
  ▎ libraries/engines are for genuinely complex or long-running workflows. Match
  ▎ the tool to the complexity — don't default to the heaviest one.

  So for you: BookCopy → transition table (you're already doing it right). If
  you want to practice the State pattern, do it on Loan (in
  domain/entities/loan_state.py) where the divergent-behavior justification at
  least exists — and step 8b walks that implementation. Want me to spec the Loan
  State-pattern version for you to write next?

✻ Sautéed for 32s

❯ entity class for Fine.                                                        

⏺ Next: Fine — the debt's lifecycle. A supporting-subdomain entity, so it stays
  simple. The one big lesson it carries: Fine is given its amount — it never 
  computes the ₹ itself (that's the Strategy's job, the fact-vs-policy split
  from steps 4 & 7). I'll spec it; you write it.

  The contract (from step 7)

  Fine : pay · waive                              (commands)
         is_paid · amount                         (queries)
         guards: I-5 amount never negative (via Money) · settle-once (can't pay
  an already-settled fine)
         Must NOT: calculate its own amount · know Loan internals

  What to build, piece by piece

  1. Fields
  - id: FineId — its own identity.
  - member_id: MemberId, loan_id: LoanId — by-ID references (step 6) to the
    member who owes it and the loan that caused it. Never hold the Member/Loan
    objects.
  - _amount: Money — passed in, already a valid Money (so I-5 "never negative"
    is already guaranteed by the VO — Fine doesn't re-check it). Private.
  - _status: FineStatus — private, defaulting to UNPAID.

  2. Equality by id — same __eq__/__hash__ shape as Book/BookCopy.

  3. Commands (CQS — change state, return nothing):
  - pay() — guard settle-once first, then move to PAID.
  - waive() — guard settle-once first, then move to WAIVED.
  - A private _require_unpaid() helper — if _status isn't UNPAID, raise
    FineAlreadySettledError. Both commands call it. (This is the "you can't
    settle a fine twice" invariant — paying an already-paid/waived fine is
    illegal.)

  4. Queries (CQS — facts, no side effects):
  - amount (property) → the Money
  - is_paid (property) → status is PAID

  Imports you'll need

  from domain.ids import FineId, MemberId, LoanId
  from domain.enums import FineStatus
  from domain.value_objects import Money
  from domain.exceptions import FineAlreadySettledError

  ⚠️ Prerequisite — same as BookCopy

  enums needs FineStatus (UNPAID / PAID / WAIVED). If you haven't added it yet:
  1. Define FineStatus in enums/enums.py.
  2. Re-export from enums/__init__.py so from domain.enums import FineStatus
     works.

  The design point to internalize

  The constructor takes amount: Money — it's handed the amount, never computes
  it:
  def __init__(self, fine_id, member_id, loan_id, amount: Money,
  status=FineStatus.UNPAID):
  The FineCalculationStrategy turns "days overdue" into that Money; Fine just
  holds it. That's the step-7 "must NOT calculate its own amount" — keep any × 
  ₹5 logic out of this class entirely.

  Self-check when you're done

  - pay() then pay() again → FineAlreadySettledError
  - pay() then waive() → FineAlreadySettledError (already settled)
  - is_paid is True only after pay(), False after waive() or while UNPAID
  - a fresh Fine is UNPAID; two fines with the same id are equal

  Go write enums (if needed) + entities/fine.py. Paste it when done and I'll
  review against the contract — or say the word.

✻ Cogitated for 44s

❯ In DateRange VO. end shouldnt be policy driven ?                              

⏺ Sharp catch — and you're half right, which is the interesting part. Let me
  split DateRange into its two responsibilities, because they fall on opposite
  sides of the invariant-vs-policy line.

  Two different things live in DateRange

  1. The structural invariant: end >= start. This is not policy — it's always
  true, for any date range, regardless of any business rule. A range that ends
  before it starts is incoherent, full stop. So this correctly belongs baked
  into the VO's __post_init__. No business decision could ever make "due date
  before borrow date" valid. ✅

  2. The loan-period length (the "5 days"). This is a policy — exactly like the
  ₹5/day fine rate. It can change (7 days next year, 14 for premium members,
  etc.). And here's the key point:

  ▎ DateRange does NOT hardcode the 5. Look at the factory: 
  ▎ for_loan(borrow_date, loan_days) — it takes loan_days as a parameter. The VO
  ▎ never knows the number is 5. It just stores whatever end it's handed.

  So the VO is already policy-agnostic. Your instinct ("the due date is
  policy-driven") is correct — but DateRange isn't where the policy lives, and
  it shouldn't be. It's a pure value: "a window from X to Y," plus the
  always-true rule that Y can't precede X.

  So where's the actual smell? The default =5

  The one place the policy leaks as a magic constant is the default argument:

  # DateRange.for_loan(borrow_date, loan_days)        ← param, good
  # Loan.create(..., loan_days: int = 5)              ← the "5" hardcoded here 
  ⚠️

  That = 5 is a policy sitting as a literal default. The step-8a doc even flags
  this: "the 5 will be supplied by a loan policy in 8b, not hard-coded here."
  The clean treatment mirrors the fine Strategy:

  - A LoanPolicy (or just a config value) owns the loan-period length, injected
    by the application service — not a literal buried in create().
  - Then Loan.create(..., period=loan_policy.period_for(member)) or similar.
    Change the policy → no entity edits (Open/Closed again).

  The deeper design question you're really raising

  Should for_loan even be on DateRange? Two valid views:

  ┌───────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     View      │                        Argument                         │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Keep it       │ It's just a param-driven convenience constructor; the   │
  │ (pragmatic)   │ policy value still comes from outside. Harmless.        │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ A value object should be a dumb value — (start, end) —  │
  │ Move it out   │ and know nothing about "loans" or "+N days." The        │
  │ (purist)      │ policy-aware factory belongs on Loan / a LoanPolicy,    │
  │               │ keeping DateRange a generic, reusable range.            │
  └───────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The purist view is cleaner and I lean toward it: DateRange is a generic VO (it
  could describe any window — a membership period, a reservation hold); the
  meaning "due = borrow + loan period" is lending policy, which belongs in the
  Lending subdomain (the Loan factory or a LoanPolicy), not in a generic date
  VO. Naming it for_loan drags lending vocabulary into a generic value — a mild
  smell.

  The bottom line

  ▎ The invariant (end >= start) is correctly in DateRange — it's always true, 
  ▎ not policy. The loan-period length is policy and is correctly not baked into
  ▎ the VO (it's a parameter). The only thing to clean up is the hardcoded 
  ▎ loan_days = 5 default — push it into an injected loan policy/config, the 
  ▎ same way ₹5/day lives in the FineCalculationStrategy. And arguably, move the
  ▎ for_loan factory off the generic DateRange and onto Loan/LoanPolicy, so the
  ▎ VO stays a pure, reusable value.

  So: your radar is right that due-date computation is policy — just aim the fix
  at the =5 default and the factory's location, not at DateRange's
  end/invariant, which are doing the right thing.

  Want me to capture this as a note in the step-8a doc (next to the DateRange
  section, since it explicitly defers the "5" to a policy)?

❯ why didnt we consider 5 subdomain here catalog/ lending/ fines/ membership/ ? 

⏺ Genuinely good catch — and it points at a real divergence between the scaffold
  you built and what CLAUDE.md / step 1 actually called for. Let me lay it out
  honestly.

  There are two ways to organize the code — two different axes

  By technical type (what the current scaffold does):
  domain/
  ├── entities/        # Book, BookCopy, Member, Loan, Fine   ← all subdomains
  mixed
  ├── value_objects/   # Money, ISBN, DateRange               ← all mixed
  ├── enums/  ids/  exceptions/

  By subdomain (what CLAUDE.md / step 1 specified):
  domain/
  ├── catalog/     # Book, BookCopy, ISBN, CopyStatus
  ├── lending/     # Loan, DateRange, LoanStatus, BorrowingService
  ├── fines/       # Fine, Money, FineStatus, FineCalculationStrategy
  ├── membership/  # Member
  └── shared/      # EntityId base, DomainError base

  Your project's own source of truth says the second one:

  ▎ CLAUDE.md: "Internally organized to mirror four subdomains."
  ▎ Step 1: "organized internally into modules that mirror the subdomains — 
  ▎ catalog/, lending/, fines/, membership/."

  So the honest answer to "why didn't we?" is: we drifted. The step-8a doc
  showed a flat by-type layout to teach the tactical building blocks in 
  dependency order, and the scaffold followed that — but it quietly dropped the
  subdomain organization the strategic design intended.

  Why by-subdomain is the better default for DDD

  1. High cohesion. Everything about Lending — Loan, BorrowingService,
     LoanStatus, the loan policy — sits in one folder. Change the lending rules
     → you touch one place. By-type scatters a single subdomain across
     entities/, value_objects/, enums/, services/.
  2. "Screaming architecture." Open domain/ and you see catalog / lending / 
     fines / membership — the structure screams the business, not the framework.
     By-type, you see entities / value_objects — which tells you nothing about
     what the system does.
  3. The microservice seam is literally a folder. Remember the step-1 note
     (subdomains = future service seams)? With by-subdomain, extracting Lending
     into its own service is "move the lending/ folder." With by-type, the
     subdomain is smeared across five folders — the seam is invisible.
  4. It matches how you reason. You think "the lending rules," not "all the
     entities."

  Why by-type isn't wrong, just weaker here

  - At tiny scale (5 entities) it's simple and everyone knows where an "entity"
    goes.
  - It's the common tutorial layout, which is why the 8a doc used it.
  - But it optimizes for "group by what a class is" over "group by what it's
    about" — and DDD cares about the latter.

  What I'd recommend

  Since this is a learning build that explicitly set out to practice strategic
  DDD, organizing by subdomain is the more faithful choice — it makes the
  bounded-context/subdomain structure tangible instead of theoretical. A common
  hybrid even keeps the tactical type one level down:

  domain/
  ├── shared/        # EntityId, DomainError, cross-cutting VOs
  ├── catalog/
  │   ├── book.py · book_copy.py
  │   ├── isbn.py
  │   └── enums.py   (CopyStatus)
  ├── lending/
  │   ├── loan.py · loan_state.py
  │   ├── date_range.py · borrowing_service.py
  │   └── enums.py   (LoanStatus)
  ├── fines/
  │   ├── fine.py · money.py
  │   ├── fine_calculation.py
  │   └── enums.py   (FineStatus)
  └── membership/
      └── member.py

  ▎ The trade-off in one line: package-by-type groups by "what a class is" 
  ▎ (simple, framework-shaped); package-by-subdomain groups by "what it's about"
  ▎ (cohesive, domain-shaped, extraction-ready) — and for a DDD exercise, the 
  ▎ second is the point.

  Two honest caveats if you switch:
  - Shared kernel: EntityId and DomainError are used by every subdomain → they
    go in a shared/ module everyone imports.
  - A few VOs are debatable. Money — does it belong to fines/ or shared/? It's
    only used by fines today, so fines/ is fine; promote to shared/ if another
    subdomain needs it. (Don't pre-share — YAGNI.)

  Want me to reorganize the existing code into subdomain modules (I'd move the
  files and fix imports, then re-run the checks), keep the current by-type
  layout, or pause and think about it? Given CLAUDE.md explicitly asks for
  subdomain organization, I lean toward reorganizing — but it's your call since
  you're driving the structure.

❯ Entity has no logic of concurrency, that i understood why.                    
  If two user come simultaneously for same bookcopy. both sees AVAILBLE.        
  Two choices:-                                                                 
  A. Why not to put a lock in application service ?                             
  B. Or, should we make it available to all users and let DB handle the         
  concurrency ?                                                                 
                                                                                
  I would choose option B. because DB is the source of truth Option A will fail 
  when we decide to scale with multiple processes.                              

⏺ Your reasoning is correct, and your justification is exactly the right one — A
  fails across processes because the lock lives in unshared memory; the
  contended resource is the DB row, so the guard must live where the sharing
  happens. You've internalized the whole concurrency thread. Let me confirm,
  then push it one level deeper because "let the DB handle it" still hides a
  choice.

  Why B is right (and A's real failure mode)

  You nailed it: a threading.Lock in the application service protects only one 
  process's threads. Spin up worker-2 and the two processes share no lock object
  → both still read AVAILABLE → double-issue. The lock sat in the wrong layer.
  Since we've committed to multiple workers, A is out. ✅

  Two refinements so the model is airtight:

  - A isn't "wrong," it's scoped wrong. For a genuinely single-process
    deployment, an in-process lock is a valid, simple answer. It's disqualified
    here specifically because we chose multiple workers. (Know the rule and its
    reason — same as everywhere.)
  - B isn't passive. "Let the DB handle it" doesn't mean the application service
    does nothing. The DB enforces; the service still coordinates — it opens the
    transaction and reacts to a conflict (retry, or return "please try again").
    And the entity's copy.issue() check still runs — it just isn't what wins 
    the race (recall: the service-side check does 0% of race prevention; it's
    there for the normal, uncontended "already loaned yesterday" case).

  The choice hiding inside B: how does the DB enforce?

  "DB handles it" splits into two mechanisms — this is the next decision for
  UC-1:

  ┌─────────┬───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │         │  Optimistic (version column)  │  Pessimistic (SELECT … FOR    │
  │         │                               │            UPDATE)            │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │         │ read with version; save with  │ lock the row on read; the     │
  │ How     │ UPDATE … WHERE id=? AND       │ second borrow waits until the │
  │         │ version=?; loser gets 0 rows  │  first commits                │
  │         │ → conflict                    │                               │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Best    │ conflicts are rare            │ conflicts are frequent        │
  │ when    │                               │                               │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Cost    │ a retry on the rare clash     │ holding a lock; risk of       │
  │         │                               │ waiting/deadlocks             │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Fits    │ ✅ — two people grabbing the  │                               │
  │ our     │ same copy at the same instant │ overkill here                 │
  │ case?   │  is uncommon                  │                               │
  └─────────┴───────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  For UC-1, optimistic is the natural fit: collisions on one specific copy are
  rare, so paying a cheap retry on the rare clash beats locking every borrow.
  The BookCopy carries a version; the repository does the compare-and-set; the
  application service catches ConcurrencyError and retries or fails.

  What UC-1 then looks like (concretely)

  borrow(copy_id):
    copy = copies.get(copy_id)            # reads row + version (say v7)
    copy.issue()                          # entity: AVAILABLE→LOANED (business
  check)
    loan = Loan.create(member_id, copy.id, today)
    try:
        copies.save(copy)                 # UPDATE ... WHERE version=7   ← the
  REAL guard
        loans.save(loan)
    except ConcurrencyError:
        retry / "copy just got taken, try another"

  Both racers run copy.issue() and pass — but only one UPDATE … WHERE version=7
  succeeds; the other hits 0 rows and is rejected. The DB, the shared source of
  truth, breaks the tie. Exactly your option B.

  ▎ So your choice stands, refined: enforce at the shared resource (the DB), 
  ▎ because it's the source of truth and survives multiple processes — and 
  ▎ specifically use optimistic version-checking for UC-1, with the application 
  ▎ service coordinating the retry. The entity stays pure; the DB wins the race.

  One thing to flag for later: optimistic locking only protects what's in the 
  row you're updating. Check (b) "< 2 active loans" spans multiple loan rows —
  so it can race differently (two borrows by the same member for different
  copies). Want to brainstorm that one next, or settle the copy-selection
  question (#1) first?

✻ Churned for 7s

❯ next. so when we actually code for any Entity, should we first decide what    
  attributes these entity should have and then behaviours it posses ?           

⏺ Good methodology question — and the DDD answer is the opposite of what most
  people's instinct says:

  ▎ Behavior first, attributes second. Decide what the entity does (and what it 
  ▎ must protect), and let the attributes fall out of what that behavior needs. 
  ▎ Starting from attributes is the road to an anemic model.

  Why not attributes-first

  If you start by listing fields — title, author, isbn, status, … — you
  naturally then add getters/setters to expose them, and the logic about those
  fields drifts off into services. You end up with a data bag, which is exactly
  the anemic anti-pattern step 7 warned against. Attributes-first optimizes for
  "what data do I store," when the real question is "what does this thing do and
  guarantee."

  You also end up inventing speculative fields nobody needs ("might as well add
  created_at, notes, …") — fields with no behavior behind them. YAGNI violation.

  The order I'd actually follow

  1. Identity — it's an entity, so: what's its ID? (BookId, FineId). One line,
     settled immediately.
  2. Behavior / the contract — this is the real design work, and it's exactly
     the step-7 contract:
     - Commands it accepts (issue(), pay())
     - Invariants it must guard (settle-once, only-available-can-issue)
     - Queries it answers (is_available, is_paid)
     - Must NOT (what belongs elsewhere)
  3. Attributes — derived from steps 1–2. Add a field only because a behavior or
     invariant needs it. "pay() must reject an already-settled fine" →
     therefore I need a _status. "Fine owes an amount" → _amount. Each attribute
     earns its place by being required by some behavior.
  4. Implement — constructor (enforce construction-time invariants), commands
     (guarded, private state), queries (CQS).

  The litmus test for each attribute: "Which behavior or invariant needs this?"
  If the answer is "none, it just seemed like data we'd have" → don't add it
  yet.

  We actually did this already

  Look back at Fine. We didn't start with "a fine has an amount, a status, a
  member, a loan." We started with the contract — pay / waive, settle-once, 
  is-given-its-amount — and the attributes followed:
  - "settle-once" invariant → needs _status
  - "is given its amount" → needs _amount (and tells us it's a constructor
    param, not computed)
  - by-ID references → member_id, loan_id

  Behavior drove every field. That's why Fine has no stray attributes.

  The honest nuance

  In practice it's a quick dialogue, not a rigid one-then-the-other. You'll jot
  the few obvious attributes (identity + the one or two core values) and the
  behavior almost together. But the guiding question stays behavior: "what must 
  this entity do and protect?" — and attributes are answers to that, not the
  starting point.

  ▎ One-liner: model the verbs first (commands, invariants, queries), and let 
  ▎ the nouns (attributes) be whatever those verbs require. Data in service of 
  ▎ behavior — that's what keeps the model rich instead of anemic.

  So when you write BookCopy or Member next: open the step-7 contract, write the
  behavior, and add a field only when a behavior forces it. Want to do BookCopy
  that way now — behavior-first, you driving?

✻ Crunched for 31s

❯ so while designing entity class first thing is consider behaviour and         
  invariants. wherever attributes are required add them to entity class. The    
  variable if has own lifecycle then candidate for VO.                          

⏺ Points 1 and 2 are spot-on. Point 3 is inverted — and it's an important fix,
  because "lifecycle" is the exact word that signals the opposite of a VO.

  Points 1 & 2 — correct ✅

  1. Behavior + invariants first. Yes.
  2. Add an attribute only when a behavior/invariant requires it. Yes.

  Point 3 — flip it

  You said: "if the variable has its own lifecycle → candidate for VO." But
  recall steps 3 & 5:

  ▎ A Value Object has NO identity and NO lifecycle of its own. Having a 
  ▎ lifecycle is precisely what makes something an Entity, not a VO.

  So "has its own lifecycle" points toward Entity, not VO. You've swapped the
  criteria.

  The correct three-way decision for each attribute

  When deciding what type an attribute should be, ask in this order:

  ┌─────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │     The attribute…      │        → It's a…        │       Example       │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ has its own identity +  │ separate Entity /       │ Loan referencing    │
  │ lifecycle, referenced   │ aggregate → hold it by  │ member_id (Member   │
  │ independently           │ ID, not as a            │ is its own entity)  │
  │                         │ field-object            │                     │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ has rules/meaning but   │                         │ Money, ISBN,        │
  │ no identity, immutable, │ Value Object (a field)  │ DateRange           │
  │  equal-by-value         │                         │                     │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ is a dumb scalar with   │ primitive               │ title: str,         │
  │ no rules                │                         │ page_count: int     │
  └─────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  So the VO test is: "defined entirely by its value, no identity, never changes 
  (you replace it)" — not "has a lifecycle." Lifecycle = entity.

  Your summary, corrected

  ▎ While designing an entity: (1) start from behavior + invariants; (2) add an 
  ▎ attribute only when a behavior/invariant needs it; (3) for each attribute 
  ▎ decide its type — if it has its own identity/lifecycle, it's a separate 
  ▎ entity, referenced by ID (not embedded); if it has rules but no identity 
  ▎ (immutable, value-equal), it's a VO; if it's a plain scalar with no rules, 
  ▎ leave it a primitive.

  Sanity-check against Fine

  - _amount → has rules (never negative) but no identity → VO (Money). ✓
  - _status → a fixed set of values, no identity → VO (enum, FineStatus). ✓
  - member_id → the Member has its own identity + lifecycle → separate entity, 
    held by ID. ✓ (Not embedded as a Member object, not a VO.)
  - id → the Fine's own identity → it's the entity's identifier (a typed-id VO).

  See how the member_id case is exactly your point 3, corrected: because Member
  has its own lifecycle, it's not a VO inside Fine — it's a separate entity
  referenced by ID.

  So: 1 and 2 nailed; for 3, remember lifecycle → entity (by ID); 
  value-without-identity → VO; bare scalar → primitive. Want to apply this live
  on BookCopy or Member next?

  It captures the corrected methodology:
  - Behavior first, attributes second — with the anemic-model warning and the
    4-step order (identity → contract → attributes → implement), including the
    per-field litmus test.
  - The three-way attribute-type decision table — lifecycle → separate entity
    (by ID); rules-but-no-identity → VO; bare scalar → primitive.
  - The ⚠️ "common inversion to avoid" — calling out explicitly that "has a
    lifecycle → VO" is backwards (lifecycle = entity), since that was the exact
