✻ Sautéed for 36s

❯ if aggregates have separate lifecycle in its own, then why are we doing       
  aggregate relationship in step06 ?                                            

⏺ Excellent question — it sounds like a contradiction ("independent" vs
  "related"), but it isn't. The resolution is that independence and relationship
  describe two different things:

  ▎ Independent lifecycle = they're created, changed, deleted, and stored 
  ▎ separately (no shared transaction, no cascade, no containment).
  ▎ Relationship = they still need to know about / refer to each other to do 
  ▎ business.

  Those coexist. A Loan is, by definition, a connection between a member and a
  copy — you literally cannot model lending without that link. Independent
  lifecycle doesn't mean "no connection"; it means "the connection is a
  reference, not a merger."

  The key: step 6 models associations, not composition

  Recall step 6's two relationship worlds:
  - Composition (inside an aggregate) = shared lifecycle, the part dies with the
    whole. We do not have this across aggregates.
  - Association (across aggregates) = a reference by ID, independent lifecycles.
    This is exactly what step 6 maps.

  So step 6 isn't binding lifecycles together — it's the opposite. It documents
  the loose, by-ID connections between things that stay separate: how many
  (cardinality), which way they point (direction), and that they're held by ID.
  The relationship modeling is precisely the discipline that lets aggregates be
  connected but not coupled.

  Why you need step 6 because they're independent

  Here's the twist: if the aggregates weren't independent — if Loan were just
  internal structure inside one giant aggregate — there'd be nothing to model in
  step 6. It'd be private composition, hidden inside the root. Relationship
  modeling becomes a thing only when you have separate aggregates that must
  reference each other across their boundaries. Step 6 exists because of the 
  independence, not in spite of it.

  The analogy

  Two people have independent lives (separate lifecycles — one can move, change
  jobs, even pass away without the other ceasing to exist). Yet they have a
  relationship (colleague, friend). The relationship is a connection, not a
  merger — it doesn't fuse them into one person, and modeling "they're
  colleagues" doesn't contradict "they're separate individuals."

  Loan → Member is the same: the Loan knows member_id (the relationship), but
  the Member lives its own life (independent lifecycle). Deleting/changing the
  Member isn't a cascade through the Loan, and vice versa — they're connected by
  a reference, not bound by a shared fate.

  Concretely in our model

  Loan (its own aggregate, own lifecycle)
    ├── member_id ──► Member   (association: by ID, independent lifecycles)   ←
  step 6 models this
    └── copy_id   ──► BookCopy (association: by ID, independent lifecycles)   ←
  and this

  Step 6 says: one Member has many Loans (cardinality), the reference lives on
  the Loan side pointing to Member (direction), and it's held as member_id (by
  ID). None of that makes their lifecycles shared — it makes them navigable
  while staying independent.

  ▎ One-liner: independent lifecycle is about consistency and storage 
  ▎ boundaries; a relationship is about how independent units refer to each 
  ▎ other. Step 6 models the by-ID associations between separate aggregates — 
  ▎ and that modeling is needed precisely because the aggregates are 
  ▎ independent. If they shared a lifecycle, they'd be one aggregate and there'd
  ▎ be no cross-boundary relationship to draw.

  So step 6 and "separate lifecycles" aren't in tension — step 6 is how you
  connect separate-lifecycle aggregates without sacrificing their separateness.

  Does that dissolve the contradiction? Happy to add it as a clarifying note to
  step 6 if it's worth pinning down.

✻ Cooked for 31s

❯ so, while creating Entity class i need to have member_id, copy_id as          
  attribute  aswell                                                             

⏺ Yes — exactly. The by-ID references are attributes of the entity. They're just
  held as IDs (the typed-id VOs), not as full objects.

  Confirming with Loan

  Loan's whole reason to exist is "a specific copy out to a specific member" —
  so the behavior requires it to know which member and which copy.
  Behavior-first gives you these fields:

  class Loan:
      def __init__(self, loan_id: LoanId, member_id: MemberId, copy_id: CopyId,
                   period: DateRange, ...):
          self.id = loan_id            # its OWN identity
          self.member_id = member_id   # by-ID ref → Member (separate aggregate)
          self.copy_id = copy_id       # by-ID ref → BookCopy (separate 
  aggregate)
          self.period = period         # VO

  So member_id and copy_id are real attributes — held as MemberId / CopyId
  (typed-id VOs), not as Member / BookCopy objects.

  Where this lands in the three-way decision

  This is exactly the first row of the §8a.7 table, in action:

  ┌──────────────┬─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │  Attribute   │      Type decision      │              Why              │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ member_id,   │ other aggregate has its │ a MemberId/CopyId, never an   │
  │ copy_id      │  own lifecycle → hold   │ embedded Member/BookCopy      │
  │              │ by ID                   │                               │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ period       │ rules, no identity → VO │ DateRange                     │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ id           │ the Loan's own identity │ LoanId                        │
  │              │  → typed id             │                               │
  └──────────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  The behavior-first justification

  You don't add member_id "because a loan has a member field." You add it
  because a behavior/invariant needs it:
  - "a loan ties one member to one copy" → must store both ids.
  - later, BorrowingService counts "this member's active loans" → needs
    member_id to query by.
  - returning a copy needs to find which copy → needs copy_id.

  Each id traces back to something the loan must do.

  The one rule to hold

  ▎ Cross-aggregate references are attributes — stored as IDs (MemberId, 
  ▎ CopyId), never as objects. That's how an entity stays connected to other 
  ▎ aggregates (step 6's association) while keeping them independent. The moment
  ▎ you'd write self.member = member (the whole object), you've merged two 
  ▎ aggregates by accident — hold self.member_id instead.

  So yes: Fine has member_id + loan_id, Loan has member_id + copy_id, BookCopy
  has book_id — all by-ID attributes. Only Member and Book have no outbound
  references (nothing they depend on), which is why the reference always lives
  on the "many"/dependent side (step 6's direction rule).

  Want to build Loan next — it's the one that pulls all of this together (by-ID
  refs + the DateRange VO + the state question)?

✻ Crunched for 20s

❯ isnt this job of domain service ?                                             

⏺ Good instinct to question it — but no, these are two different jobs, and
  conflating them is a common slip. Let me separate them cleanly.

  Holding member_id/copy_id = the entity's data. The decision = the domain 
  service's job.

  There are two distinct "connectings" happening, and they live in different
  places:

  ┌────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │       The thing        │      What it is       │       Whose job       │
  ├────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Loan records which     │ state / data — the    │                       │
  │ member + which copy    │ loan is that          │ the Loan entity (its  │
  │ (member_id, copy_id)   │ relationship,         │ attributes)           │
  │                        │ persisted             │                       │
  ├────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Deciding whether this  │                       │                       │
  │ member may borrow this │ a cross-aggregate     │ the domain service    │
  │  copy (active?         │ rule / decision       │ (BorrowingService)    │
  │ available? < 2 loans?) │                       │                       │
  └────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  So:
  - The entity holds the reference because a loan, by definition, is "this copy
    out to this member." That's just its data — and a domain service holds no
    state, so it can't be the home for member_id.
  - The domain service doesn't store ids and doesn't own the loan's fields. It's
    stateless logic that decides — "given this member, this copy, and this
    count, is the borrow allowed?"

  Why the domain service can't be where member_id lives

  Two reasons, straight from step 9:
  1. Domain services are stateless — no fields holding entities/ids between
     calls. If member_id lived "on the service," the service would have state.
     It doesn't.
  2. The id is the loan's data, and it persists. When you save a Loan and reload
     it next week, it must still know its member_id. That data belongs to the
     entity (and its row), not to a transient service call.

  The borrow flow shows all three roles

  borrow_book(member_id, copy_id):                       [application service —
  orchestrates]
    member = members.get(member_id)
    copy   = copies.get(copy_id)
    active = loans.count_active(member_id)

    borrowing.check_can_borrow(member, copy, active)     [domain SERVICE —
  DECIDES the rule]

    copy.issue()                                         [entity — its own
  invariant]
    loan = Loan.create(member_id, copy_id, today)        [entity FACTORY —
  records the ids as DATA]
    loans.save(loan)

  - The domain service decides (holds nothing).
  - The Loan entity records member_id/copy_id as its own state via
    Loan.create(...).
  - The application service passes the ids around and wires it together.

  The domain service uses member and copy to decide — but it doesn't own the
  resulting loan's references. Those are the loan's.

  The clean separation

  ▎ Data (which member, which copy) → the entity's attributes (member_id, 
  ▎ copy_id), because a loan is that link and the data persists.
  ▎ Decision (is this link allowed?) → the domain service, stateless, spanning 
  ▎ aggregates.
  ▎ Orchestration (load, call the decision, create the loan, save) → the 
  ▎ application service.

  So member_id/copy_id as fields on Loan is firmly the entity's job — the domain
  service is about the rule, not the reference. Different concerns: one is
  state the loan carries, the other is a decision made once at borrow time.

  Does that draw the line clearly?

✻ Sautéed for 27s

❯ wherever there is something that needed to be created, is that a hint for     
  factory design pattern ?                                                      

⏺ Careful — this is exactly the kind of reflexive-pattern thinking the project
  warns against. Creation is everywhere; Factories are not. The honest rule:

  ▎ Needing to create something is not by itself a hint for a Factory. A plain 
  ▎ constructor is the default. A Factory earns its place only when creation has
  ▎ to do something a plain constructor shouldn't or can't cleanly do.

  If "something needs to be created → Factory," you'd wrap every new in a
  factory — pure ceremony (a KISS/YAGNI violation, and the project's "patterns
  only when a real problem demands them" rule).

  When creation does hint at a factory

  Ask: does the creation need any of these? If yes → factory. If no → just call
  the constructor.

  ┌─────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │     Signal      │   Why a factory helps    │        Our example        │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Creation must   │ guarantee a valid object │ Loan.create() sets due =  │
  │ enforce an      │  at birth                │ borrow + 5 (I-3)          │
  │ invariant       │                          │                           │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ There's a       │                          │                           │
  │ meaningful name │ intention-revealing      │ Money.rupees(5),          │
  │  for a way to   │ "named constructor"      │ DateRange.for_loan(...)   │
  │ build it        │                          │                           │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ It generates    │                          │ EntityId.new() (mints a   │
  │ something (id,  │ hide the generation      │ uuid)                     │
  │ default)        │                          │                           │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Multiple        │ one named factory per    │ Money.rupees(...) vs      │
  │ distinct ways   │ way                      │ Money(paise)              │
  │ to construct    │                          │                           │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Complex         │                          │ building an aggregate     │
  │ assembly (build │ hide the wiring          │ from sub-parts            │
  │  from parts)    │                          │                           │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Must choose a   │ hide which class is      │                           │
  │ concrete        │ instantiated             │ picking a                 │
  │ subtype at      │ (polymorphic creation)   │ FineCalculationStrategy   │
  │ runtime         │                          │                           │
  └─────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  When it does NOT — just use the constructor

  If construction is a single, obvious, rule-free assignment of fields, a
  factory adds nothing. Book(book_id, isbn, title, author) — plain constructor.
  We didn't write Book.create() because there's no due-date-style rule to
  encode, no id to generate (the caller supplies BookId), no name to add. A
  factory there would be noise.

  Two different "factories" — don't conflate

  - Factory method (a @classmethod like Money.rupees, Loan.create, EntityId.new)
    — lightweight, the common one. This is what most "creation needs a rule"
    cases want.
  - Factory class / Abstract Factory (a separate object whose whole job is
    producing families of objects) — heavyweight. Reserve for genuine
    polymorphic creation. We essentially never need this at our scale.

  Most of the time the answer is the method, not the class.

  The sharper question to ask

  Instead of "does something get created?", ask:

  ▎ "Is there a rule, a name, generation, or a choice of type involved in 
  ▎ building this?"
  ▎ - Yes → a factory method (named classmethod) is the clean home for it.
  ▎ - No, it's just assigning fields → a plain constructor.

  We've already applied this correctly

  - Money.rupees, DateRange.for_loan, Loan.create, EntityId.new → factory
    methods, because each encodes a rule/name/generation. ✅
  - Book(...), Fine(...) → plain constructors, because creation is just
    rule-free field assignment. ✅

  ▎ One-liner: creation alone isn't the trigger — creation that carries a rule, 
  ▎ a meaningful name, generation, or a type-choice is. Otherwise the 
  ▎ constructor is correct, and a Factory is over-engineering.

  So Loan will get a create() (it enforces the due-date rule); BookCopy and
  Member probably won't need one (plain construction) — though BookCopy could
  take a book_id + default AVAILABLE status, which is borderline. Want to weigh
  that one when we build BookCopy?

✻ Crunched for 31s

❯ so wherever we need to create an object, first ask if invariant is associated 
  with creation then use factory as it will guarantee correct objcet creation   
  else simple constructor call would work.                                      

⏺ Yes — that's the right rule, and you've captured the most important trigger
  (invariant) precisely. Let me just confirm it and round it out so it's
  complete.

  Your rule — correct ✅

  ▎ Need to create an object → ask "is there an invariant tied to the creation?"
  ▎ - Yes → factory method — it guarantees a valid object at birth (can't 
  ▎ construct an invalid one).
  ▎ - No → plain constructor.

  That's exactly right, and invariant-at-creation is the primary reason — e.g.
  Loan.create() guaranteeing due = borrow + 5, or Money rejecting negatives.

  One small widening

  "Invariant" is the headline trigger, but it's really one case of a broader
  test: "does building this need to do more than just assign fields?" The other
  forms that also warrant a factory method:

  - A meaningful name for a way to build it — Money.rupees(5) (vs raw paise)
    reads better and documents intent.
  - Generation — EntityId.new() mints a uuid; the caller shouldn't.
  - A type choice — picking which concrete subtype to instantiate.

  All four collapse to the same idea: creation that carries logic → factory; 
  creation that's pure field-assignment → constructor. Invariant is just the
  most common and most important of them.

  So the refined one-liner

  ▎ When creating an object, ask: does construction carry any logic — an 
  ▎ invariant to enforce, a name worth giving, something to generate, or a type 
  ▎ to choose? If yes → factory method (guarantees correctness / clarity). If 
  ▎ it's just assigning fields → plain constructor.

  Your version is the 90% rule and it's solid — keep "invariant → factory" as
  your instinct, and just remember the three sibling cases are the same
  principle wearing different hats.

  Want to add this as a short note to the docs (step 8b, next to the Loan.create
  factory discussion), or carry on?

✻ Brewed for 14s

❯ any design pattern that we will build will stay in domain directory only ?    

⏺ No — patterns live in whatever layer their concern belongs to, not all in
  domain/. A design pattern is just a structuring technique; where it goes
  depends on what problem it solves. The deciding question is always: is this 
  pattern solving a business-rule concern, or an infrastructure/orchestration 
  concern?

  The rule

  ▎ A pattern's home = the layer of the concern it addresses. Business behavior 
  ▎ → domain/. Persistence / external systems / wiring → outer layers 
  ▎ (infrastructure/, application/, api/).

  Our patterns, mapped to their layers

  ┌─────────────────────────────┬───────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │           Pattern           │ Concern it solves │       Lives in        │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Strategy                    │ a business policy │ domain/ (services)    │
  │ (FineCalculationStrategy)   │  (₹/day)          │                       │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │                             │ the loan's        │                       │
  │ State (LoanState)           │ business behavior │ domain/ (entities)    │
  │                             │  per state        │                       │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Factory Method              │ enforce creation  │ domain/ (on the       │
  │ (Loan.create, Money.rupees) │ invariants        │ entity/VO)            │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Repository — the interface  │ what the domain   │                       │
  │ (port)                      │ needs to          │ domain/               │
  │                             │ load/save         │                       │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Repository — the            │ how it actually   │                       │
  │ implementation              │ persists          │ infrastructure/       │
  │                             │ (SQL/in-memory)   │                       │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Adapter / Ports (Clock,     │ talking to the    │ infrastructure/ (port │
  │ Notifier, LockManager       │ outside world     │  may sit in           │
  │ impls)                      │                   │ application/domain)   │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │                             │ event definition  │ event in domain/,     │
  │ Observer / Domain Events    │ vs event handlers │ handlers often        │
  │                             │                   │ application/          │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Dependency Injection /      │ wiring concretes  │ api/ / application/   │
  │ composition root            │ into interfaces   │ (the entry point)     │
  │                             │ at startup        │                       │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ DTO / Mapper                │ HTTP ↔ domain     │ api/                  │
  │                             │ translation       │                       │
  └─────────────────────────────┴───────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  The pattern that makes it clearest: Repository

  Repository spans two layers on purpose — and it's the perfect illustration
  that "patterns don't all live in domain":
  - The interface (LoanRepository) lives in domain/ — it expresses what the 
    domain needs, in domain language, depending on nothing.
  - The implementation (SqlLoanRepository) lives in infrastructure/ — the how,
    with the DB driver.

  Same pattern, split across layers by concern. That's Dependency Inversion in
  action.

  Why this matters

  Two patterns that look similar can land in different layers depending on what 
  they model:
  - Strategy for fine calculation → domain/, because the fine rate is a business
    policy.
  - A Strategy for, say, retry-backoff (how often to retry a failed DB write) →
    infrastructure/, because it's a technical concern with no business meaning.

  The pattern is the same; the concern decides the home.

  The litmus test

  For any pattern you're about to add, ask:

  ▎ "If I swapped the database / web framework / email provider, would this 
  ▎ pattern change?"
  ▎ - No — it's a business rule → domain/ (Strategy-for-fines, State-for-loan, 
  ▎ Factory, Repository interface).
  ▎ - Yes — it's a technical detail → infrastructure/ (Repository impl, 
  ▎ Adapters) or application//api/ (DI wiring, DTOs).

  ▎ One-liner: patterns go where their concern lives. The ones encoding business
  ▎ rules/behavior (Strategy-for-policy, State, Factory, Repository interface) 
  ▎ stay in domain/; the ones handling persistence, external systems, or wiring 
  ▎ live in infrastructure/, application/, or api/. Most of our visible patterns
  ▎ happen to be domain ones — but Repository deliberately straddles domain 
  ▎ (interface) and infrastructure (impl), which proves the rule.

  So: not "all patterns in domain" — patterns follow their concern, and the
  domain only keeps the ones that are genuinely about business rules.

⏺ Great instinct — and this reframes one of my judgment calls into a clear
  decision. Let me explain the principles first, because they change how we
  structure, then I'll build it.

  What actually makes a monolith "splittable" later

  Splitting into microservices is cheap only if the subdomains are already
  decoupled the right way inside the monolith. Four rules:

  1. Package by subdomain = each folder is a future service. catalog/, lending/,
     fines/, membership/, notifications/ — extracting one becomes "lift out the
     folder."
  2. THE golden rule: a subdomain never imports another subdomain's internals.
     No lending importing catalog.Book. If it did, you couldn't split them —
     they're welded at compile time. Subdomains communicate only by (a) IDs by 
     value, (b) published interfaces/contracts, (c) events later.
  3. Cross-subdomain references by ID only — Loan holds member_id, never a
     Member object. (Already our rule — now it's load-bearing for splitting.)
  4. Keep the shared kernel tiny and stable. A big shared kernel is the enemy of
     splitting — it's the thing that doesn't separate. Only genuinely
     universal, stable types belong there.

  The decision this forces (and it's the clean one)

  Putting the typed IDs in shared/ turns out to be the most splittable choice —
  because then the dependency graph is:

  catalog  ─┐
  lending  ─┤
  fines    ─┼──►  shared   (EntityId + typed IDs + DomainError base)
  membership┤
  notifications┘

  NO subdomain → subdomain edges.

  No subdomain imports another subdomain — they only share a tiny, stable 
  kernel. So every subdomain is independently extractable. At split time,
  shared/ becomes a thin published "contracts" library each service depends on
  (or each service just re-declares the ID as a string wrapper — it's only a
  UUID over the wire). Either way, the split is a lift-out.

  Each subdomain is also a full vertical slice — it will own its entities, VOs,
  its own repository interfaces (step 10) and domain services (step 9), not a
  central repositories/ folder. That's what makes a extracted service
  self-contained.

  microservice-split-ready.

  Final structure

  domain/
  ├── shared/        # EntityId + typed IDs, DomainError base  ← the only shared
  kernel
  ├── catalog/       # book, bookcopy, isbn, enums(CopyStatus), exceptions
  ├── lending/       # loan, daterange, enums(LoanStatus), exceptions
  ├── fines/         # fine, money, enums(FineStatus), exceptions
  ├── membership/    # member (WIP)
  └── notifications/ # placeholder (generic subdomain)

  What makes it splittable (verified, not just claimed)

  - ✅ All imports resolve; Book, Fine, DateRange behave correctly.
  - ✅ Zero subdomain→subdomain imports — the grep check printed nothing. Every
    subdomain depends only on domain.shared. The dependency graph is a clean
    star:

  catalog, lending, fines, membership, notifications  ──►  shared
  (no edges between subdomains)

  That's the whole game: to extract lending into its own service tomorrow, you
  lift the folder — it drags in nothing but the tiny shared kernel (which
  becomes a published contracts library, or the IDs get re-declared as string
  wrappers over the wire). Cross-subdomain references are already by ID only, so
  a local lookup becomes a remote call/event without touching the domain logic.

  ⎿  Added 40 lines, removed 10 lines
      37  
      38  **Why this is the payoff, not bureaucracy:** because the domain
           imports nothing, the bulk of your tests (the domain) are insta
          nt and mock-free; you can swap Postgres for SQLite or FastAPI f
          or a CLI by touching only the outer rings; and a reviewer alway
          s knows where a given concern lives. The layering *buys* testab
          ility, swappability, and navigability — it isn't ceremony for i
          ts own sake.
      39  
      40 -### This chapter's corner: the `domain` package                
      40 +### This chapter's corner: the `domain` package — organized *by
         + subdomain*                                                    
      41  
      42 -Everything in 8a lives in the `domain` layer — pure Python, **z
         -ero framework imports** (no FastAPI, no ORM). That purity is wh
         -at makes it instantly unit-testable.                           
      42 +Everything in 8a lives in the `domain` layer — pure Python, **z
         +ero framework imports**. But there's a second, deliberate organ
         +izing decision *inside* `domain/`: we package **by subdomain (b
         +ounded-context module)**, not by technical type.               
      43

      57  
      58 +> **Two ways to package — and why we chose this one.** You can 
         +group code *by technical type* (`entities/`, `value_objects/`, 
         +`enums/` — every subdomain's classes mixed together) or *by sub
         +domain* (`catalog/`, `lending/`, … — each a self-contained slic
         +e). By-type is the common tutorial default, but it **scatters o
         +ne subdomain across five folders** and the structure tells you 
         +nothing about what the system *does*. By-subdomain gives **high
         + cohesion** (everything about Lending in one place), **"screami
         +ng architecture"** (the folders name the business, not the fram
         +ework), and — the reason that matters most below — it makes the
         + bounded-context boundaries *tangible* instead of theoretical. 
         +This is exactly what Step 1 called for ("modules that mirror th
         +e subdomains").                                                
      59 +                                                               
      60 +#### Why this layout is microservice-friendly (the load-bearing
         + reason)                                                       
      61 +                                                               
      62 +We deliberately keep this a **modular monolith** at our scale (
         +Step 1 / the Step-5 aggregate-vs-microservice sidebar). But the
         + *point* of packaging by subdomain is that a future split into 
         +microservices becomes a **lift-out**, not a rewrite. That works
         + only if two rules hold:                                       
      63 +                                                               
      64 +1. **A subdomain never imports another subdomain's internals.**
         + No `lending` importing `catalog.Book`. If it did, they'd be we
         +lded at compile time and couldn't be separated. Subdomains talk
         + **only** by (a) **IDs by value**, (b) **published interfaces/c
         +ontracts**, (c) **domain events** (later).                     
      65 +2. **The shared kernel stays tiny and stable.** A big shared ke
         +rnel is the thing that *doesn't* split — so `shared/` holds onl
         +y the universal, rarely-changing types: the typed IDs and the b
         +ase exception.                                                 
      66 +                                                               
      67 +Putting the **typed IDs in `shared/`** is what turns rule #1 in
         +to a clean fact rather than a hope. The dependency graph become
         +s a star with **no subdomain → subdomain edges**:              
      68 +                                                               
      69 +```                                                            
      70 +catalog  ─┐                                                    
      71 +lending  ─┤                                                    
      72 +fines    ─┼──►  shared        (EntityId + typed IDs + DomainErr
         +or base)                                                       
      73 +membership┤                                                    
      74 +notifications┘                                                 
      75 +        NO edges between subdomains                            
      76 +```                                                            
      77 +                                                               
      78 +Because every subdomain depends *only* on `shared`, each one is
         + **independently extractable**. At split time:                 
      79 +- `shared/` becomes a thin published "contracts" library each s
         +ervice depends on (or each service re-declares the ID as a stri
         +ng wrapper — over the wire it's just a UUID);                  
      80 +- cross-subdomain references are **already by ID** (`Loan` hold
         +s `member_id`, never a `Member`), so a local lookup becomes a r
         +emote call/event **without changing the domain logic**;        
      81 +- the transaction that spans aggregates (`borrow` touches BookC
         +opy + Loan) is *within one subdomain* (Lending), so it stays a 
         +local transaction — the split doesn't force a distributed saga.
      82 +                                                               
      83 +> **The one-liner:** package by subdomain, let each subdomain d
         +epend only on a tiny `shared` kernel, and reference other subdo
         +mains **by ID only** — then "extract a microservice" is "move t
         +he folder," not "untangle the codebase." We stay a monolith tod
         +ay (KISS/YAGNI for ~100 members) while keeping the split a chea
         +p future option.                                               
      84 +                                                               
      85  ---

❯ what will happen to api, application infra in case we plan to split it into   
  microservices.                                                                

⏺ Great question — because the domain being split-ready is only one third of the
  story. Let me answer what happens to each outer layer, then the honest hard
  parts, then how to organize now so the split stays cheap.

  The mental model: a microservice is a full vertical slice

  In the monolith you have layers across all subdomains. When you split, each
  service becomes its own complete hexagon — its own api + application + domain 
  + infrastructure, for one subdomain:

  MONOLITH (layer-first)                 MICROSERVICES (subdomain-first)
  src/                                   lending-service/
  catalog-service/
  ├── api/            (all subdomains)     ├── api/                 ├── api/
  ├── application/    (all subdomains)     ├── application/         ├──
  application/
  ├── domain/         (by subdomain ✓)     ├── domain/  (lending)   ├── domain/
  (catalog)
  └── infrastructure/ (all subdomains)     └── infrastructure/      └──
  infrastructure/
                                           └── its OWN database     └── its OWN
  database

  So "split" = slice vertically through all four layers along subdomain lines,
  and each slice becomes a deployable.

  What happens to each layer

  Layer: domain/
  On split: Already sliced by subdomain → moves cleanly (the work we just did).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Layer: application/
  On split: Use-case orchestrators split per subdomain (borrow → lending
  service,
  search → catalog service). The hard part: a use case that spans subdomains no
   longer has one in-process transaction.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Layer: infrastructure/
  On split: Database-per-service — the shared DB splits; each service owns its
  tables. Repository implementations move with their subdomain. New  infra 
  appears that didn't exist in the monolith: HTTP clients / a message broker /
  an event bus for inter-service comms. Shared adapters (email, cache) get
  duplicated or become shared infra.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Layer: api/
  On split: Splits per service; each exposes only its own endpoints. An API 
  gateway often appears in front to route/aggregate. Shared middleware (auth,
  DomainError→400 mapping) is duplicated or pulled into a shared library.

  The genuinely hard parts (the real cost of splitting)

  These are what the monolith gives you for free and the split takes away:

  1. Cross-subdomain transactions. borrow touches BookCopy (catalog) + Loan
     (lending). In the monolith: one local DB transaction. Split across
     services: no shared transaction → you need a saga / eventual consistency
     with compensating actions. (This is exactly why the step-5 sidebar said 
     keep borrow's aggregates within one subdomain — Lending — so this doesn't 
     bite.)
  2. Cross-subdomain queries. A screen showing a member + their loans + fines
     was a JOIN. Split: no cross-service JOIN → you need API composition (call 3
     services and stitch) or a read model / materialized view.
  3. Data ownership. Database-per-service means no reaching into another
     service's tables — only its API/events. (The by-ID references make this
     natural: member_id was never a JOIN you depended on.)
  4. Distributed-system tax. Network failures, retries, timeouts, contract
     versioning, distributed tracing, deployment orchestration. None of it
     exists in the monolith.

  How to organize now so the split stays a lift-out

  The current tree is a hybrid — domain/ is by-subdomain, but
  api/application/infrastructure/ are still flat (empty for now). To keep the
  seam clean across all layers, two options:

  Option A — keep layer-first, add subdomain submodules inside each layer:
  application/catalog/ · application/lending/ · application/fines/ …
  infrastructure/catalog/ · infrastructure/lending/ …
  api/catalog/ · api/lending/ …
  Option B — go full vertical-slice (subdomain-first), the most split-ready:
  src/lending/{api,application,domain,infrastructure}/
  src/catalog/{api,application,domain,infrastructure}/

  Both make "extract a service" a folder move. Option B is the most faithful
  (each top-level folder is the future service) but is a bigger reshuffle;
  Option A is a lighter touch that matches your current layout. Since
  api/application/infrastructure/ are still empty, now is the cheapest time to 
  pick the convention — before we write step 9/10/11 code into them.

  The KISS caveat (important)

  Don't build any of the distributed machinery now — no message broker, no saga
  engine, no gateway. That'd be over-engineering for ~100 members. The job today
  is only to keep the seams clean:
  - subdomain-aligned modules across all layers (pick A or B),
  - cross-subdomain references by ID only,
  - no subdomain imports another subdomain's internals,
  - each subdomain's use-cases/repos/adapters live with that subdomain.

  Do that, and the day scale forces a split, it's "lift the folder + add the
  plumbing" — not "untangle and rewrite."

  ▎ One-liner: a microservice = one subdomain's full vertical slice 
  ▎ (api+application+domain+infra) + its own database. On split, application 
  ▎ gains sagas/events for cross-subdomain flows, infrastructure gains a 
  ▎ database-per-service and inter-service comms, api gains a gateway — but only
  ▎ where scale demands it. Keep the seams subdomain-aligned across every layer
  ▎ now (Option A or B), and the split stays a lift-out.

  Want me to decide the api/application/infrastructure convention now (I'd lean
  Option A for a lighter touch, or B for maximum readiness) so step 9–11 code
  lands in the right shape — or capture this as a doc note?

✻ Baked for 1m 1s

❯ i want to know basically, if at starting a architect decides to go for        
  monolith but want to leverage code in such a way that there is scope for      
  future microservices. how should an architect design monolith so there is     
  minimum changes.                                                              

⏺ The discipline has a name — the modular monolith — and the whole trick is one
  mindset:

  ▎ Design each module as if it's already a separate service — just deployed 
  ▎ in-process. Then "going to microservices" changes how modules talk and 
  ▎ deploy, not what the code is.

  Here's the architect's playbook, ordered by how much pain they save you.

  The principles (in priority order)

  1. Modularize by business capability, not by technical layer.
  Top-level structure = bounded contexts / subdomains (catalog, lending,
  billing), each a vertical slice (its own api → application → domain → infra).
  Not controllers/, services/, models/ folders that mix all features. Each
  module = one future service. This is the single most important call.

  2. Enforce hard module boundaries — no reaching into internals.
  Each module exposes a public interface/facade; everything else is private.
  Module A calls B's published contract, never B's entities/repos/tables. In a
  monolith this discipline erodes silently (nothing stops an import), so
  automate the check — import-linter (Python), ArchUnit (Java), Nx/module
  boundaries (TS). A boundary you don't enforce isn't a boundary.

  3. Each module owns its data — logical database-per-service from day one.
  Even in one physical DB: separate schema per module, and forbid cross-module 
  JOINs and foreign keys across boundaries. Reference other modules' data by ID,
  not by JOIN. This is the hardest thing to retrofit — a shared table with
  cross-module FKs is what makes a split take months. Pay it early; it's nearly
  free at the start.

  4. Talk between modules the way services would — even in-process.
  - Synchronous: call B's interface through an in-process implementation that
    could later become an HTTP client. The caller depends on the interface, so
    swapping in-process→network changes one adapter, not the caller.
  - Asynchronous: publish domain events to an in-process bus that could later
    become Kafka/RabbitMQ. Handlers don't care if the event came from memory or
    a broker.
    This is what makes the transport swap on split a config change, not a code 
    change.

  5. Keep the shared kernel tiny and stable.
  Only universal, rarely-changing types (IDs, base error, a couple of shared
  value objects). A fat shared kernel is the thing that won't separate.
  Everything domain-specific belongs to its module.

  6. Never rely on cross-module ACID transactions.
  Keep a transaction within one module. Where a flow spans modules, coordinate
  with events + eventual consistency (saga) — or at least design so you could,
  so a split doesn't force a rewrite. Within-module stays transactional (that's
  fine).

  7. Ports & adapters (hexagonal) for all infrastructure.
  DB, cache, message bus, external APIs — all behind interfaces the domain owns.
  On split, you replace an adapter (in-process → network), and
  domain/application code is untouched.

  8. Design module APIs as coarse-grained and remote-shaped.
  Pass DTOs/IDs, not internal entities. Make calls chunky (one meaningful
  operation), not chatty (dozens of fine calls). Add idempotency where it'll
  matter. "In-process, but shaped like it's over a network."

  9. Statelessness + externalized config (12-factor) + observability.
  No hidden shared in-memory state between modules; config/secrets from the
  environment; structured logs with correlation IDs from day one — so
  cross-module tracing works the moment they become network calls.

  What changes vs. what stays on the split (the payoff)

  ┌──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │      Stays the same ✅       │                Changes 🔧                │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Domain logic & invariants    │ Transport: in-process call →             │
  │                              │ HTTP/gRPC/event                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Module boundaries & public   │ Deployment: 1 process → N services +     │
  │ contracts                    │ gateway                                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ By-ID references between     │ Physical data: shared DB →               │
  │ modules                      │ database-per-service                     │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Ports (the interfaces)       │ Adapters behind ports (in-proc → network │
  │                              │  client)                                 │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Event definitions & handlers │ Event bus: in-memory → real broker       │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ —                            │ New: retries, timeouts, saga, tracing,   │
  │                              │ versioning                               │
  └──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  If you did it right, domain and application code barely move — you add
  plumbing at the edges. That's "minimum changes."

  The two failure modes to avoid

  - Distributed monolith (worst outcome): you split deployment but modules still
    share a database / call each other's internals / need synchronous chains
    for every request. You get microservice pain with none of the benefit.
    Prevented by principles #2, #3, #4.
  - Premature distribution: building brokers, gateways, and sagas before you
    have the scale/team pressure. Over-engineering. Build the seams, not the 
    plumbing.

  The KISS caveat

  Do not build the distributed machinery upfront. The monolith should stay a
  monolith — one process, one deployment, in-process calls, one physical DB
  (with logical separation). You're only paying for clean seams: capability
  modules, enforced boundaries, data ownership, by-ID refs, ports, events. Those
  are cheap now and are 90% of the split cost later.

  ▎ One-liner: organize by business capability into modules with enforced 
  ▎ boundaries, owned data, by-ID references, and port/event-based communication
  ▎ — then a microservice split is "give a module its own process, database, 
  ▎ and network transport," while its domain, contracts, and structure stay put.
  ▎ Build the seams from day one; add the distributed plumbing only when scale 
  ▎ demands it.

  This is exactly what we did to the LMS domain (capability modules + shared
  kernel + by-ID + no cross-module imports) — the same thinking, applied to all
  layers, is the general answer.

  Want me to save this as a standalone note (it's broader than one step — could
  live in docs/ as an architecture principle, or in the path-to-production steps
  13–15)?

❯ so, now tell me how would i approach this considering my previous question.   

⏺ Here's how I'd approach it for this project — the key realization is that the
  microservice-ready principles apply to all four layers, not just domain/.
  Right now you have a slight inconsistency: domain/ is subdomain-first (each
  capability is a folder), but the top level is still layer-first (api/ 
  application/ domain/ infrastructure/). To make a split a true lift-out, the
  subdomain seam must run through every layer.

  The approach: make each subdomain a full vertical slice

  A future service = one subdomain's api + application + domain + infrastructure
  + its data. So the cleanest end-state is to promote subdomains to the top 
  level, each containing its own layers:

  src/
  ├── shared/                     # tiny kernel (IDs, base error) — the only
  thing shared
  ├── catalog/
  │   ├── domain/                 # book, bookcopy, isbn, enums, exceptions
  │   ├── application/            # catalog use cases (step 9)
  │   ├── infrastructure/         # catalog repo impls (step 10)
  │   └── api/                    # catalog endpoints (step 11)
  ├── lending/    { domain, application, infrastructure, api }
  ├── fines/      { domain, application, infrastructure, api }
  ├── membership/ { domain, application, infrastructure, api }
  └── notifications/ { ... }

  Extracting lending becomes "copy the lending/ folder + shared/, add transport"
  — one top-level folder is one service.

  Why decide this now

  It's the cheapest moment. Only domain/ has code;
  api/application/infrastructure/ are empty. If you commit to vertical slices
  now, the only cost is moving domain/catalog/* → catalog/domain/* (imports
  change domain.catalog → catalog.domain) — trivial while it's fresh. If you
  wait until steps 9–11 fill the outer layers, the reshuffle gets much more
  expensive.

  What stays true in either structure

  - Modules depend only on shared/; no module imports another module's 
    internals.
  - Cross-module references by ID; within-module transactions only.
  - The 4-layer dependency rule still holds — just nested inside each module
    (catalog/api → catalog/application → catalog/domain ← 
    catalog/infrastructure).

✻ Churned for 4m 7s

❯ now, tell me what happens to shared folder , is this part of every services   
  and hence duplicated ?                                                        

⏺ Sharp question — and it exposes the single biggest risk with a shared kernel:
  shared code = coupling between services. The whole reason we kept shared/ tiny
  is so this question has a cheap answer. Here's what actually happens.

  Three possible fates for shared/ on a split

  There's no single answer — you pick per shared type:

  Option: A. Published shared library
  What it means: shared/ becomes a versioned package (lms-shared==1.2.0 on a
  private registry); every service depends on it
  Cost / risk: Simple, but creates coupling — changing it means coordinated
  version bumps + redeploys across services
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: B. Duplicated per service
  What it means: Each service copies the couple of types it needs and owns its
  copy
  Cost / risk: No shared dependency, full independence — but risk of drift
  (copies diverge)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: C. Dissolved into primitives at the boundary
  What it means: The shared types are so trivial they vanish over the wire — an
  ID is just a string/UUID in JSON; each service uses its own local type
  Cost / risk: Zero sharing, zero coupling — best when the shared thing is
  genuinely trivial

  For our shared/ specifically, the answer is mostly C (with a bit of B)

  Look at what's actually in it — typed IDs + a base exception. Neither needs to
  be a heavyweight shared dependency:

  - IDs: over the wire, MemberId("abc-123") is just the string "abc-123" in
    JSON. A service receiving it doesn't need the class — it needs the value. So
    each service either re-declares the couple of ID types it uses (trivial, ~3
    lines each) or uses plain strings at its API boundary. The EntityId base
    dissolves.
  - DomainError base: each service has its own error-handling anyway; a 5-line
    base class is duplicated without a second thought.

  So our shared kernel doesn't become a big shared library that couples everyone
  — it largely evaporates on split, exactly because we kept it to trivial,
  stable types. That was the point of rule #5 ("keep the shared kernel tiny and
  stable") — a tiny kernel is cheap to duplicate or dissolve; a fat one becomes
  a coupling nightmare.

  The distinction that matters most: shared kernel ≠ shared contracts

  This trips people up. Two different things:

  - Shared kernel = shared domain code (our shared/). You want to minimize this
    — every shared line couples the services' deployments. The ideal shared
    kernel approaches nothing.
  - Shared contracts = the API request/response schemas and event schemas
    services use to talk. Services must agree on these — but they're versioned 
    interface definitions (OpenAPI, protobuf, event schemas), not shared domain 
    code. The domain logic behind each contract stays private to its service.

  So when lending calls catalog after the split, they share a contract ("give me
  copy X, here's the response shape"), not the BookCopy class. catalog's
  BookCopy entity stays private; only its API schema is public.

  The danger to avoid: the shared kernel as a hidden distributed-monolith

  If the shared library keeps growing — someone drops a shared entity, a shared
  service, common business logic into it — you get the worst outcome: services
  that must be redeployed in lockstep whenever shared changes. That's a
  distributed monolith wearing a microservices badge. The guardrail: only truly 
  universal, rarely-changing, behavior-light types belong in shared/. IDs and a
  base error qualify. A Money VO used by two services? Debatable — lean toward
  duplicate it rather than share it, to keep services independent.

  The answer in one line

  ▎ shared/ is available to every module now (in-process), but on a split it 
  ▎ does NOT have to become a duplicated dependency baked into every service. 
  ▎ Because we kept it trivial, it either dissolves (IDs → strings over the 
  ▎ wire), gets duplicated (the 5-line base error), or — only if worth it — 
  ▎ becomes a thin versioned library. What services genuinely share isn't the 
  ▎ kernel code, it's the versioned API/event contracts — and those keep each 
  ▎ service's domain private. The smaller the shared kernel, the more 
  ▎ independent the services; ours is small on purpose.

  So: not "duplicated into every service" as an inevitability — it's a
  deliberate choice, and the reason we made the kernel tiny is precisely so that
  choice is cheap and doesn't re-couple the services you just separated.

✻ Cooked for 44s

❯ now, consider there are third party libraries which almost all micro services 
  need , what should be approach ?                                              

⏺ Good — and note this is a different problem from the shared kernel. That was
  our domain code; this is third-party/platform dependencies (logging, HTTP
  client, tracing, auth/JWT, serialization, DB driver, resilience). The instinct
  to "share them all in one place" is exactly the trap to be careful about, so
  let me give the balanced approach.

  First principle: standardize the choice, not a forced shared artifact

  The microservice default is that each service owns its own dependency manifest
  (pyproject.toml/requirements.txt) and pins its own versions — because
  independent deployability requires independent versioning. If all services
  must be on the exact same version of a shared bundle, you've recreated
  lockstep deploys = distributed monolith.

  So the goal isn't "one shared dependency everyone imports." It's consistency 
  of choice + convention, achieved with the lightest coupling that works.

  Categorize the "common" libraries — treat each differently

  Not all common deps are the same. Three buckets, three approaches:

  Bucket: Trivial ubiquitous libs
  Examples: http client, JSON, datetime, pydantic
  Approach: Each service just declares its own. Standardize the choice
  ("everyone
  uses httpx") via a guideline/lint — no shared artifact needed.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Bucket: Cross-cutting glue (behavioral, needs consistent config)
  Examples: logging format, tracing init, auth middleware, error-envelope,
  health
  checks
  Approach: A thin internal "platform"/"starter" library — OR a service 
  template.
  See below.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Bucket: Pure infra concerns (no app code needed)
  Examples: mTLS, retries, rate-limiting, traffic tracing
  Approach: Push out of the app into a sidecar / service mesh (Envoy/Istio) or
  the platform. Services don't depend on a library at all.

  For the cross-cutting glue — two good patterns

  The stuff that benefits from consistency (every service should
  log/trace/authenticate the same way):

  Option 1 — thin internal platform library ("starter"). Wrap the common setup
  (configure logging, init tracing, auth middleware, standard error handler)
  into one small internal package services depend on. Like Spring Boot starters
  or a company "platform SDK." Rules to keep it from becoming the trap:
  - Thin and stable — glue/config only, no business logic.
  - Backward-compatible + independently adoptable — services pin their own
    version and upgrade on their own schedule. Never "everyone must be on 2.0 by
    Friday."
  - Additive changes, semantic versioning.

  Option 2 — service template / golden path (often better). Instead of a runtime
  shared library, provide a scaffold (cookiecutter/template repo) that
  pre-includes the standard deps + config. New services start consistent, then
  own their copy. No runtime coupling at all; drift is the cost. Many teams
  prefer this precisely because it gives day-1 consistency without a
  shared-dependency chain.

  Aligning versions without forcing lockstep

  To keep everyone roughly aligned without a mandatory single artifact: publish
  a BOM / constraints file (a recommended-versions list — like a Maven BOM or a
  shared constraints.txt). Services reference it to get sane defaults but can
  override when they need to. Recommendation, not a mandate.

  The coupling caution (same trap as the shared kernel)

  ▎ The more a shared library forces — same version, lockstep upgrades, business
  ▎ logic inside it — the more it re-couples the services you split apart. A 
  ▎ fat "common" library everyone must upgrade together is a distributed 
  ▎ monolith in disguise.

  So: keep shared platform libs thin, stable, backward-compatible, and 
  independently versioned; prefer templates + guidelines + a BOM (loose
  coupling) over a mandatory runtime bundle; and push pure-infra concerns to the
  mesh/sidecar so they aren't dependencies at all.

  The answer in one line

  ▎ Don't bundle all common third-party libs into one shared dependency every 
  ▎ service must share — that re-couples them. Instead: each service owns its 
  ▎ manifest (independence); standardize the choice via guidelines/templates; 
  ▎ put cross-cutting glue in a thin, backward-compatible, independently-adopted
  ▎ platform library or a service template; push pure-infra concerns (tracing, 
  ▎ mTLS, retries) into a sidecar/mesh; and align versions with a recommended 
  ▎ BOM, not a mandate. Consistency by convention, coupling by choice — never 
  ▎ lockstep.

  Want me to fold both this and the shared-kernel discussion into the
  microservices appendix doc (a "what to share, and how" section)? It rounds out
  the split story nicely.

✻ Crunched for 49s

❯ yes, in short you are saying every service is independant and whatever shared 
  library every service need that has to be maintened separatly by the service  
  owner ?                                                                       

⏺ Close, but let me correct one word — "maintained separately by each service 
  owner" isn't quite right, and the distinction matters.

  The precise version

  Two things are independent; one is usually not duplicated:

  ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │             Aspect             │              Who owns it               │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Deployment & version choice —  │                                        │
  │ which version of a lib a       │ The service owner — always independent │
  │ service runs, and when it      │  ✅                                    │
  │ upgrades                       │                                        │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                │ Maintained centrally in ONE place (a   │
  │ The shared library's code —    │ platform/owning team), published as a  │
  │ the actual implementation      │ versioned artifact — not               │
  │                                │ re-implemented per service             │
  └────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  So the accurate statement is: the shared library is maintained centrally as 
  one versioned package; each service independently decides which version to 
  adopt and when. Central maintenance, decentralized consumption. That's
  different from "each owner maintains their own copy."

  When it is "each owner maintains their own"

  Only if you deliberately picked the duplication / template approach — copy the
  code into each service. Then yes, each owner owns their copy (and you accept
  drift as the price of zero coupling). That's a valid choice for trivial things
  (a 5-line base error), but you wouldn't duplicate a real logging/tracing
  setup — that gets maintained centrally.

  And the third category

  For plain external third-party libs (httpx, pydantic): nobody internal
  maintains them — each service just declares them in its own manifest. The team
  only standardizes the choice ("we all use httpx") via a guideline.

  So the corrected one-liner

  ▎ Every service is independently deployed and independently chooses its 
  ▎ versions — yes. But shared internal code is maintained centrally (one owner,
  ▎ one versioned artifact) and consumed independently, not re-implemented per 
  ▎ team. Duplication-per-service is a separate, deliberate choice reserved for 
  ▎ trivial things. The independence is in adoption timing, not in 
  ▎ re-maintaining the same code five times.

  Now folding both this and the shared-kernel discussion into the appendix:

      110  ## How the LMS applies this
      111  
      112  - **Capability modules:** `domain/` is organized by subdomain 
           (`catalog`, `lending`, `fines`, `membership`, `notifications`)
            — each a future service (see step-08a §8a.1).

⏺ Corrected the nuance and folded it into the appendix.

  The one correction to your summary

  "Every service independent" ✅ — but shared code isn't re-maintained by each 
  owner. The accurate version:

  - Independent: deployment + which version each service runs + when it upgrades
    → owned by the service team.
  - Shared internal code: maintained centrally (one owner, one versioned
    artifact), consumed independently. Not five copies.
  - Duplication per service happens only when you deliberately choose it (for
    trivial things like a 5-line base error).
  - Plain external libs (httpx, pydantic): nobody internal maintains them; each
    service just declares them, standardizing only the choice.

  So the independence is in adoption timing, not in re-implementing the same
  code N times.

  Added to docs/appendix-modular-monolith-to-microservices.md

  A new "What to share across services — and how" section covering:
  1. The shared kernel's three fates (dissolve / duplicate / thin library) —
     ours mostly dissolves.
  2. Shared contracts ≠ shared kernel (versioned API/event schemas, not domain
     code).
  3. Third-party/platform libs — standardize the choice; thin platform lib or
     template for glue; sidecar/mesh for pure-infra; BOM for version alignment.
  4. The ownership rule (central maintenance, independent adoption) and the trap
     (fat lockstep library = re-coupling).

  The appendix now tells the full "what to share and how" story end to end. Say
  next, or point me at the next entity.

✻ Baked for 21s

❯ outside user generally dont talk to fines services directly over api, what if 
  any service want to connect to another services internally and do not want    
  to expose to end user ?                                                       

⏺ Great question — this is the north-south vs east-west distinction, and it's
  central to how real microservice systems are laid out.

  Two kinds of traffic

  - North-south = external clients ↔ your system. Goes through a public API 
    gateway. This is what end users hit.
  - East-west = service ↔ service, inside the system. Never touches the public
    internet.

  fines is a classic internal-only service: end users don't call it directly;
  lending (or a member-facing endpoint) talks to it east-west.

  How a service stays internal (not exposed to end users)

  You don't rely on "please don't call it" — you make it unreachable from
  outside, at the network layer:

  1. No public ingress. Only the gateway (and whatever public-facing services it
     fronts) gets a public route. Internal services get a cluster-internal 
     address only — e.g. in Kubernetes, a ClusterIP Service (fines.internal),
     not a LoadBalancer/Ingress. There's simply no public URL to hit.
  2. The gateway exposes only public endpoints. It routes external requests to
     public services and does not proxy internal ones. fines' API isn't
     registered on the gateway → invisible to users.
  3. Network policies / service mesh. A mesh (Istio/Linkerd) or network policy
     says "fines accepts traffic only from lending and membership," enforced
     with mTLS + authorization policies. Even inside the cluster, only allowed
     callers get through.
  4. Service identity, not user identity. Internal calls authenticate with
     service credentials (mTLS certs / service accounts / internal tokens) — a
     different auth path from end-user JWTs. So fines trusts services, and the
     outside world has no valid identity to reach it.

  Net effect: fines has a real API, but it's a private, east-west API — routable
  only from named internal callers.

  How services actually talk east-west

  Two mechanisms — prefer async where you can:

  - Asynchronous (events) — preferred for decoupling. lending publishes
    LoanReturnedLate to a broker; fines subscribes and assesses the fine.
    lending doesn't even know fines exists — maximum independence, and fines can
    be down without blocking a return.
  - Synchronous (internal HTTP/gRPC) — when you need an immediate answer.
    lending calls http://fines.internal/assess via service discovery. Simpler,
    but couples the two at request time (fines must be up).

  For "assess a fine on late return," the event approach is the cleaner fit — it
  keeps lending unaware of fines.

  The internal API still has a contract — just a private one

  Internal endpoints still have request/response schemas (the contract). The
  difference: they're private contracts with a handful of known internal
  consumers, so they can evolve faster and don't need public
  versioning/backward-compat guarantees the way north-south public APIs do.

  A service can be internal-only, public-only, or both

  - Internal-only: fines, notifications — no public route.
  - Public + internal: catalog might expose search to users (north-south) and
    answer "is copy X available?" to lending (east-west).
  - Member-facing views of internal data: a user can see their fines — but not
    by calling fines directly. The gateway / BFF (backend-for-frontend) calls
    fines internally and returns a member-friendly response. The user hits the
    gateway; the gateway does the east-west call.

  How this maps to our monolith today

  You already have this split in-process — no new machinery needed:
  - api/ layer = north-south (what's exposed to users).
  - A module calling another module's interface / publishing an event =
    east-west (internal).

  lending telling fines to assess a fine is, today, an in-process call to fines'
  interface or an in-process domain event. On a split, that same call becomes
  an east-west network call or a broker event, and you choose not to expose
  fines on the gateway. The code doesn't change — only the transport and the
  routing config. That's exactly the "design modules as if already services"
  payoff.

  ▎ One-liner: internal (east-west) service-to-service traffic never goes 
  ▎ through the public gateway and isn't exposed to users — internal services 
  ▎ get a cluster-internal address only, are protected by network policy / mesh 
  ▎ mTLS + service identity, and talk via internal HTTP/gRPC or (preferably) 
  ▎ events. Users only ever reach the gateway, which fronts public services and 
  ▎ does the east-west calls on their behalf. In the monolith, "internal call" 
  ▎ is just calling another module's interface / an in-process event — the split
  ▎ turns that into east-west transport without changing the code.

  Want this added to the appendix doc (a "north-south vs east-west / internal
  APIs" section)?

❯ so, all these decisions are part of HLD not LLD ...right ?                    

⏺ Added the "Internal vs public APIs — north-south and east-west" section to the
  appendix (north-south vs east-west, how a service stays internal, async/sync
  east-west, internal-only vs public services, the private-contract point, and
  the in-process mapping).

  Now your question:

  Yes — mostly HLD, and that's an important distinction to be crisp about

  High-Level Design (HLD) = the macro architecture: how the system is decomposed
  into services/modules, how they communicate, deployment topology, network
  boundaries, data ownership, gateways, messaging. Low-Level Design (LLD) = the
  micro design inside a component: classes, methods, aggregates, invariants,
  patterns, interfaces.

  Sorting our recent discussions:

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │                       Topic                        │    HLD or LLD?     │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Monolith vs microservices; when to split           │ HLD                │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Subdomain/module boundaries, bounded contexts      │ HLD (strategic     │
  │                                                    │ DDD)               │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ North-south vs east-west, API gateway, service     │ HLD                │
  │ mesh, network isolation                            │                    │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Database-per-service, event bus vs sync calls      │ HLD                │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Shared kernel / shared library / dependency        │ HLD (with LLD      │
  │ strategy                                           │ spillover)         │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Entities vs VOs, aggregates, invariants            │ LLD (tactical DDD) │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Strategy/State/Factory patterns, CQS, class design │ LLD                │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Book/Fine class internals, by-ID references        │ LLD                │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────┘

  But there's a nuance worth holding

  The line isn't a wall — strategic DDD (subdomains/bounded contexts) is the 
  bridge between them. That's why our by-subdomain package structure matters: an
  HLD decision (these are the service seams) gets encoded in LLD (module
  boundaries, shared/ kernel, by-ID references). Good design makes the HLD
  visible in the code, so the two stay consistent.

  So for this project, which is framed as an LLD/tactical-DDD learning exercise
  (per CLAUDE.md), the recent thread drifted into HLD territory — deliberately,
  because you asked "how do I keep the monolith split-ready?" That's a
  legitimate excursion, but note:

  - The docs steps 1–12 are LLD (design the domain). ← the project's core
  - The docs steps 13–28 are the HLD/ops path to production (containers, k8s,
    service topology). ← where the microservice discussion actually belongs
  - The appendix we've been filling is squarely HLD.

  ▎ One-liner: decomposition, communication, topology, and network boundaries 
  ▎ are HLD; classes, aggregates, invariants, and patterns are LLD. Strategic 
  ▎ DDD (subdomains) is the seam between them — which is exactly why we let an 
  ▎ HLD-shaped concern (microservice-readiness) drive an LLD decision 
  ▎ (by-subdomain packaging).

  Given the project is primarily LLD, want to steer back to the tactical work —
  finishing Member/Loan/BookCopy — or keep exploring the HLD/production side?
