✻ Churned for 13s

❯ do you thik inside sub-domain there should be directory for enums and         
  exceptions ?                                                                  

⏺ No — keep them as single modules (enums.py, exceptions.py) inside each
  subdomain. A directory there would be pure ceremony right now. Here's the
  reasoning, same KISS test we used earlier.

  Count what's actually in them (per subdomain, after the split)

  ┌──────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │             File             │      Contents       │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ catalog/domain/enums.py      │ 1 enum (CopyStatus) │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ catalog/domain/exceptions.py │ 3 exceptions        │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ lending/domain/enums.py      │ 1 enum (LoanStatus) │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ lending/domain/exceptions.py │ 3 exceptions        │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ fines/domain/enums.py        │ 1 enum (FineStatus) │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ fines/domain/exceptions.py   │ 2 exceptions        │
  └──────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  A directory (package) to hold one enum or two-to-three exception classes buys
  nothing — you'd add an __init__.py + re-exports to wrap ~10 trivial lines.
  That's structure without payoff, and it fights the project's YAGNI default.

  The deciding rule (same as the domain-layout call earlier)

  ▎ A directory earns its place only when a single file gets unwieldy — many 
  ▎ items, or per-item logic worth separating into its own file.

  - VOs got their own files (money.py, isbn.py, daterange.py) because each has
    real logic (validation, factories, methods) — one file each stays readable,
    and one-VO-per-file is a sensible unit.
  - Enums/exceptions are just declarations — a flat enums.py / exceptions.py per
    subdomain is the readable unit. Splitting CopyStatus into
    enums/copy_status.py would be over-engineering.

  And note: the split reduced how much lives in each — after moving to
  per-subdomain, catalog's enums file holds just CopyStatus, not all three. So
  directories make even less sense now than before.

  When would a directory earn its place?

  If a subdomain grew such that a single file got hard to scan — e.g. catalog
  accumulates 5+ enums, or lending grows a large exception hierarchy with
  per-error logic. Then you'd promote exceptions.py → exceptions/ with a file
  per group and an __init__.py re-export. Do it reactively, when the file hurts
  — not preemptively.

  The consistent convention

  ▎ Within a subdomain: one flat module per concern (enums.py, exceptions.py), 
  ▎ one file per value object / entity (they carry logic). Promote a module to a
  ▎ package only when it outgrows one readable file.

  So: leave enums.py and exceptions.py as modules. It's the KISS-correct choice,
  and it's consistent with why VOs did get their own files (logic) while
  enums/exceptions don't (declarations).

✻ Baked for 37s

❯ or VO                                                                         

⏺ Same answer, same reasoning: keep VOs flat in domain/ too — for now. Look at
  what each subdomain's domain/ actually holds:

  ┌───────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │      catalog/domain/      │   lending/domain/    │    fines/domain/    │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ book.py, bookcopy.py      │ loan.py (entity)     │ fine.py (entity)    │
  │ (entities)                │                      │                     │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ isbn.py (1 VO)            │ daterange.py (1 VO)  │ money.py (1 VO)     │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ enums.py, exceptions.py   │ enums.py,            │ enums.py,           │
  │                           │ exceptions.py        │ exceptions.py       │
  └───────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  Each subdomain has ~5 files and exactly one VO. A value_objects/ directory to
  hold a single file is the same ceremony we just rejected for enums/exceptions
  — over-engineering.

  The consistent rule (now applied at every level)

  ▎ A directory earns its place only when a single flat folder gets hard to scan
  ▎ — promote reactively, not preemptively.

  - Whole domain/ was split into files because there were ~13 classes across the
    domain → one file each is the readable unit.
  - Within one subdomain, domain/ has ~5 files → flat is more readable than
    sprinkling entities/, value_objects/, services/ subdirectories over 5 files.
    The subdirectories would add nesting for no navigation gain.
  - The tactical role is already self-evident from the code:
    @dataclass(frozen=True) = VO; identity + equality-by-id = entity. You don't
    need a folder to label it.

  When VOs would earn a subdirectory

  If a subdomain's domain/ grew to, say, 8+ files mixing several entities,
  several VOs, and domain services — then grouping by tactical type aids
  navigation:

  lending/domain/
  ├── entities/       # loan.py, reservation.py, …
  ├── value_objects/  # daterange.py, loan_period.py, …
  ├── services/       # borrowing_service.py
  ├── enums.py
  └── exceptions.py

  But that's a future refactor triggered by size, not something to build now
  with one VO per subdomain.

  The convention, complete

  ▎ - Whole domain → split by subdomain (done).
  ▎ - Within a subdomain → flat files while it's small (one file per entity/VO; 
  ▎ enums.py/exceptions.py as modules).
  ▎ - Promote to subdirectories (entities/, value_objects/, services/, or 
  ▎ exceptions/) only when a folder outgrows a quick scan (~8+ files).

  So: leave isbn.py, money.py, daterange.py flat in their subdomain's domain/.
  Grouping one VO into a value_objects/ folder now would be nesting for its own
  sake — the exact opposite of the KISS calls we've been making.

✻ Crunched for 34s

❯ let's design the Member entity, i will do it give me idea how to do it ?      

⏺ Perfect one to design yourself — Member is deliberately narrow (the step-7
  surprise), so the real lesson is as much about what it doesn't have as what it
  does. I'll spec it; you write it.

  The contract (from step 7)

  Member : register · update_profile · block / unblock     (commands)
           is_active · profile getters                     (queries)
           guards: valid email; active/blocked status
           Must NOT: count its own active loans · hold a list of Loans · decide
  borrowing eligibility alone

  Behavior-first, then attributes

  1. Identity — MemberId (from shared.ids), equality by id (same __eq__/__hash__
  shape as Book/Fine).

  2. The commands drive the fields:
  - block() / unblock() → needs an _active: bool (default True), private.
  - update_profile(...) → edits name/email.
  - So attributes: id, name, email, _active.

  3. Invariants at construction: non-empty name; a valid email.

  4. Queries: is_active (property), profile getters (name/email — plain
  attributes are fine).

  The one real design decision: is email a VO or a str?

  Run it through the three-way test (§8a.7): email has a rule (format), so it's
  a VO candidate — that's the "wrap when it carries rules" case.

  ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │             Option             │               Trade-off                │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Email VO                       │ Validated once at construction,        │
  │ (membership/domain/email.py)   │ reusable, type-safe. The "correct" DDD │
  │                                │  choice — email has a genuine rule.    │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ plain str + validate in        │ Simpler; fine for a supporting         │
  │ Member.__init__                │ subdomain (CLAUDE.md says keep         │
  │                                │ Membership "boringly simple").         │
  └────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  My lean: make an Email VO — it's the same reasoning that made ISBN a VO (a
  rule beyond the raw type), and it's a nice parallel. But str-with-validation
  is a defensible KISS call for a supporting subdomain. Your call — decide
  before you write.

  The deliberate ABSENCE (the important part)

  Member must NOT have:
  - ❌ a loans list or can_borrow()
  - ❌ any active-loan count

  Add a comment saying why: loans are a separate aggregate; Member can't hold
  them (step 5). "≤ 2 active loans" is decided by the BorrowingService (step 9)
  using a LoanRepository count. Member owns only member-level facts (is_active).
  That missing method is the aggregate boundary showing up as an absence.

  Optional: a register() factory?

  Creation carries a name + generates an id → a mild factory candidate:
  @classmethod
  def register(cls, name, email) -> "Member":
      return cls(MemberId.new(), name, email)   # active by default
  Reasonable (it's a domain verb + mints the id), but a plain constructor with
  the id passed in is also fine. Your judgment — apply the "does creation carry
  logic?" test.

  Self-check when you're done

  - a fresh member is is_active == True
  - block() → is_active == False; unblock() → back to True
  - empty name → rejected at construction
  - invalid email → rejected (by the Email VO, or by the constructor if you went
    with str)
  - two members with the same id are equal
  - there is no way to ask a Member about its loans

  Imports you'll need: from shared.ids import MemberId (+ from 
  membership.domain.email import Email if you make the VO).

  Go write membership/domain/member.py (and email.py if you choose the VO).
  Paste it when done and I'll review against the contract.

❯ why to remove MemberId.new() override ..is it wrong ?                         

⏺ Not wrong — it produces the correct result. It's redundant, and slightly worse
  than the base. Let me show you exactly why.

  The base already returns a MemberId

  @dataclass(frozen=True)
  class EntityId:
      @classmethod
      def new(cls) -> "EntityId":
          return cls(str(uuid.uuid4()))    # ← uses cls, not EntityId

  The key is cls. When you call MemberId.new(), Python binds cls = MemberId, so
  cls(...) constructs a MemberId. The base classmethod is already polymorphic —
  every subclass inherits a .new() that returns its own type. Let me prove it:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Proven: with no override, MemberId.new() already returns a MemberId. So your
  override isn't adding anything.

  Why remove it — three reasons

  Your override:
  class MemberId(EntityId):
      @classmethod
      def new(cls):
          return MemberId(EntityId.new().value)   # convoluted, same result

  1. Redundant. The base already returns a MemberId (just demonstrated). Your
     override reproduces behavior you already inherit — dead effort.
  2. Slightly worse mechanically. It calls EntityId.new() (constructs a
     throwaway EntityId), pulls out .value, then wraps it in MemberId — two
     allocations to do what the base does in one. And it hardcodes MemberId
     instead of using cls, which is the very pattern that makes the base
     reusable.
  3. Inconsistent / confusing. Only MemberId has this override; BookId, CopyId,
     LoanId, FineId all rely on the base. A reader sees the override and wonders
     "what's special about MemberId?" — and the answer is "nothing," which is
     the worst kind of code: it implies intent that isn't there.

  The rule it illustrates

  ▎ Don't override a method to reproduce what you already inherit. If the 
  ▎ subclass override is byte-for-byte equivalent in behavior to the base, 
  ▎ delete it — inheritance already does the job.

  So the whole block collapses back to the one-liner, matching its siblings:

  class MemberId(EntityId): ...

  Not a bug — just noise that suggests a special case that doesn't exist.
  Removing it makes the five IDs uniform and the base's cls polymorphism do its
  job.

✻ Sautéed for 28s

❯ do you think email_address should be common VO ?                              

⏺ Good question, and it's genuinely debatable — but my answer is no, not yet. 
  Keep EmailAddress in membership/. Here's the reasoning, which is the same rule
  we applied to Money.

  The instinct isn't wrong — email feels universal

  Email is a generic concept (unlike, say, ISBN which is obviously
  catalog-specific). It has a stable, behavior-light validation rule. So it
  looks like a shared-kernel candidate. But "generic" isn't the deciding factor
  — reuse is.

  The deciding rule: put a VO where it's used; promote only on genuine reuse

  ▎ A VO lives in the subdomain that uses it. It graduates to shared/ only when 
  ▎ a second subdomain actually needs the same VO — and even then, weigh sharing
  ▎ (coupling) against duplication. Don't pre-share on the hunch it'll be 
  ▎ reused (YAGNI).

  Right now, only membership uses email. So it belongs in
  membership/domain/email.py — exactly like we put Money in fines/ (only fines
  uses it), not in shared/. Consistency: neither is in the kernel, because
  neither is yet cross-subdomain.

  Why not pre-share it (the coupling cost)

  Everything in shared/ couples every subdomain to it. Add EmailAddress and:
  - catalog, lending, fines — none of which touch email — now transitively
    depend on it.
  - If email validation changes, it ripples through the kernel that everyone
    imports.
  - On a microservice split, a shared VO is a kernel dependency you then have to
    dissolve/duplicate anyway (email is just a string over the wire — same as
    IDs).

  The kernel's whole value is being tiny and stable. A VO that only one
  subdomain uses doesn't earn a spot there.

  When it would graduate

  The trigger is concrete: notifications starts sending emails and needs the 
  same validated EmailAddress. Then you have real two-subdomain reuse and a
  decision to make:

  ┌─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Option          │                     When                      │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Promote to a shared     │ genuinely identical concept + rules, reused   │
  │ common-VO module        │ widely, worth central maintenance             │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Duplicate it (each      │ trivial VO; you'd rather keep subdomains      │
  │ subdomain owns its      │ decoupled than share (the split-readiness     │
  │ copy)                   │ lean)                                         │
  └─────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Note it wouldn't go into shared/ids.py (that's the ID kernel) — it'd be a
  separate shared/value_objects/ or a "generic" module. But you cross that
  bridge when the second user appears, not before.

  The honest nuance

  There is a school of thought that generic value objects (Email, PhoneNumber,
  Address, Money) form a small shared "common types" kernel from day one. It's
  defensible. But it trades early consistency for early coupling — and given
  this project's explicit stance (minimal shared kernel, 
  microservice-split-ready), the lean is: start local, promote on real reuse.

  ▎ One-liner: EmailAddress is a membership VO until a second subdomain 
  ▎ genuinely needs it — same call we made for Money in fines/. Generic-ness 
  ▎ invites sharing; only actual reuse justifies paying the coupling. Keep the 
  ▎ kernel tiny; promote reactively, and even then consider duplication over 
  ▎ sharing.

  So: membership/domain/email.py. If notifications later needs it, we revisit —
  and I'll flag it as the moment to decide promote-vs-duplicate.

❯ while writing functionality within update_profile what is should consider in  
  entity class ?                                                                

⏺ Good — update_profile is a mutating command, and there's a surprising amount
  of judgment in it. Here's what to consider, most important first.

  1. It's a command (CQS) → changes state, returns nothing

  Returns None. It does, it doesn't answer. No return value to inspect.

  2. Re-assert the same invariants the constructor guards

  An update must never poke the entity into a state the constructor would have
  rejected. So the same rules apply: non-empty name, valid email. This is a DRY
  smell waiting to happen — you don't want the check in two places. Extract a
  private helper and call it from both constructor and update_profile:
  def _set_name(self, name: str) -> None:
      if not name.strip():
          raise ValueError("name cannot be empty")
      self._name = name.strip()
  Now __init__ and update_profile both call _set_name — one source of truth for
  the rule.

  3. Decide what is updatable — and what is NOT

  Be explicit about the boundary:
  - ❌ id — never. Identity is fixed for life.
  - ❌ status — not here. Blocking is a different command (block/unblock) with
    its own rules. Don't let update_profile change status — that would blur two
    distinct behaviors (Single Responsibility). update_profile touches profile
    fields only.
  - ✅ name, email — the mutable profile.

  The method's shape should make illegal edits impossible — if you don't accept
  status, no one can change it via this path.

  4. VOs get replaced, not mutated

  If email is an EmailAddress VO: you assign a new EmailAddress, never reach
  inside the old one (it's frozen). Validation happens at the VO's construction
  — so if update_profile receives a raw string, converting it to
  EmailAddress(...) is the validation. Cleaner: accept an already-built
  EmailAddress, so the VO guarantees validity before it even reaches the entity.

  5. Partial update — only touch what's provided

  A profile update usually changes some fields. Use keyword-only, optional
  params and apply only the ones passed:
  def update_profile(self, *, name: str | None = None, email: EmailAddress |
  None = None) -> None:
      if name is not None:
          self._set_name(name)
      if email is not None:
          self._email = email        # replace the VO
  None means "leave unchanged" (distinct from "set to empty"). Keyword-only (*)
  prevents update_profile("a", "b") positional mix-ups.

  6. Stay pure — no repository, no I/O

  update_profile mutates in-memory state and nothing else. It does not save,
  does not check uniqueness against a DB. Persistence and cross-aggregate checks
  (e.g. "is this email already taken?") are the application service's job: load
  → update_profile(...) → save. (Same rule as everywhere — the entity acts only
  on data it holds.)

  7. Fail loudly with the right error type

  Invalid input → raise (ValueError for input-shape, or a domain error). Don't
  silently ignore a bad value or return False.

  The considerations, distilled

  ┌────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Consider    │                   For update_profile                   │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ CQS            │ command → returns None                                 │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Invariants     │ re-assert constructor's rules (share via a helper —    │
  │                │ DRY)                                                   │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Updatable      │ name/email yes; id never; status belongs to            │
  │ scope          │ block/unblock                                          │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ VOs            │ replace, don't mutate (email → new EmailAddress)       │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Partial update │ keyword-only optional args; None = unchanged           │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Purity         │ no repo/IO; app service loads & saves                  │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Failure        │ raise a meaningful error                               │
  └────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ▎ The through-line: a mutating method is a guarded doorway — it re-checks the 
  ▎ same invariants as birth, changes only the fields it's supposed to, replaces
  ▎ VOs rather than mutating them, and never reaches outside the entity. 
  ▎ Anything about status or persistence is somebody else's command/job.

  Write it and paste — I'll review update_profile against these.

✻ Worked for 44s

❯ so, update_profile should return Member Object ?                              

⏺ No — for Member, update_profile should return None. Returning the Member would
  be a mistake here, and why is a nice test of two principles we've covered.

  Reason 1 — CQS: a command doesn't return data

  update_profile changes state → it's a command → returns nothing. Returning the
  Member blurs command and query (it both does and answers). Keep commands void
  so callers never wonder "did this mutate, or just compute?"

  Reason 2 — entities are mutable; the caller already holds the reference

  This is the key distinction from VOs. A Member mutates in place. The caller
  already has the object — after member.update_profile(name="X"), their member
  is already updated. There's nothing to hand back:

  member = members.get(member_id)     # app service holds the reference
  member.update_profile(name="X")     # mutates in place, returns None
  members.save(member)                # same object, already changed

  No return needed — returning self would be redundant.

  Don't confuse this with VO style (Money.add)

  Here's the trap. Money.add() does return a new object:
  new_total = price.add(tax)   # VO: immutable → returns a NEW Money
  That's because a VO is immutable — you can't mutate it, so you replace it,
  which means returning the new value. An entity is mutable — you change it in
  place, so there's nothing new to return.

  ▎ The rule: immutable VO → operations return a new instance. Mutable entity → 
  ▎ commands mutate in place and return None. Don't apply VO-return-style to an 
  ▎ entity command.

  The one exception you might have seen (and should skip)

  Some codebases return self for method chaining
  (member.update_profile(...).block()) — a "fluent interface." It's a style
  choice, but it conflicts with CQS and isn't idiomatic for a DDD domain model.
  Prefer explicit statements over chaining here. So: skip it.

  Bottom line

  def update_profile(self, *, name=None, email=None) -> None:   # ← returns None
      ...
  The application service that called it already has the reference and will save
  it. Member mutates; it doesn't hand itself back.

✻ Brewed for 24s

❯ can you show me by just writing update_profile ?                              

⏺ Here's update_profile applying all the considerations — plus the little
  validation helper it shares with __init__ (that's the DRY part):

  def _set_name(self, name: str) -> None:
      # single source of truth for the name rule — called by __init__ AND 
  update_profile
      if not name.strip():
          raise ValueError("name cannot be empty")
      self._name = name.strip()

  def update_profile(self, *, name: str | None = None,
                     email: EmailAddress | None = None) -> None:
      # command (CQS) → mutates in place, returns None
      # keyword-only + optional → partial update; None means "leave unchanged"
      if name is not None:
          self._set_name(name)          # re-asserts the same invariant as 
  construction
      if email is not None:
          self._email = email           # VO: replace, don't mutate (validity 
  guaranteed by EmailAddress)
      # NOTE: no `id` (identity is fixed) and no `status` (that's 
  block()/unblock()'s job)

  And your __init__ reuses the helper, so the name rule lives in exactly one
  place:

  def __init__(self, member_id: MemberId, name: str, email: EmailAddress):
      self._id = member_id
      self._set_name(name)              # ← same validation path as 
  update_profile
      self._email = email
      self._status = MemberStatus.ACTIVE

  How each consideration shows up

  - CQS → returns None (mutates in place).
  - Invariant re-asserted → _set_name runs on both create and update; can't set
    an empty name either way.
  - DRY → the name rule exists once, in _set_name.
  - Partial update → * (keyword-only), both params default None, only provided
    fields change.
  - VO replacement → self._email = email assigns a new EmailAddress; validity is
    guaranteed by the VO's own constructor, so Member doesn't re-check format.
  - Scope guarded → no id, no status — status changes go through
    block()/unblock() (SRP).
  - Pure → no repo/IO; the application service loads, calls this, and saves.

  One design choice baked in: email is typed as EmailAddress (the VO), so the
  caller hands in an already-valid email — the entity never touches format
  rules. If you went with a plain str instead, you'd validate here (or better, a
  _set_email helper mirroring _set_name).

  Drop it in and paste the full Member — I'll re-review the whole thing.

✻ Crunched for 19s

❯ if we are not returning Member object then how application will use it or     
  what is the point of just modifying.                                          

⏺ Ah, this is the crux — let me make it click. The key fact: the application 
  service already holds the reference to the Member. Mutating in place changes
  that object, and the service then saves that same object. Nothing is lost, and
  nothing needs returning.

  The full flow (load → mutate → save)

  # application service — the use case
  def update_member_profile(self, member_id, new_name):
      member = self._members.get(member_id)     # 1. LOAD — service now holds a 
  reference
      member.update_profile(name=new_name)       # 2. MUTATE in place — THIS 
  object changed
      self._members.save(member)                 # 3. SAVE — persist the same 
  (now-changed) object

  Trace the member variable: it points to one object the whole time.
  update_profile changed that object's internal state. save(member) writes it
  back. The service doesn't need update_profile to return anything — member is 
  already the updated object.

  Why returning it would be pointless

  member = self._members.get(id)
  same = member.update_profile(name="X")   # if it returned self...
  # `same` and `member` are the SAME object — you gained nothing

  Returning self hands back the reference the caller already has. Redundant.
  (This is exactly why VOs are different — see below.)

  The point of "just modifying"

  The modification is the work. The entity's job is to change its own state
  correctly (guarding invariants). Persistence is a separate, explicit step —
  the application service's job (save). That separation is deliberate:
  - Entity = "make my state valid and correct" (pure, no DB).
  - Application service = "load it, tell it to change, persist it" (the
    transaction boundary).

  In-memory mutation alone does not hit the database — save() does. (Note: some
  ORMs with a unit of work / dirty-tracking — SQLAlchemy, Hibernate —
  auto-detect the change and flush at commit, so you might not even write an
  explicit save. But conceptually the service owns persistence.)

  Contrast: why a VO must return, but an entity must not

  # VO (immutable): the old reference still points to the OLD value
  price = Money.rupees(50)
  price.add(tax)            # ❌ result thrown away — price is STILL ₹50
  price = price.add(tax)    # ✅ MUST capture the returned new object

  # Entity (mutable): the reference points to the SAME object, now changed
  member.update_profile(name="X")   # ✅ member is already updated — nothing to 
  capture

  - VO returns a new instance because your variable still holds the unchanged
    old one — you must reassign.
  - Entity returns None because your variable already is the changed object —
    reassigning would be pointless.

  ▎ The answer to your question: the application doesn't need update_profile to 
  ▎ return the Member because it already holds that same object — the mutation 
  ▎ happened on the object it's holding. The "point" of modifying is to put the 
  ▎ entity into its new valid state; the service then persists that object with 
  ▎ save(). Load → mutate → save. Returning self would just hand back what the 
  ▎ caller already has.

✻ Baked for 30s

❯ so, from repo should always return Enity Object ?                             

⏺ Mostly yes — but with an important split. The precise rule:

  ▎ The aggregate repository returns the aggregate-root entity for the write 
  ▎ path (load → mutate → save). But not everything you fetch is an entity — 
  ▎ read/display and count/existence queries legitimately return scalars or 
  ▎ DTOs.

  The write path: yes, return the entity

  When a use case is going to change something, its repository returns the
  fully-reconstituted aggregate root — a real domain object with behavior:

  member = self._members.get(member_id)   # -> Member (entity, with methods)
  member.update_profile(name="X")         # you need the real object to call 
  this
  self._members.save(member)

  You need the entity (not a data bag) because you're about to call
  update_profile/block/etc. — the behavior lives on the object. Two details:
  - Not found → get returns Member | None, or raises NotFound. So "always an
    entity" really means "the entity, or a clear absence."
  - It returns the aggregate root, not internal child entities directly — you
    reach children through the root.

  Not everything is an entity

  Two big exceptions where returning an entity is wrong or wasteful:

  1. Count / existence → scalars. For "how many active loans?" you don't load
  loan objects — the DB returns a number:
  count = self._loans.count_active_for_member(member_id)   # -> int, not 
  list[Loan]
  exists = self._members.exists(member_id)                 # -> bool
  (This is exactly why BorrowingService gets an int, not a list — the DB counts;
  no entities are hydrated.)

  2. Read / display / reporting → DTOs (read models). To show "my current loans"
  on a screen, loading full aggregates is heavy and unnecessary — return a
  lightweight DTO shaped for the view:
  rows = self._loan_queries.list_for_member(member_id)   # -> 
  list[LoanSummaryDTO]
  This is the CQRS-lite idea: load aggregates to write; project to DTOs to read.
  Some designs even separate these — the aggregate repository returns entities
  (write side), while query/read services return DTOs (read side).

  Always domain-shaped, never DB rows

  Whatever it returns — entity, scalar, or DTO — a repository returns
  domain-layer objects, never raw SQL rows or ORM-specific types. It maps
  persistence → domain at its boundary, so the application/domain code never
  sees the database's shape. That's the whole point of the Repository pattern
  (and the port/adapter split: interface in domain, impl in infrastructure).

  The deciding rule

  ┌──────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │             Purpose              │                Return                │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Going to mutate (a command/use   │ the aggregate-root entity (or        │
  │ case)                            │ None/raise)                          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Just counting / checking         │ a scalar (int, bool)                 │
  │ existence                        │                                      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Just reading for                 │ a DTO / read model                   │
  │ display/reporting                │                                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘

  ▎ One-liner: the repository returns the entity when you're going to change it 
  ▎ (you need its behavior), a scalar for counts/existence, and a DTO for 
  ▎ read-only views — but always a domain object, never a raw DB row. Write path
  ▎ → entities; read path → DTOs.

  So for Member: members.get(id) -> Member (you'll mutate it),
  members.exists(id) -> bool, and a "member directory" screen would use a
  MemberSummaryDTO from a query service — not the Member entity.

⏺ Welcome back! Here's where we are.

  Where we are: Step 8 (writing domain classes) — the coding phase of the DDD 
  build

  The design steps (1–7) are done as prose+decisions; we're in step 8, turning

  What we were doing

  1. Building the domain entities, behavior-first, from the step-7 contracts.
  Status:

  ┌────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │       Entity       │                       State                        │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Book (catalog)     │ ✅ done + verified                                 │

⏺ Stray domain/ gone — clean vertical-slice tree now. Here's the re-review of 
  Member. Progress since last time: MemberStatus now exists ✅. But a couple of
  things got worse, including one that will crash.

  🔴 Blockers (won't run)

  1. MemberId.new() is now infinite recursion (shared/ids.py:28-31):
  class MemberId(EntityId):
      @classmethod
      def new(cls):
          return MemberId.new()      # ← calls itself forever → RecursionError
  Last time this was merely redundant; the edit turned it into a stack-overflow 
  bug — MemberId.new() calls MemberId.new() endlessly. As we proved earlier, the
  base already returns a MemberId via cls. Delete the override entirely:
  class MemberId(EntityId): ...

  2. EmailAddress still doesn't exist (member.py:1). Imported from shared.ids,
  but it's defined nowhere — and it must not live in the shared kernel (it's a
  Membership VO, not an ID). → create membership/domain/email.py with an
  EmailAddress VO and import from there; drop EmailAddress/EntityId from the
  shared.ids import.

  🟠 Modeling error — MemberStatus.UNBLOCKED

  class MemberStatus(Enum):
      BLOCKED = "block"
      ACTIVE = "active"
      UNBLOCKED = "unblock"     # ← this isn't a real state
  A member is either ACTIVE or BLOCKED — that's it. "Unblock" is an action (a
  transition), not a state. UNBLOCKED and ACTIVE would be indistinguishable —
  what's the difference? None. This confuses a state with a command. The
  unblock() method moves a member back to ACTIVE; there's no separate unblocked
  status.
  - Remove UNBLOCKED. States = {ACTIVE, BLOCKED}.
  - Also the values are verbs — BLOCKED = "block". Enum values should name the
    state: BLOCKED = "blocked", ACTIVE = "active".

  🐞 Bug (unchanged from last review)

  3. member.py:11 — self._email: EmailAddress = EmailAddress assigns the class,
  not the email parameter. → self._email = email.

  🟡 Still missing

  4. No __eq__/__hash__ — an entity needs equality by id:
  def __eq__(self, other): return isinstance(other, Member) and self._id ==
  other._id
  def __hash__(self): return hash(self._id)
  5. No non-empty name check at construction (the contract's invariant).
  6. Stubs update_profile/block/unblock are still pass. Now that we designed
     update_profile together, you can fill them:
     - block() → self._status = MemberStatus.BLOCKED
     - unblock() → self._status = MemberStatus.ACTIVE  ← this is what "unblock"
       means (no UNBLOCKED state needed)

  🟢 Style

  7. is_active blacklist → whitelist: return self._status is 
     MemberStatus.ACTIVE.

  ✅ Good

  - register() factory, MemberStatus enum choice, id/name getters, deliberate
    absence of loans/can_borrow — all still solid.

  1. shared/ids.py → delete the MemberId.new() override (crash fix).
  2. enums.py → drop UNBLOCKED; fix values to "blocked"/"active".
  3. email.py → create the EmailAddress VO; fix member.py import.
  4. member.py → line 11 = email; add __eq__/__hash__; name check; whitelist
     is_active; fill block/unblock.

❯ what should be tests file structure , should it be according to src ?         

⏺ Yes — tests should mirror src/ exactly. Now that src/ is vertical-slice
  (subdomain-first), the tests follow the same shape, so the test for any module
  is trivially findable.

  Recommended structure (mirrors the vertical slices)

  tests/
  ├── conftest.py                    # shared fixtures (optional)
  ├── shared/
  │   └── test_ids.py
  ├── catalog/
  │   └── domain/
  │       ├── test_book.py
  │       ├── test_bookcopy.py
  │       ├── test_isbn.py
  │       └── test_enums.py
  ├── lending/
  │   └── domain/
  │       ├── test_loan.py
  │       └── test_daterange.py
  ├── fines/
  │   └── domain/
  │       ├── test_fine.py
  │       └── test_money.py
  └── membership/
      └── domain/
          ├── test_member.py
          └── test_email.py

  - One test_<module>.py per source module — catalog/domain/book.py ↔
    tests/catalog/domain/test_book.py.
  - Tests import from the real modules via pythonpath=["src"] (already set):
    from catalog.domain.book import Book.
  - application/ and api/ test folders come later (steps 9/11) — mirror them
    then. Most tests live in domain/ (pure, instant, no mocks); application
    tests use fake/in-memory repos; only infra tests touch real I/O.

  ⚠️ One config gotcha — duplicate test filenames

  You'll have tests/catalog/domain/test_enums.py and
  tests/fines/domain/test_enums.py — same basename. pytest's default import mode
  chokes on duplicate test-file basenames unless the dirs are packages. Two
  clean fixes (pick one):

  Option A — add __init__.py to every tests dir (make them packages):
  tests/__init__.py, tests/catalog/__init__.py,
  tests/catalog/domain/__init__.py, …

  Option B — use importlib import mode (modern, no __init__.py needed) in
  pyproject.toml:
  [tool.pytest.ini_options]
  pythonpath = ["src"]
  testpaths = ["tests"]
  addopts = "--import-mode=importlib"
  I'd lean Option B — cleaner, no boilerplate __init__.py sprinkled everywhere,
  and it's the current pytest recommendation.

  Cleanup needed

  Your tests/ still has the old layer-first stubs (tests/domain/test_money.py,
  tests/api/, tests/application/, tests/infrastructure/) from before the reorg.
  Those should be replaced by the mirrored structure above — e.g.
  tests/domain/test_money.py → tests/fines/domain/test_money.py.

  The principle

  ▎ Tests mirror src/ one-to-one. Same axis (vertical slices), one 
  ▎ test_<module>.py per source module. It keeps "where's the test for X?" a 
  ▎ zero-thought question, and makes a subdomain's tests travel with it when you
  ▎ extract it into a service (the split-readiness payoff applies to tests 
  ▎ too).

  Want me to set this up now — clean out the old layer-first test stubs, create
  the mirrored tree, add the importlib config, and drop in a first real test
  (say test_member.py or test_money.py) — or just leave you the structure to
  build?

❯ what about api, infrastructure,repository ?                                   

⏺ Good — these are the outer layers (steps 10–11), and each has a clear home in
  the vertical slice, plus a distinct testing approach. Let me lay it out.

  Where each lives in the slice

  The Repository deliberately spans two layers (Dependency Inversion):

  membership/
  ├── domain/
  │   ├── member.py
  │   └── repository.py          # ← Repository INTERFACE (port) — what the
  domain needs
  ├── application/
  │   └── register_member.py     # use case — depends on the interface
  ├── infrastructure/
  │   ├── in_memory_member_repository.py   # ← IMPL (adapter) — fast, for
  tests/dev
  │   └── sql_member_repository.py         # ← IMPL (adapter) — real DB
  └── api/
      └── routes.py              # FastAPI endpoints — thin

  - Repository interface → domain/ (abstract; "I need get(id)/save(m)"). Part of
    the domain because it expresses a domain need.
  - Repository implementations → infrastructure/ (in-memory + SQL). Both satisfy
    the same interface.
  - API → api/ (controllers translating HTTP ↔ application service).

  How you test each layer (and with what double)

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────────────────┬─────────────────┬─────────┐
  │      Layer      │       What you test       │   Test double   │  Speed  │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ domain/         │ entities, VOs, domain     │ none — plain    │ ⚡      │
  │                 │ services                  │ objects         │ instant │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────┤
  │                 │ use-case orchestration    │ in-memory       │         │
  │ application/    │ (load→decide→save)        │ repository      │ ⚡ fast │
  │                 │                           │ (fake)          │         │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────┤
  │                 │ the real SQL repo         │ real/test DB    │ 🐢      │
  │ infrastructure/ │ actually                  │ (integration)   │ slower  │
  │                 │ persists/reconstitutes    │                 │         │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────┤
  │                 │ endpoints: status codes,  │ TestClient +    │ 🐢      │
  │ api/            │ DomainError→400,          │ fakes wired     │ slower  │
  │                 │ request/response          │ under it        │         │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────────────────┴─────────────────┴─────────┘

  The key insight: the in-memory repository is what makes application/ tests 
  fast. Because the use case depends on the interface, you inject the in-memory
  impl in tests — no DB. The SQL impl is only exercised in the few
  infrastructure/ integration tests.

  The mirrored test tree (all layers)

  tests/membership/
  ├── domain/
  │   ├── test_member.py
  │   └── test_email.py
  ├── application/
  │   └── test_register_member.py            # injects InMemoryMemberRepository
  ├── infrastructure/
  │   └── test_sql_member_repository.py       # real DB —
  @pytest.mark.integration
  └── api/
      └── test_member_endpoints.py            # FastAPI TestClient

  Tests mirror src/ at every layer — same one-to-one rule.

  Two patterns worth knowing

  1. Repository contract test (shared). You don't test the abstract interface
  directly — instead, write one test suite that any implementation must pass,
  and run both the in-memory and SQL repos through it. Guarantees the fake
  behaves like the real thing:
  # tests/membership/infrastructure/repository_contract.py
  class MemberRepositoryContract:
      def test_save_then_get_roundtrips(self, repo): ...
      def test_get_missing_returns_none(self, repo): ...
  # InMemory and SQL test classes both inherit it, each providing `repo`

  2. Separate fast unit tests from slow integration. Mark DB/API tests so you
  can skip them locally:
  @pytest.mark.integration
  def test_sql_member_repository(): ...
  Run pytest -m "not integration" for the fast inner loop; full suite in CI.
  This is the test pyramid: many domain unit tests, fewer application tests,
  fewest infra/api integration tests.

  The principle

  ▎ Repository = interface in domain/, implementations in infrastructure/. Test 
  ▎ domain/ with plain objects, application/ with the in-memory repo (fast, no 
  ▎ DB), infrastructure/ with a real DB (few, marked integration), api/ with a 
  ▎ test client. Tests mirror src/ at every layer — so a subdomain's tests, like
  ▎ its code, travel with it on a split.

  Note we're previewing steps 10 (repositories) and 11 (api) here — no such code
  exists yet, only the empty application/ infrastructure/ api/ scaffolds. When
  we build them, this is the shape.

  Want me to set up the full mirrored tests/ tree now (with the importlib config
  and a couple of real domain tests to start — e.g. test_member, test_money),
  or keep it as a blueprint for when we reach steps 10–11?

❯ what is DDD, how many such principles exist ? and can they be grouped based   
  on problems that exists . I want to group all such solving LLD problems       
  techniques.                                                                   

⏺ Great synthesis question — and the honest first answer is: DDD isn't a fixed 
  list of N principles. It's one body of knowledge that composes with several 
  others in LLD. Let me define it, name the bodies, then give you the thing you
  actually want — everything grouped by the problem it solves.

  What DDD is

  Domain-Driven Design = design the software around the business domain and its
  language, so the model directly expresses the rules of the problem. It has two
  halves:
  - Strategic DDD — the big picture: ubiquitous language, bounded contexts,
    subdomains, context mapping.
  - Tactical DDD — the building blocks inside a context: entities, value
    objects, aggregates, domain services, repositories, factories, domain
    events.

  "How many principles?" — it's several toolkits, not one list

  LLD design draws on multiple bodies, and DDD is one of them:

  ┌──────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │     Body     │             Rough size             │ What it gives you  │
  ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ DDD          │ ~8 strategic + ~7 tactical         │ how to model the   │
  │ (strategic + │ building blocks                    │ domain             │
  │  tactical)   │                                    │                    │
  ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ SOLID        │ 5 principles                       │ how to structure   │
  │              │                                    │ classes for change │
  ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ GRASP        │ 9 principles                       │ how to assign      │
  │              │                                    │ responsibility     │
  ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │              │                                    │ reusable solutions │
  │ GoF patterns │ 23 patterns                        │  to recurring      │
  │              │                                    │ designs            │
  ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ General      │ KISS, YAGNI, DRY, CQS,             │ restraint +        │
  │ principles   │ Tell-Don't-Ask,                    │ everyday           │
  │              │ composition-over-inheritance       │ discipline         │
  └──────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────┘

  So there's no single count — it's a toolbox of toolboxes. Which is exactly why
  your instinct to group by problem is the right way to hold it.

  The grouping — LLD techniques by the problem they solve

  1. Discovering & naming the model — problem: turn vague requirements into 
  concepts everyone agrees on.
  → Ubiquitous Language · Bounded Contexts · Subdomains
  (core/supporting/generic) · Context Mapping. (all strategic DDD)

  2. Classifying the building blocks — problem: what KIND of thing is each 
  concept?
  → Entity vs Value Object · Aggregate + Aggregate Root · Domain Service vs
  Application Service · Repository. (tactical DDD)

  3. Protecting correctness (invariants) — problem: prevent invalid state from 
  ever existing.
  → Make illegal states unrepresentable · enforce invariants in
  constructors/guarded methods · VO immutability + self-validation ·
  Encapsulation (private state) · Aggregate as consistency boundary · Fail-fast
  · named domain exceptions.

  4. Placing behavior & responsibility — problem: which object does what? avoid 
  anemic bags AND god objects.
  → GRASP (Information Expert, Creator, Controller, High Cohesion, Low Coupling)
  · Tell-Don't-Ask · Rich domain model · SRP · CQS (command/query separation).

  5. Absorbing change / extensibility — problem: add or swap behavior without 
  editing stable code.
  → OCP / LSP / DIP · Strategy (swappable policy) · State · Factory
  (creation-with-rules) · Observer / Domain Events · Ports & Adapters ·
  Dependency Injection.

  6. Managing dependencies & structure — problem: keep coupling low and the 
  codebase navigable.
  → Layered architecture (domain/app/infra/api) · Dependency Inversion (arrows
  point inward) · reference other aggregates by ID · ISP · package-by-subdomain
  (vertical slices) · hexagonal/clean architecture.

  7. Concurrency & consistency at scale — problem: stay correct under 
  simultaneous access / distribution.
  → Aggregate as the unit of concurrency · small aggregates (less contention) ·
  optimistic/pessimistic locking (in infra) · eventual consistency / sagas ·
  domain events.

  8. Restraint / not over-engineering — problem: complexity for its own sake.
  → KISS · YAGNI · DRY · composition over inheritance · "patterns only when a 
  real problem demands them."

  How this maps to our build (so it's not abstract)

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Problem group  │                      What we did                      │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1 Discover      │ Book vs BookCopy language; five subdomains            │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2 Classify      │ Money/ISBN = VO; Loan = entity; 5 aggregates          │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 3 Correctness   │ Money rejects negatives; Fine settle-once; private    │
  │                 │ _status                                               │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 4               │ Member has no can_borrow (Info Expert); CQS on every  │
  │ Responsibility  │ method                                                │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 5 Change        │ FineCalculationStrategy; Loan.create factory; ports   │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 6 Dependencies  │ vertical slices; by-ID refs; shared/ kernel           │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 7 Concurrency   │ small aggregates; optimistic locking for borrow       │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 8 Restraint     │ BookCopy = transition table not State pattern; no     │
  │                 │ premature microservices                               │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ▎ The mental model: DDD gives you the modeling vocabulary (groups 1–2); 
  ▎ SOLID/GRASP/patterns give you the structuring tools (groups 4–6); general 
  ▎ principles keep you honest (group 8) — and you reach for each when its 
  ▎ specific problem shows up, never as a checklist. That "problem → technique" 
  ▎ reflex is design maturity.

  This is genuinely doc-worthy — a one-page "LLD techniques, grouped by problem"
  map. Want me to save it as docs/appendix-lld-techniques-map.md (or a memory
  note)? It'd be a handy reference to revisit.

✻ Crunched for 34s

❯ This is good, but i want to know how many such techniques exist like DDD and  
  what type of other problems can be solved using all those techniques.         

⏺ Now you're zooming out to the whole landscape — DDD is one methodology among 
  many, sitting at one level of a stack. There's no fixed count (dozens of named
  approaches), but they cluster into 6 categories by level. Here's the map,
  with what problem each level solves.

  The 6 levels of design approaches (DDD lives at level 2)

  Level 1 — Design paradigms (the basic unit of decomposition)

  Problem: what is the fundamental building block?

  ┌───────────────────────┬────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │       Paradigm        │        Unit        │          Best for          │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ OOP                   │ objects            │ domains with rich          │
  │                       │ (state+behavior)   │ behavior/rules (our LMS)   │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Functional            │ pure functions,    │ data transformation,       │
  │                       │ immutability       │ concurrency, correctness   │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Procedural/Structured │ procedures         │ simple scripts, top-down   │
  │                       │                    │ tasks                      │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Data-Oriented         │ data layout        │ performance-critical       │
  │                       │                    │ (games, HPC)               │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Logic/Declarative     │ rules/facts        │ constraint solving,        │
  │                       │                    │ queries                    │
  └───────────────────────┴────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  Level 2 — Domain / analysis methodologies (how to find the model)

  Problem: turn vague requirements into a model. ← DDD is here

  - DDD (strategic + tactical) — complex business domains.
  - OOAD (Object-Oriented Analysis & Design) — general OO modeling; DDD's
    parent.
  - Structured Analysis (DFDs) — older, process/data-flow modeling.
  - Event Storming / Event Modeling — workshop techniques to discover the domain
    via events.
  - CRC cards — responsibility discovery.

  Level 3 — Principle sets (rules for good structure)

  Problem: is this class/responsibility well-formed?

  - SOLID (5) · GRASP (9) · general (KISS, DRY, YAGNI, Tell-Don't-Ask, CQS) ·
    Design by Contract (pre/post-conditions, invariants) · Law of Demeter.

  Level 4 — Pattern catalogs (reusable named solutions)

  Problem: this exact design situation recurs — what's the known solution?

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │               Catalog                │              Solves              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ GoF (23:                             │ object-level recurring designs   │
  │ creational/structural/behavioral)    │                                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                      │ enterprise app patterns          │
  │ PoEAA (Fowler)                       │ (Repository, Unit of Work, DTO,  │
  │                                      │ Identity Map)                    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Enterprise Integration Patterns      │ messaging between systems        │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Concurrency patterns                 │ threads, locks, actors           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Cloud/Distributed patterns           │ Circuit Breaker, Saga, CQRS,     │
  │                                      │ Sidecar, Bulkhead                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  Level 5 — Architectural styles (the shape of the whole system)

  Problem: overall structure + which quality attributes you optimize.

  - Layered · Hexagonal / Clean / Onion (dependency direction, testability) ·
    MVC/MVVM (UI separation) · CQRS + Event Sourcing (read/write split, audit) ·
    Event-Driven / Reactive (decoupling, responsiveness) · Microservices / SOA
    (org scale) · Pipes-and-Filters (stream processing).

  Level 6 — Design processes (how design evolves)

  Problem: arrive at and keep a good design over time.

  - TDD (design via tests) · BDD (design via behavior specs) · Refactoring
    (improve without changing behavior) · Continuous/iterative design (DDD's own
    "boundaries shift as you learn").

  The other axis: the types of problems all of this serves

  Everything above ultimately exists to improve a handful of quality attributes.
  This is the "what types of problems" you asked about — every technique is a
  tool aimed at one or more of these:

  ┌──────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │       Concern        │    "The problem"    │ Techniques that target it  │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Complexity           │ too much to reason  │ DDD, bounded contexts,     │
  │                      │ about               │ modularization             │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Correctness          │ invalid states /    │ invariants, DbC, TDD,      │
  │                      │ bugs                │ types, FP purity           │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Changeability        │ change is           │ SOLID, Strategy, OCP,      │
  │                      │ expensive/risky     │ hexagonal                  │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Coupling/Cohesion    │ tangled             │ GRASP, by-ID refs, DIP,    │
  │                      │ dependencies        │ modules                    │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Testability          │ hard to test        │ ports & adapters, DI, pure │
  │                      │                     │  domain                    │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Performance          │ too slow            │ data-oriented design,      │
  │                      │                     │ caching patterns           │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Concurrency          │ races/contention    │ aggregates, locking,       │
  │                      │                     │ actors, immutability       │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Scalability          │ won't grow          │ CQRS, event-driven,        │
  │                      │                     │ microservices              │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Comprehensibility    │ hard to understand  │ ubiquitous language,       │
  │                      │                     │ screaming architecture     │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Reusability          │ duplication         │ components, composition,   │
  │                      │                     │ patterns                   │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Security/Reliability │ failures, attacks   │ bulkhead, circuit breaker, │
  │                      │                     │  DbC                       │
  └──────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  The crucial insight: you don't pick one — you layer them

  They're not alternatives; they compose down the stack. Our LMS uses,
  simultaneously:
  - OOP paradigm (L1) → DDD methodology (L2) → SOLID/GRASP principles (L3) →
    Strategy/Factory/Repository patterns (L4) → Hexagonal + (future) 
    microservices architecture (L5) → and we've been doing iterative design
    (L6).

  ▎ The map in one line: paradigms decide your building block, analysis 
  ▎ methodologies (DDD) find your model, principles judge your structure, 
  ▎ pattern catalogs supply named solutions, architectural styles shape the 
  ▎ whole system, and design processes evolve it — and they all exist to move 
  ▎ the same set of quality attributes (complexity, correctness, change, 
  ▎ coupling, testability, performance, concurrency, scale). Pick techniques by 
  ▎ which quality attribute is under threat.

  That's the complete landscape — DDD is level 2, one of six levels, and the
  "types of problems" are the quality-attribute table.

  This is a natural extension of the techniques-map doc. Want me to append it to
  docs/appendix-lld-techniques-map.md as a "Landscape of design approaches"
  section?

✻ Worked for 58s

※ recap: We're building a Library Management System as a DDD learning exercise, currently coding step-8 domain entities. Your `Member` entity is mid-edit with a few bugs to fix; the next action is finishing it (crash fix, EmailAddress VO, MemberStatus cleanup). (disable recaps in /config)

❯ so, every problem can be solved by DDD by layering down. or should we apply   
  any different pattern also ?                                                  

⏺ No — and this is the most important meta-lesson of the whole map. DDD is not 
  universal. It's the right tool for one class of problem, and reaching for it
  everywhere is itself an anti-pattern. Two corrections:

  1. "Layering down" is the wrong mental model

  The 6 levels aren't "DDD's layers." DDD is just level 2 (one analysis
  methodology). The other levels are independent of it:

  - You always pick a paradigm (L1), principles (L3), maybe patterns (L4), an
    architecture (L5).
  - But DDD (L2) is optional — you can build a perfectly good system with OOP + 
    SOLID + GoF patterns + layered architecture and zero DDD.

  So it's not "DDD, then layer down." It's "pick a technique at each level that 
  fits the problem — and DDD is a level-2 choice you make only when the domain
  warrants it."

  2. Match the technique to the problem — DDD fits a specific one

  DDD earns its (considerable) modeling cost only when the domain itself is 
  complex. Otherwise it's over-engineering.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬───────────┐
  │      If the hard part is…       │        Reach for…        │   DDD?    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Complex, evolving business      │ DDD (tactical +          │ ✅ yes    │
  │ rules (our Lending core)        │ strategic)               │           │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Simple CRUD                     │ framework scaffolding,   │ ❌        │
  │                                 │ Active Record            │ overkill  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Data / reporting / analytics    │ data-model-first, SQL,   │ ❌ wrong  │
  │                                 │ warehouse                │ tool      │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Performance-critical (games,    │ Data-Oriented Design     │ ❌        │
  │ HPC)                            │                          │           │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Stateless transformation / ETL  │ Functional pipelines     │ ❌        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Algorithm-centric               │ DS&A, good functions     │ ❌        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Throwaway / short-lived         │ just ship it             │ ❌        │
  └─────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴───────────┘

  The signals DDD is worth it: rich behavior (rules are the hard part), domain
  experts + a language that matters, long-lived and evolving. Absent those → a
  lighter approach wins.

  3. Even inside a DDD system, you don't DDD everything

  This is our own project's rule. We lavish tactical DDD on the core (Lending —
  aggregates, invariants, Strategy, State) and keep supporting subdomains 
  boringly simple CRUD (Catalog, Membership). That's DDD's own guidance — spend
  modeling effort where complexity lives, not uniformly. Applying full tactical
  DDD to Book (a metadata bag) would be the over-engineering we keep rejecting.

  The meta-principle

  ▎ There's no universal method. The skill isn't "apply DDD to everything" — 
  ▎ it's diagnosis: identify which quality attribute is under threat 
  ▎ (complexity? performance? change? concurrency?) and reach for the technique 
  ▎ aimed at that. DDD targets domain complexity; if that's not your bottleneck,
  ▎ DDD is the wrong tool, and you pick from the other levels.

  ▎ One-liner: you don't "solve everything by layering DDD down" — you compose 
  ▎ across levels, choosing each technique by the problem in front of you, and 
  ▎ DDD is the level-2 choice reserved for complex, behavior-rich domains. When 
  ▎ the problem is data, performance, or transformation, you reach for a 
  ▎ different paradigm/methodology entirely. Matching tool to problem — that 
  ▎ diagnosis — is the craft.

  So: different problems, different patterns. DDD is a specialist, not a Swiss
  Army knife — and knowing when not to use it is as much a part of design
  maturity as knowing how.

  This is the perfect capstone to the techniques-map doc. Want me to fold both
  this and the "landscape of design approaches" into
  docs/appendix-lld-techniques-map.md?

❯ what is framework scaffolding and Active Records ?                            

⏺ Both are terms from the "simple CRUD" row — and they're worth knowing
  precisely because they're the opposite of what we deliberately built. Let me
  explain each, then the contrast.

  Framework scaffolding

  Auto-generated boilerplate CRUD code. You define a model/schema, and the
  framework generates the controllers, routes, forms, and database access for
  create/read/update/delete — often with one command.

  Examples:
  - Rails: rails generate scaffold Book title:string → full CRUD in seconds.
  - Django: models + the admin site + generic views.
  - Spring Data REST / .NET scaffolding / Laravel artisan.

  The trade-off: blazing fast for simple apps, near-zero code — but you get
  thin, generic behavior. There's no place for rich domain rules; the logic ends
  up anemic. Great when your "domain" is basically forms over a database; wrong
  when the rules are the hard part.

  Active Record

  A persistence pattern (Fowler, PoEAA) where a domain object is tied to a 
  database row and knows how to save itself. The object holds both the data and
  the DB operations:

  # Active Record — the object IS a row and persists itself
  book = Book.find(1)      # class method hits the DB
  book.title = "Clean Code"
  book.save()              # the object writes ITSELF to the DB
  book.delete()

  The class maps 1:1 to a table; instances carry save/delete/find. Examples:
  Rails ActiveRecord, Django ORM models, Laravel Eloquent, SQLAlchemy 
  (AR-style).

  The key contrast — Active Record vs what we built (Repository / Data Mapper)

  This is the important part, because we chose the opposite:

  ┌───────────────┬─────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │               │      Active Record      │  Repository + Data Mapper    │
  │               │                         │       (our DDD choice)       │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Persistence   │ inside the domain       │ in a separate                │
  │ lives…        │ object (book.save())    │ repository/mapper            │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Domain object │ knows about the DB      │ pure — zero DB knowledge     │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Coupling      │ domain ↔ database,      │ domain independent of        │
  │               │ welded                  │ storage                      │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Tests         │ need a DB (or heavy     │ plain objects, no DB         │
  │               │ mocks)                  │                              │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Fits          │ simple CRUD,            │ complex domains, rich        │
  │               │ object≈table            │ invariants, aggregates       │
  └───────────────┴─────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  Our Book has no save() method — that's deliberate. Persistence is the
  repository's job (members.save(member)), the interface lives in domain/, the
  implementation in infrastructure/. That's what keeps the domain pure and
  testable — the entire reason we can unit-test entities with no database.

  Why AR fits CRUD but not our domain

  - Active Record shines when the model is basically a table with little
    behavior — you want speed and less code, and coupling domain to DB is an
    acceptable trade. That's the "simple CRUD" case where DDD would be overkill.
  - It fights you when you need rich invariants, aggregates, a pure domain, and
    framework independence — because it mixes domain logic with persistence (the
    concern-separation DDD insists on). Hence we picked Repository/Data Mapper
    instead.

  ▎ One-liner: framework scaffolding = auto-generated CRUD boilerplate (fast, 
  ▎ shallow); Active Record = objects that persist themselves (book.save(), 
  ▎ object≈row) — both are great for simple CRUD and both couple domain to 
  ▎ database, which is exactly why our complex-domain build rejected them in 
  ▎ favor of the Repository pattern with a pure, persistence-ignorant domain.

  So when that earlier table said "simple CRUD → scaffolding / Active Record,"
  it meant: for the boring cases, use the fast coupled approach; we only paid 
  for Repository because our core domain is complex enough to need a pure model.

❯ so what you mean to say and i what i understand it, let me explain below:-    
  So, Every LLD problems that exist in the world is categorized like heavy      
  business problems, simple CRUD Problems, Algorithm Heavy Problems etc...      
  So, DDD is one of many techniques which specifically target business heavy    
  problems.                                                                     
  Simple Class and DB implementation(dont know what is exact term for this) is  
  enough to design CRUD Problems. Language Framework supports this.             
  And class or functional code(Dont know exact term for this) is just enough to 
  design Algorithm heavy problems.                                              
                                                                                
  Is my understanding correct ?                                                 

⏺ Your understanding is correct — and the good news is there are precise,
  well-known names for the two you weren't sure about. Let me fill in the
  vocabulary and add one refinement.

  Your model, confirmed + the exact terms

  ▎ LLD problems are categorized by type (business-heavy, CRUD, 
  ▎ algorithm-heavy…), and you pick the technique that fits each type.

  ✅ Exactly right. Now the terms:

  ┌──────────────────────┬───────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │     Problem type     │   The technique   │         Exact name          │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Business-heavy       │ rich OO domain    │ Domain Model pattern → this │
  │ (complex rules)      │ model             │  is what DDD builds         │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Simple CRUD ("simple │ procedural per    │ Transaction Script / Active │
  │  class + DB")        │ use-case, or      │  Record                     │
  │                      │ object=row        │                             │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Algorithm-heavy      │ data structures + │ DS&A +                      │
  │ ("class or           │  algorithms,      │ procedural/functional       │
  │ functional code")    │ plain functions   │ design (Big-O thinking)     │
  └──────────────────────┴───────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  So the terms you were reaching for:
  - "Simple class and DB implementation" → Transaction Script (a plain procedure
    per operation) or Active Record (object persists itself). Framework support
    = ORM / scaffolding.
  - "Class or functional code for algorithms" → there's no fancy architecture
    here — it's Data Structures & Algorithms: pick the right data structure +
    algorithm, reason about Big-O. Design-wise it's just clean
    functions/procedures, not domain modeling.

  This is a named, authoritative spectrum (Martin Fowler)

  Your intuition matches Fowler's "domain logic patterns" (from PoEAA) — he
  literally maps complexity of logic → pattern:

  simple logic ─────────────────────────► complex logic
  Transaction Script → Table Module → Domain Model (DDD)

  - Transaction Script — simple, procedural. Good for CRUD/simple logic.
  - Domain Model — rich OO model. Good for complex business logic. ← DDD
  - Between them, Table Module (one class per table).

  Fowler's own guidance: use the simplest one the logic allows — Domain Model
  (DDD) only when complexity justifies it. Which is exactly the "don't
  over-apply DDD" point.

  The one refinement: you categorize parts, not whole systems

  Real systems are mixtures. You don't label the whole app "CRUD" or
  "business-heavy" — you label each part and apply the fitting technique per
  part:

  - Our LMS: Lending = business-heavy → Domain Model / DDD; Catalog & Membership
    = CRUD → could be Transaction Script/Active Record (we kept them "boringly
    simple"); a hypothetical recommendation engine = algorithm-heavy → DS&A.

  So one system can run all three approaches at once, each where it fits.
  (That's why our supporting subdomains are deliberately lightweight while the
  core is richly modeled.)

  ▎ One-liner: you've got it right — problem type → technique: complex business 
  ▎ rules → Domain Model (DDD); simple CRUD → Transaction Script / Active Record
  ▎ (framework-supported); algorithm-heavy → DS&A + plain functions. And you 
  ▎ apply this per part of a system, not to the whole thing — pick the simplest 
  ▎ pattern the logic allows (Fowler's spectrum), reserving DDD for where 
  ▎ complexity genuinely lives.

  So your mental model is sound — you were just missing the names, which are
  Transaction Script / Active Record (CRUD) and DS&A / procedural design
  (algorithms), with Domain Model being the formal name for what DDD produces.

✻ Brewed for 35s

❯ for CRUD, can we say its data model ?                                         

⏺ Close — and the spirit is right, but let me sharpen the term, because "data
  model" means something slightly different from a pattern name.

  The distinction

  - Domain Model = a pattern — a behavior-rich object model (what DDD builds).
    It's a way to organize logic.
  - Data Model = the structure/schema of the data itself — tables, columns,
    relationships, the ER diagram. It describes data, not a logic pattern.

  So "Data Model" isn't the pattern-name counterpart to "Domain Model." They're
  on different axes: one names a logic approach, the other names the data's
  shape.

  But your instinct is correct in spirit

  CRUD apps are data-centric / data-model-driven design — you design the schema 
  (the data model) first, and the code is thin operations over it. That
  genuinely contrasts with Domain Model, which is behavior-centric (design the
  behavior first; persistence is secondary). So:

  ┌─────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │             │        Data-centric (CRUD)        │   Behavior-centric    │
  │             │                                   │         (DDD)         │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Design      │ the data model (schema/ER)        │ the behavior (rules,  │
  │ starts from │                                   │ aggregates)           │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ The code is │ thin CRUD over tables             │ rich domain objects   │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Pattern     │ Transaction Script / Active       │                       │
  │ names       │ Record; objects are often an      │ Domain Model          │
  │             │ Anemic Model                      │                       │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ The hard    │ the data & queries                │ the rules             │
  │ part is     │                                   │                       │
  └─────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  So you can absolutely say "CRUD is data-model-driven / data-centric design."
  Just don't call the code pattern "Data Model" — that's the schema. The code
  patterns are Transaction Script / Active Record, and the object shape (data +
  getters/setters, no behavior) is called an Anemic Model.

  The exact vocabulary

  - Data Model = the schema / ER structure (the data design).
  - Data-centric / data-driven design = the approach where the data model leads
    (CRUD, reporting, analytics).
  - Anemic Model = an object that's just data, no behavior — the norm in CRUD,
    an anti-pattern in DDD.
  - Domain Model = behavior-rich object model — DDD.

  Tie-back to what we already noted (step 6)

  This is the same axis from the step-6 ERD sidebar: "is the hard part the 
  behavior/rules or the data/queries?"
  - Behavior-heavy (our LMS core) → model the domain first, persist last (Domain
    Model).
  - Data-/reporting-heavy (warehouses, analytics, plain CRUD) → the data model 
    leads, design storage/access patterns early (data-centric).

  ▎ One-liner: yes — CRUD is data-centric / data-model-driven design (contrast: 
  ▎ DDD is behavior-centric). But "Data Model" names the schema, not the code 
  ▎ pattern; the CRUD code patterns are Transaction Script / Active Record, with
  ▎ an Anemic Model object. The clean opposition to remember is data-centric vs
  ▎ behavior-centric — that's the axis, and it decides whether you design the 
  ▎ schema first or the domain first.

  So: right axis, right instinct — just anchor "data model" to the schema, and
  use data-centric design for the approach opposite to DDD's Domain Model.
