✻ Crunched for 27s

❯ what is this direction- upstream/downstream ?                                 

⏺ Great to pin down — it's borrowed from a river, and it's about
  influence/dependency, not (necessarily) data-flow direction.

  The river metaphor

        UPSTREAM  ────────────►  DOWNSTREAM
     (source of the water)      (receives the water)

  - Whatever happens upstream flows down to downstream.
  - Downstream cannot affect upstream. If you pollute the river upstream,
    everyone downstream suffers — but nothing downstream does can change the
    water upstream.

  What it means in DDD

  ▎ Upstream context = the one that provides / influences (a model, data, an 
  ▎ API). It changes on its own terms.
  ▎ Downstream context = the one that depends on / consumes it, and must adapt 
  ▎ when upstream changes.

  The essence is an asymmetry of influence:
  - When upstream changes its model/API → downstream must react.
  - When downstream changes → upstream is unaffected, doesn't care.

  So upstream holds the power; downstream carries the risk.

  The test to find the direction

  Ask: "If context A changes, must context B adapt — but not the reverse?"
  - Yes → A is upstream, B is downstream.

  Note: it's about dependency/influence, which usually (but not always) matches
  data flow. The reliable question is "who is forced to adapt to whom?"

  Concrete examples

  ┌───────────────┬───────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Upstream (U)  │  Downstream   │                  Why                   │
  │               │      (D)      │                                        │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Stripe        │ your Ordering │ Stripe changes its API → you scramble  │
  │ (payment      │  context      │ to adapt. You can't make Stripe change │
  │ provider)     │               │  for you.                              │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │               │ Catalog defines products; Ordering     │
  │ Catalog       │ Ordering      │ consumes them by ID. Catalog reshapes  │
  │               │               │ a product → Ordering adapts.           │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Identity      │ everyone      │ Identity owns auth; consumers conform  │
  │               │               │ to its tokens/API.                     │
  └───────────────┴───────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Why the direction decides the pattern

  Downstream's options depend on how much power it has relative to upstream:

  ┌───────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Downstream's situation     │            Pattern it picks             │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ No power, upstream won't      │ Conformist (just accept it) or          │
  │ change (e.g. an external      │ Anti-Corruption Layer (translate &      │
  │ vendor)                       │ protect)                                │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Some negotiating power (same  │ Customer/Supplier (upstream agrees to   │
  │ company, cooperative team)    │ consider your needs)                    │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Upstream wants to serve many  │ Upstream provides Open Host Service +   │
  │ consumers well                │ Published Language                      │
  └───────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┘

  That's why "which pattern?" always starts with "who's upstream?" — the
  direction sets the power dynamic, and the power dynamic picks the integration
  style.

  Peer relationships (no clear up/down)

  Not everything is directional:
  - Partnership — mutual dependency; both affect each other, they coordinate as
    equals (succeed/fail together).
  - Shared Kernel — both co-own the shared piece; neither is purely upstream.

  The one-liner

  ▎ Upstream/downstream = who influences whom. The upstream context changes on 
  ▎ its own terms and the downstream must adapt to it (never the reverse) — like
  ▎ a river, effects flow one way. Upstream has the power; downstream has the 
  ▎ risk. Find the direction by asking "who is forced to adapt when the other 
  ▎ changes?" — and the direction then decides the integration pattern (no power
  ▎ → Conformist/ACL; some power → Customer/Supplier; good provider → Open Host
  ▎ Service). Peer relationships (Partnership, Shared Kernel) have no up/down —
  ▎ both sides influence each other.

  So on a context map, every directional arrow points from upstream to 
  downstream — read it as "changes here force adaptation there."

⏺ Added "Upstream / Downstream — the direction concept (in depth)" as a
  subsection in §4 of docs/appendix-strategic-ddd.md, right before the
  named-patterns tables. It captures:

  - The river metaphor (influence flows downhill; downstream can't affect
    upstream).
  - The definition — upstream provides/influences; downstream depends and must
    adapt — and the asymmetry (upstream = power, downstream = risk).
  - The test — "if A changes, must B adapt but not the reverse?" → A is
    upstream.
  - Examples table (Stripe/Ordering, Catalog/Ordering, Identity/everyone).
  - Why direction decides the pattern — downstream's power → which integration
    pattern (no power → Conformist/ACL; some power → Customer/Supplier; good
    provider → OHS+Published Language).
  - The peer exception (Partnership, Shared Kernel — no up/down).

  It flows naturally into the pattern tables that follow (since the patterns are
  labeled by their stance toward that direction). The strategic-DDD appendix
  now explains the direction concept in full before using it.

✻ Baked for 23s

❯ The context map can have below relationships:-                                
  . U-D Relationship                                                            
   . Customer/Supplier                                                          
   . Conformist                                                                 
   . ACL                                                                        
   . OHS                                                                        
   . Published Language                                                         
  . Symmetric                                                                   
  . No Relationship                                                             
  . Anti Pattern                                                                
                                                                                
  I want to know when and how and why they exis, to use in deep with examples.  

⏺ Here's each context-map relationship in depth — When (the situation), How (the
  mechanism), Why (the problem it solves), and an Example. Grouped by family.

  ---

  A. Upstream–Downstream family (directional)

  1. Customer/Supplier

  - When: U–D relationship, same organization, and the downstream (customer) has
    legitimate needs the upstream (supplier) can accommodate. Cooperative,
    downstream has some power.
  - How: a formal relationship — downstream's requirements enter upstream's
    backlog; upstream commits to a stable interface; changes are negotiated;
    downstream's automated tests guard the contract.
  - Why: gives downstream a voice (upstream won't break it carelessly) without 
    merging the contexts.
  - Example: Ordering (customer) depends on Catalog (supplier), both internal
    teams. Ordering needs a new field → it goes into Catalog's backlog → Catalog
    adds it while keeping the API stable.

  2. Conformist

  - When: U–D, but downstream has no negotiating power and upstream won't adapt
    (big external vendor, dominant internal team) — and upstream's model is
    "good enough" that translating isn't worth it.
  - How: downstream adopts upstream's model/language as-is. No translation
    layer.
  - Why: cheapest option when you can't change upstream and its model is
    acceptable. Trade-off: you're coupled to upstream's model — its concepts
    leak into yours, its changes ripple in.
  - Example: integrating a government tax API and just using their data
    structures directly, because an ACL isn't worth it and their model is fine.

  3. Anti-Corruption Layer (ACL)

  - When: U–D, can't change upstream, and upstream's model is messy/foreign and
    would corrupt your clean model. You want to protect your model.
  - How: a translation layer at the boundary — adapters convert upstream's
    shapes into your model (and back). Your domain never sees upstream's
    concepts.
  - Why: protects your model's integrity; isolates upstream changes to the ACL;
    keeps your own clean language.
  - Example: our Reservations bubble wrapping the legacy BookManager; Ordering
    wrapping Stripe; a new service wrapping a legacy mainframe.
  - Conformist vs ACL: Conformist = accept upstream's model (cheap, coupled).
    ACL = translate & protect (costs a layer, stays clean). Choose ACL when
    protecting your model is worth the code.

  4. Open Host Service (OHS)

  - When: an upstream context has many downstream consumers and per-consumer
    custom integration is unsustainable.
  - How: upstream publishes a well-documented, stable, general-purpose public 
    API (the "host service"), versioned and backward-compatible; any consumer
    uses it.
  - Why: one clean interface instead of N bespoke integrations; consumers
    decouple from upstream internals; upstream can evolve internals behind the
    stable API.
  - Example: an Identity service exposing a public OAuth API for all internal
    services; Stripe itself is an OHS.

  5. Published Language

  - When: parties need to exchange data in a well-understood shared format —
    especially alongside OHS or across many consumers/orgs.
  - How: a formal, documented shared schema for the interchange (OpenAPI, JSON
    Schema, protobuf, or an industry standard like HL7 FHIR in healthcare; a
    canonical event schema). Each side translates to/from it at its edge.
  - Why: a stable contract no single context owns internally — integrate without
    coupling internal models; standards enable cross-org integration.
  - Example: an e-commerce OHS publishing an OpenAPI spec; a canonical
    OrderPlaced event schema many services consume.
  - OHS vs Published Language: OHS is the door (the service/API); Published
    Language is the language spoken at the door (the schema). Usually together:
    "Open Host Service with a Published Language."

  ---

  B. Symmetric family (peer — no up/down)

  6. Partnership

  - When: two contexts/teams are deeply interdependent — succeed or fail
    together; a change in one usually needs a change in the other. Mutual, no
    clear upstream.
  - How: close coordination — synchronized releases, joint planning, shared 
    integration tests, mutual commitment not to break each other.
  - Why: when coupling is genuinely bidirectional, pretending one is upstream
    fails; partnership acknowledges and manages the mutual dependency.
  - Example: Inventory ↔ Fulfillment — how stock is reserved affects how orders
    are picked, and vice versa; the two teams plan together.
  - Watch: if you need Partnership everywhere, your boundaries may be wrong
    (maybe it should be one context).

  7. Shared Kernel

  - When: two contexts share a subset of the model that's wasteful/error-prone 
    to duplicate, and the teams can coordinate on it.
  - How: a small, explicitly-shared, co-owned piece of model/code both depend
    on; changes require agreement from both. Keep it minimal.
  - Why: avoids duplicating-and-diverging a genuinely shared concept — but it 
    couples the contexts (every change needs coordination), so keep it tiny.
  - Example: our shared/ kernel (IDs + base error); two contexts sharing a small
    Money/Address.
  - Shared Kernel vs Published Language: Shared Kernel = share code (tight,
    co-owned). Published Language = share a schema (looser, no shared code).
    Prefer Published Language when you can — less coupling.

  ---

  C. No relationship

  8. Separate Ways

  - When: two contexts have no compelling reason to integrate — integration cost
    exceeds the value, or needs are genuinely independent.
  - How: deliberately don't integrate; duplicate any small overlap rather than
    couple.
  - Why: integration always costs (coupling, coordination); if the benefit is
    small, avoiding it is correct. Duplication is cheaper than coupling here.
  - Example: two departments each track "customer satisfaction" their own way
    with no need to share; a report that re-computes a value rather than calling
    another service.
  - Note: a deliberate, legitimate choice — "the best integration is sometimes
    no integration."

  ---

  D. Anti-pattern

  9. Big Ball of Mud

  - When: you don't choose it — you identify it: a region with no clear
    boundaries, tangled model, mixed languages.
  - How: mark it on the map as a "here be dragons" zone, and draw a boundary 
    around it (usually an ACL) so its mess can't spread into clean contexts.
  - Why: naming it lets you contain it (never call into it directly — wrap it)
    and plan to strangle it over time.
  - Example: the legacy monolith everyone fears; wrap it in an ACL and grow
    clean contexts around it (Strangler Fig).

  ---

  Decision guide

  Two contexts interact?
  ├─ No / not worth it            → Separate Ways
  ├─ Yes, mutual & interdependent → Partnership (peers)
  ├─ Yes, share a small model     → Shared Kernel (co-own, keep tiny)  [prefer
  Published Language]
  └─ Yes, one depends on the other (U→D):
       Am I (downstream) able to influence upstream?
       ├─ Yes (same org, cooperative)      → Customer/Supplier
       └─ No:
            Is upstream's model acceptable to use directly?
            ├─ Yes → Conformist (cheap, coupled)
            └─ No  → Anti-Corruption Layer (translate & protect)
       Am I (upstream) serving many consumers?
            → Open Host Service + Published Language
       Is the other side a hopeless mess?
            → Big Ball of Mud → wrap in an ACL, strangle over time

  ▎ The through-line: the direction (U/D) + your power + how messy/shared the 
  ▎ models are pick the pattern. Downstream with power → Customer/Supplier; 
  ▎ downstream without power → Conformist (accept) or ACL (protect); upstream 
  ▎ serving many → OHS + Published Language; true peers → Partnership or Shared 
  ▎ Kernel; not worth integrating → Separate Ways; a mess → contain it as a Big 
  ▎ Ball of Mud behind an ACL.

  Want me to save this deep-dive (per-pattern When/How/Why/Example + the
  decision guide) into the strategic-DDD appendix — expanding the pattern tables
  that are already there?

      138 +- *How:* downstream **adopts upstream's model as-is**, no tran
          +slation.                                                      
      139 +- *Why:* cheapest when you can't change upstream and its model
          + is acceptable. *Trade-off:* coupled to upstream's model; its 
          +changes ripple in.                                            
      140 +- *Example:* using a government tax API's data structures dire
          +ctly.                                                         
      141 +                                                              
      142 +**Anti-Corruption Layer (ACL)**                               
      143 +- *When:* U–D, can't change upstream, **and** its model is mes
          +sy/foreign and would corrupt yours.                           
      144 +- *How:* a **translation layer** — adapters convert upstream s
          +hapes ↔ your model; your domain never sees upstream concepts. 
      145 +- *Why:* protects your model's integrity; **isolates upstream 
          +changes** to the ACL.                                         
      146 +- *Example:* Reservations wrapping legacy `BookManager`; Order
          +ing wrapping Stripe.                                          
      147 +- *Conformist vs ACL:* accept (cheap, coupled) vs translate-&-
          +protect (a layer, stays clean).                               
      148 +                                                              
      149 +**Open Host Service (OHS)**                                   
      150 +- *When:* an upstream has **many consumers**; per-consumer int
          +egration is unsustainable.                                    
      151 +- *How:* publish a **stable, general-purpose public API**, ver
          +sioned + backward-compatible.                                 
      152 +- *Why:* one clean interface instead of N bespoke ones; consum
          +ers decouple from internals.                                  
      153 +- *Example:* an Identity service's public OAuth API; Stripe it
          +self.                                                         
      154 +                                                              
      155 +**Published Language**                                        
      156 +- *When:* parties exchange data in a **shared format** (esp. w
          +ith OHS / many consumers/orgs).                               
      157 +- *How:* a **formal documented schema** (OpenAPI, protobuf, HL
          +7 FHIR, a canonical event schema); each side translates at its
          + edge.                                                        
      158 +- *Why:* a stable contract **no context owns internally** — in
          +tegrate without coupling internal models.                     
      159 +- *Example:* a canonical `OrderPlaced` event schema on the bus
          +.                                                             
      160 +- *OHS vs Published Language:* the **door** (service/API) vs t
          +he **language at the door** (schema) — usually together.      
      161 +                                                              
      162 +**B. Symmetric family (peers — no up/down)**                  
      163 +                                                              
      164 +**Partnership**                                               
      165 +- *When:* two contexts **deeply interdependent** — succeed/fai
          +l together; changes usually co-occur.                         
      166 +- *How:* synchronized releases, joint planning, shared integra
          +tion tests, mutual commitment.                                
      167 +- *Why:* when coupling is genuinely bidirectional, pretending 
          +one is upstream fails.                                        
      168 +- *Example:* Inventory ↔ Fulfillment. *Watch:* needing it ever
          +ywhere = boundaries may be wrong.                             
      169 +                                                              
      170 +**Shared Kernel**                                             
      171 +- *When:* a shared model subset that's wasteful/error-prone to
          + duplicate; teams can coordinate.                             
      172 +- *How:* a **small, co-owned** shared piece; changes need **bo
          +th** teams' agreement; keep minimal.                          
      173 +- *Why:* avoids diverging a shared concept — **but couples** t
          +he contexts, so keep it tiny.                                 
      174 +- *Example:* our `shared/` kernel (IDs + base error).         
      175 +- *Shared Kernel vs Published Language:* share **code** (tight
          +) vs share a **schema** (loose — prefer this when you can).   
      176 +                                                              
      177 +**C. No relationship**                                        
      178 +                                                              
      179 +**Separate Ways**                                             
      180 +- *When:* **no compelling reason to integrate** — cost > value
          +, or needs are independent.                                   
      181 +- *How:* deliberately don't integrate; duplicate any small ove
          +rlap.                                                         
      182 +- *Why:* integration always costs; if benefit is small, avoidi
          +ng it is correct.                                             
      183 +- *Example:* two departments each tracking "satisfaction" thei
          +r own way. *"The best integration is sometimes none."*        
      184 +                                                              
      185 +**D. Anti-pattern**                                           
      186 +                                                              
      187 +**Big Ball of Mud**                                           
      188 +- *When:* you **identify** (not choose) a boundary-less, tangl
          +ed region.                                                    
      189 +- *How:* mark it; **draw a boundary (usually an ACL)** so its 
          +mess can't spread; plan to strangle it.                       
      190 +- *Why:* naming it lets you **contain** it and evolve it out. 
      191 +- *Example:* the legacy monolith — wrap in an ACL, grow clean 
          +contexts around it (Strangler Fig).                           
      192 +                                                              
      193 +### Decision guide                                            
      194 +```                                                           
      195 +Two contexts interact?                                        
      196 +├─ No / not worth it            → Separate Ways               
      197 +├─ Mutual & interdependent      → Partnership (peers)         
      198 +├─ Share a small model          → Shared Kernel (co-own, tiny)
          +  [prefer Published Language]                                 
      199 +└─ One depends on the other (U→D):                            
      200 +     Can I (downstream) influence upstream?                   
      201 +     ├─ Yes (same org, cooperative)  → Customer/Supplier      
      202 +     └─ No:                                                   
      203 +          Upstream's model acceptable to use directly?        
      204 +          ├─ Yes → Conformist (cheap, coupled)                
      205 +          └─ No  → Anti-Corruption Layer (translate & protect)
      206 +     Am I (upstream) serving many consumers? → Open Host Servi
          +ce + Published Language                                       
      207 +     Is the other side a hopeless mess?      → Big Ball of Mud
          + → wrap in ACL, strangle                                      
      208 +```                                                           
      209 +> **Through-line:** direction (U/D) + your **power** + how **m
          +essy/shared** the models are pick the pattern. Downstream *wit
          +h* power → Customer/Supplier; *without* → Conformist (accept) 
          +or ACL (protect); upstream serving many → OHS + Published Lang
          +uage; true peers → Partnership or Shared Kernel; not worth it 
          +→ Separate Ways; a mess → contain as Big Ball of Mud behind an
          + ACL.                                                         
      210 +                                                              
      211  ---
      212  
      213  ## How they nest

❯ what is anti-pattern cpncept ?                                                

⏺ Login expired · Please run /login

✻ Sautéed for 0s

❯ /login 
  ⎿  Login successful

⏺ Good to define properly — it's a general software concept, not DDD-specific.
  Here it is.

  Definition

  ▎ An anti-pattern is a common, tempting "solution" to a recurring problem that
  ▎ looks reasonable but actually makes things worse — it has 
  ▎ repeatedly-observed negative consequences, and there's a known better 
  ▎ alternative.

  Two parts, both required:
  1. It's commonly used (people keep reaching for it — it's a recurring bad
     habit, not a one-off mistake).
  2. It's counterproductive — it seems to help but causes more problems than it
     solves, and a better solution exists.

  Term coined by Andrew Koenig (1995), popularized by the AntiPatterns book —
  deliberately the inverse of a design pattern (a proven good recurring
  solution).

  Pattern vs Anti-pattern

  ┌─────────┬────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │         │       Design Pattern       │           Anti-pattern           │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Nature  │ proven good recurring      │ common bad recurring "solution"  │
  │         │ solution                   │                                  │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Effect  │ solves cleanly             │ seems to help, worsens things    │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Purpose │ do this                    │ recognize & avoid this (know the │
  │         │                            │  fix)                            │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Example │ Strategy, Repository       │ Big Ball of Mud, God Object      │
  └─────────┴────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  Why the concept is useful

  Naming a bad habit makes it recognizable and discussable. "This is a God
  Object" instantly conveys the problem and implies the remedy — like a
  diagnosis. Anti-patterns are a shared vocabulary for mistakes, so teams can
  spot and fix them fast.

  The key nuance — context matters

  Something is an anti-pattern relative to a context, not absolutely. A pattern
  used in the wrong situation becomes an anti-pattern. Examples:
  - A Singleton — fine occasionally; sprinkled everywhere it's an anti-pattern
    (global state).
  - Microservices — great at scale; for a 3-person app, splitting is the
    "distributed monolith" anti-pattern.
  - DDD itself — right for complex domains; applied to simple CRUD it's
    over-engineering (an anti-pattern there).

  So the same technique can be a pattern or an anti-pattern depending on fit —
  which ties straight back to our "match the technique to the problem" reflex.

  Common examples (across levels)

  - Big Ball of Mud — no structure, everything tangled (the DDD context-map
    one).
  - God Object / God Class — one class that knows/does everything.
  - Anemic Domain Model — objects are pure data bags, logic in services (DDD's
    target).
  - Spaghetti Code — tangled, no clear flow.
  - Golden Hammer — "when all you have is a hammer, everything looks like a
    nail" (forcing one favorite tool onto every problem).
  - Premature Optimization / Distributed Monolith / Copy-Paste Programming.

  Note several of these are ones we've already flagged — anemic model (step 7),
  big ball of mud (strategic DDD), distributed monolith (microservices
  appendix), over-engineering DDD (techniques map). The whole course has been
  teaching patterns and their anti-patterns side by side.

  The one-liner

  ▎ An anti-pattern is a recurring, tempting-but-counterproductive "solution" 
  ▎ with known bad consequences and a known better fix — the deliberate inverse 
  ▎ of a design pattern. Its value is vocabulary: naming a bad habit (God 
  ▎ Object, Big Ball of Mud, Anemic Model) makes it recognizable and fixable. 
  ▎ And it's context-relative — the same technique can be a good pattern in one 
  ▎ situation and an anti-pattern in another (the Golden Hammer trap), which is 
  ▎ exactly why "match the tool to the problem" is the meta-skill.

  Want me to add a short "anti-patterns" note to the techniques-map doc (it
  pairs well with the "when NOT to use DDD" / Golden Hammer theme already
  there)?
